A declarative approach to specifying Paxos using three-valued modal fixpoint logic
Murdoch J. Gabbay Luca Zanolini

Abstract
We present Three-valued Fixpoint Quorum Logic , or 𝟑 ​ μ ​ 𝖰𝖫 {\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL} for short; a three-valued modal fixed-point logic designed for declaratively specifying and reasoning about distributed algorithms, such as the Paxos consensus algorithm. Our methodology represents a distributed algorithm as a logical theory, enabling correctness properties to be derived directly within the framework — or revealing logical errors in the algorithm’s design when they exist. 𝟑 ​ μ ​ 𝖰𝖫 {\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL} adopts a declarative approach, specifying the overall logic of computation without prescribing control flow. Notably, message-passing and state-transitions are not explicitly modelled, and notions of ‘quorum’ are abstracted from concrete thresholds to abstract modal operators. This distinguishes our framework from approaches like TLA+. The logical abstraction emphasises the essence of distributed algorithms, offering a novel perspective on their specification and reasoning. We define the syntax and semantics of 𝟑 ​ μ ​ 𝖰𝖫 {\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL} , explore its theoretical properties, and demonstrate its applicability through a detailed treatment of the Paxos consensus algorithm. By presenting Paxos as a logical theory and deriving its standard correctness properties, we showcase the framework’s capacity to handle non-trivial distributed systems. We envision 𝟑 ​ μ ​ 𝖰𝖫 {\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL} as a versatile tool for specifying and reasoning about distributed algorithms. The Paxos example highlights the framework’s ability to capture intricate details, offering a new lens through which distributed algorithms can be specified, studied, and checked. The ideas we present are easily implemented, and to demonstrate this we have implemented a freely-available proof-of-concept 𝟑 ​ μ ​ 𝖰𝖫 {\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL} model-checker, as an effective prototyping tool.



\yyyymmdddate

keywords
Modal logic, distributed consensus

1
Introduction

1.1
Initial overview

Introducing
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}

Three-valued Fixpoint Quorum Logic
, or
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}

for short, is a logical framework for studying distributed algorithms in a
declarative
manner, abstracting away in particular from notions of message-passing, concrete state-transitions, and explicit threshold-based notions of quorum.

To demonstrate the scope and utility of this approach, we focus on Paxos
[
Lam98
]
— one of the most widely deployed and influential consensus protocols in distributed systems
[
CGR07
,
BBH+11
,
RST11
,
BBC+11
]
.
By developing an axiomatisation of Paxos in our framework, we capture the essence of its design in a precise logical form and establish a principled foundation for reasoning about its correctness.
Furthermore, this axiomatic perspective paves the way for extending the methodology to more complex algorithms, offering a path toward analyses that are both conceptually cleaner, and easier to manage.

Logic is widely applied in distributed algorithms: for example the logic TLA+ (based on Linear Temporal Logic) is an industry standard to model and verify distributed protocols
[
Lam94
,
Lam02
,
YML99
]
.
TLA+ encourages us to think in terms of a (sequential) temporal structure of actions (as per the name: Temporal Logic of Actions).
In contrast, a
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
axiomatisation adopts a more abstract perspective: it distils the protocol into a set of logical principles that capture its essential structure.
This abstraction highlights the core invariants of the algorithm and also provides a clean alternative foundation for reasoning and generalisation.

This idea of abstracting an algorithm to a logical theory is itself not new:
declarative programming
is based on it.
What is new, however, is applying an axiomatic, message-free, time-free, three-valued modal logic perspective to distributed algorithms at the level of Paxos.

This paper demonstrates that such an axiomatisation is not only possible but also illuminating: it exposes the essential logical structure of the protocol in a way that is both rigorous and conceptually simple.
1
1

1

Note that just because a framework is conceptually simple does not mean that everything we say in that framework must also be simple.
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
is a core language (like first-order logic, higher-order logic, the
\lambda
-calculus, LISP or indeed C); the programs and proofs that we express within it can be arbitrarily rich.

Concrete examples appear below and throughout the paper.
2
2

2

Readers eager to see a toy illustration of the method may skip directly to Subsection
6.1
.

For reasons of length, we concentrate on Paxos in this paper.
This offers a rich and nontrivial case study — complex and non-trivial, yet sufficiently well-understood to serve as a benchmark.
In a sister paper to this one with a more explicit expository emphasis
[
Gab26b
]
, the first author applies a simpler logic to study Bracha Broadcast and Crusader Agreement (which are simpler than Paxos).

To demonstrate the applicability of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
, we created a proof-of-concept
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
model-checker
[
Gab26a
]
, vibe-coded by the first author using AI from prompts starting with the draft of this paper, and with
[
Gab26b
]
.
This is a theory paper but the existence of a parallel prototype implementation indicates that this theory
3
3

3

…which is no more complex than that justifying any other declarative environment, like Haskell or Lean.
has potential for the delivery of compact, snappy, and effective developer tools, with succinct syntax and surprisingly compact search-spaces.
The reader is welcome to play with the tool and watch the logic from this paper come alive in an implementation.
4
4

4

Note that the implementation is
not this paper
! For example, by design the implementation omits the exposition and proofs that give meaning to and justify correctness of its computations — that is the task of a maths paper like this one — and as a prototype it should not be relied on in production. Yet it illustrates and indicates the potential for what could become of the ideas in this paper in the hands of a competent software engineer. So this paper has abundant theory, but it is not
just
theory for its own sake. Quite the opposite.

The
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
model-checker is a prototype but it is not a toy.
We know this, because the AI coding agent spontaneously applied the model-checker it had just written to test the papers from its prompt, and in doing so it falsified a conjecture from
[
Gab26b
]
(as documented in the package’s README file).
So: not a toy.

In the long run, we envision our logical approach fulfilling the broader promise of declarative techniques: uncovering subtle errors, reducing development effort, accelerating productivity, and even suggesting new mathematical insights.

What is declarative programming

By ‘declarative’ we mean that we specify the logic of a computation without specifying its control flow.

Thus our axiomatisation of Paxos (in Figure
11
) relates to a concrete Paxos implementation and/or its TLA+ specification, much as this axiomatic, algebraic specification of the factorial function
5
5

5

By ‘axiomatic’ we mean that the function is specified just by asserting properties that are true of it. By ‘algebraic’ we mean that these assertions are purely equational, i.e. we just assert some equalities between terms.

0!=1\qquad(n+1)!=(n+1)*n!

relates to this concrete implementation of factorial in BASH

value=1; while [ $number -gt 1 ]; do value=$((value * number)); number=$((number - 1)); done

The reader may be familiar with programming languages based on declarative principles, such as Haskell, Erlang, or Prolog.
Their design philosophy is that we should assert things that we want to be true, and leave it to the compiler to figure out how best to make this happen.
Contrast with C, which is a canonical imperative language.
6
6

6

We are not interested here in taking sides. With a variety of tools to choose from, we pick the one that is best for the job. What matters is that the widest possible
variety
of levels of abstraction is made available.

This paper develops a methodology for specifying distributed algorithms which lies firmly on the Haskell/Erlang/Prolog side of the spectrum — concise, declarative, and abstraction-oriented — in contrast to TLA+, which (while still high-level) explicitly represents state transitions, quorum sizes, and other operational detail.

As a warm-up, we will consider a minimal example: a simple voting system.
We present this in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to illustrate the style of axiomatisation and to give the reader a concrete sense of how the framework operates in practice.

Consider a voting system such that

‘if a quorum of voters vote for candidate
c
, then
c
wins the election’.

We write this axiomatically simply as

({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{vote}(c))\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mathsf{wins}(c).

The
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
is a logical modality that is true when there exists a quorum of participants at which
\mathsf{vote}(c)
holds.
7
7

7

Details of this modality in our framework are in Proposition
4.7.14
(
3
) and Lemma
5.3.1
.

Even without going into the details of what these symbols mean, it is clear that details of message-passing, of how votes are communicated, and of how they are counted up, are absent.
In fact in this case the predicate is just a direct rendering of the English statement above it, without specifying how it is concretely implemented.
Thus, in keeping with a declarative philosophy, we specify what we want the algorithm to do, but not how it should do it: it is the point of the axiom to express formally and precisely our idea — and to add as little as possible to that, beyond what is necessary to make it logically precise.

Who should read this document

This work is intended for two main audiences:

1.

logicians
, who may be intrigued by the study of a three-valued modal fixed-point logic that combines unusual theoretical features with concrete applications to distributed computation, and

2.

distributed-systems researchers
, who may find
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
a basis for useful tools for specifying, analysing, and communicating the design of complex protocols.

Our primary case studies concern consensus algorithms, with a particular emphasis on blockchain protocols.
8
8

8

This is a particular professional interest of the second author, who was part of the core team developing consensus algorithms for Ethereum.

These represent one of the most demanding and safety-critical application domains.

References to key points in the text

The reader who might want to jump to where the action is and read backwards and forwards from there, is welcome to proceed as follows:

•

The axioms of Declarative Paxos are in Figure
11
.

•

The syntax and semantics of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
are in Figures
3
,
5
, and
6
.

•

Truth-tables for three-valued logic and its connectives are in Figure
1
.

•

An outline of the Paxos algorithm (for the reader who might like to be reminded what it is) is in Remark
6.2.1
.

•

See also toy axiomatisations for an imaginary ‘simple’ protocol in Subsection
6.1
, designed to be vaguely reminiscent of Paxos, but very greatly simplified for the sake of exposition.

1.2
Motivation and background (further details)

Let us unpack what consensus in distributed systems (like blockchains) is, why it matters, and why it is hard:

Distributed systems, and the fundamental challenge thereof

A distributed computing system is one that distributes its computation over several machines.
Distributed systems are essential to computing: they enable a network of parties to collaborate to achieve common goals
[
CGR11
]
.

The fundamental challenge of distribution
is that we cannot assume that all participants in the algorithm are correct: we need to be resilient against failure, and against hostile (also called
Byzantine
) behaviour by some participants (a typical assumption is that up to a third of participants may be faulty in some way).
This has a profound effect on the flavour of distributed systems; and it is reflected in this paper by the use of a third truth-value which we use to represent faulty/hostile behaviour, and by the use of sophisticated modalities to describe quorum-based behaviour.
9
9

9

This gives ‘distributed systems’ a flavour somewhat different from ‘concurrency’, ‘parallelism’, or ‘multi-agent systems’: the fundamental character of the theory of distributed systems is particularly marked by the fact that algorithms must survive in an unreliable and hostile space. (They solve this problem using notions of quorums, as we will discuss.)

Being distributed is attractive to software engineers because it can give us desirable properties, including some or all of: scalability, redundancy, lower latency, reliability, and resilience.
For this reason, almost any nontrivial commercial software system that the reader might care to name is implemented in a distributed manner.
Furthermore, blockchain systems (like Bitcoin and Ethereum
10
10

10

https://bitcoin.org/en/
and
https://ethereum.org/en/
) and peer-to-peer systems are inherently distributed by design; if they were centralised, then they would not even make sense.

The challenge of consensus

Among the many challenges in distributed systems,
consensus
represents a fundamental abstraction.
Consensus captures the problem of

reaching agreement among multiple participants on a common value, in the face of possibly unreliable communication, and in the presence of possibly faulty participants.

Consensus algorithms are an essential prerequisite for coordination in any distributed system, and they play a particularly critical role in blockchain networks.
In such networks, consensus is the mechanism that ensures all participants agree on the state of the ledger — the blockchain’s source of truth — thereby solving problems like
double spending
(manipulating the blockchain ledger to spend the same token twice).
Consensus protocols also enable the secure ordering of transactions, and they ensure the integrity and consistency of the blockchain, even in the face of adversarial actors.

Consensus protocols in blockchain systems are particularly challenging to design and implement correctly.
Indeed, there are numerous examples in which incomplete or insufficiently specified protocols have led to serious issues, such as network halts or vulnerabilities that adversaries could exploit.
For example:

1.

Solana (
https://solana.com
) has experienced instances where its consensus mechanism
[
Yak18
,
Sho22
]
contributed to temporary network halts (
https://solana.com/news/9-14-network-outage-initial-overview
).

2.

Ripple’s (
https://ripple.com
) consensus protocol has been analysed for conditions in which safety and liveness may not be guaranteed
[
ASCM21
]
.

3.

Avalanche’s (
https://www.avax.network
) consensus protocol has faced scrutiny regarding its assumptions and performance in adversarial scenarios
[
ASCT23
]
.

Even mature blockchains like Ethereum are not immune to challenges.
Attacks such as
balancing attacks

[
NTT21
]
and
ex-ante reorganisation
(
reorg
) attacks
[
SSNM+22
]
have highlighted vulnerabilities in Ethereum’s protocol.

These examples underscore how subtle flaws or ambiguities in protocol design can have significant consequences, emphasising the need for rigorous analysis and well-defined specifications in this complex and safety-critical field.

Compounding the fact that consensus algorithms are foundational to the security and functionality of blockchain systems, is the fact that designing and implementing these algorithms is notoriously challenging.
Distributed algorithms — including consensus protocols — are difficult (at least for human brains) because of the inherent difficulty of coordinating multiple participants, which may be faulty, hostile, drifting in and out of network connectivity
[
PS17
]
, and/or have different local clocks, states, agendas, and so forth.

In short, this is a notoriously difficult field which is full of ‘gotchas’, and within this difficult field, consensus protocols are amongst the most intricate, demanding, and practically important objects of study.

Introducing a logic-based declarative approach (applied to Paxos)

In this paper, we develop a logic-based declarative approach to modelling distributed algorithms.
It stands in relation to existing techniques in roughly the same way as declarative programming languages stand to imperative ones.

Technically speaking,
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
is a
three-valued modal fixpoint logic
.
This means:

•

There are three truth-values:
{\mathbf{t}}
(true),
{\mathbf{b}}
(no value returned, i.e. ‘crashed’), and
{\mathbf{f}}
(false)
[
Got22
]
.

•

Modalities let us express things like ‘some participant has property
\phi
’ or ‘a quorum of participants has property
\psi
’ or ‘
\phi
has been true in the past’ or ‘
\psi
will be true in the future’.
11
11

11

In order, these are as follows in our logic:
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\phi
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
,
{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi
, and
\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
. Precise semantics are in Figures
5
and
6
.

•

The operator
\mu
imports the expressive power of iteration
[
Wik24a
]
.
Consensus algorithms are often iterative, thus it is natural to include this in our logic.

We will apply these constructs to give axiomatic models of the Paxos consensus protocol
[
Lam98
,
Lam01
,
Cac11
]
and then we will state and derive standard correctness properties from the axioms.
Our example is elementary but nontrivial and strikes (we think) a good balance between being compact and still exhibiting enough richness of structure to illustrate the essential features of how our logic works.

The first half of this paper will focus on the logic, and the second half of this paper will apply the logic to the examples.
The reader from a logic background might like to start with the first half and refer to examples for motivation; the reader from a distributed systems background might like to do the opposite and start with the examples in the second half, referring back to the logic as required to understand what is being done.

Related work using logic

Logic-based approaches have long been used to model and verify distributed protocols, with TLA+
[
Lam94
,
Lam02
,
YML99
]
being one of the most prominent frameworks in the field.
Introduced by Leslie Lamport in the 1990s, TLA+ builds on Linear Temporal Logic (LTL)
[
Pnu77
]
to describe systems in terms of state variables and their evolution through explicit next-state relations.
It has since become an industry standard for specifying and verifying distributed algorithms.

TLA+ excels at specifying and verifying behaviours through operational, state-based descriptions.
In this respect, it is well-suited for detailing how algorithms proceed step by step.
The reader can find a modern example of a TLA+ Paxos specification (part of a formalisation of a
multi-Paxos
elaboration of the protocol) described in
[
CLS16
]
; see Figures 2, 3, 4, and 5 of that paper and accompanying text.
A direct link to the relevant code file is in
[
LMD
]
.

This operational perspective can create a certain distance between the specification and the invariants it seeks to uphold.
Although TLA+ provides powerful mechanisms for expressing and checking invariants, the burden lies in verifying that these invariants are preserved across all possible state transitions — an exercise that can become complex and unintuitive, especially in the setting of distributed algorithms where subtle corner cases abound.

Our approach

Our novel framework leverages the mathematics of semitopology and three-valued logic.

Semitopology, introduced in previous work
[
Gab24
,
Gab25
]
, provides a natural way to describe quorums — sets of participants that collaborate to progress the system state — by representing them as open sets.
This perspective is particularly well-suited to distributed systems, where system behaviour emerges from the interactions of coalitions of components rather than from a sequence of steps orchestrated by a central controller.

Three-valued logic (for an overview, see
[
Ber08
, Chapter 5]
) enables reasoning about the uncertainty and potential faults inherent in distributed systems.
Unlike classical logic, which admits only
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
, three-valued logic introduces a third truth value
{\mathbf{b}}
to capture indeterminate states, as arise when the behaviour of some parties cannot be fully trusted.
This yields compact and natural descriptions of distributed protocols, distinguishing clearly between normal operation and faulty behaviour.
It also provides a formal foundation that could, in future work, be connected to automated reasoning tools such as SAT solvers or theorem provers, thereby enhancing both the expressiveness and verifiability of protocol specifications.

Our approach is especially relevant in the context of blockchain consensus protocols: distributed and decentralised systems that critically depend on precise and robust specifications.
It is not an exaggeration to say that blockchains have accelerated the development of new consensus protocols at an unprecedented pace
[
CV17
]
.
Yet many of these proposals remain only informally specified, often described in blog posts or non-academic sources, leading to ambiguity about their guarantees and behaviour.
This lack of formalisation contributes to a fragmented landscape in which the properties of protocols are unclear, hindering adoption and undermining confidence.

By providing a logic-based declarative language and a systematic way to axiomatise consensus protocols, our framework aims to support formal specification, rigorous reasoning, and eventual verification of the properties of distributed systems.
We believe this can lower costs, increase reliability, and reduce development time.
More broadly, we argue that effective formalisation is essential for eliminating ambiguity in the design and evaluation of emerging consensus mechanisms, particularly in the blockchain space.

Our approach thus bridges the gap between the declarative specification of invariants and the practical verification of protocol correctness.

We will explain everything, and that’s a feature

Different readers will find some parts of this paper straightforward and other parts more challenging.
Which parts these are will largely depend on the reader’s background.
12
12

12

This is not a hypothetical:
the first author, coming from a logic background, needed time to fully understand how Paxos works, while the second author, experienced with Paxos and familiar with logic in broad terms, was less at ease with modal fixed-point logic and models of axiomatic logical theories. The overlap of readers who are already well-versed in both fields may not be large. We hope this paper contributes to narrowing this gap.

For example:

•

three-valued modal logic and its models, and technical results like the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem used in Proposition
3.5.7
may be known to the reader with a background in logic (like the first author), but perhaps not to a distributed systems theorist (like the second author); conversely,

•

the Paxos algorithm, together with standard technical devices such as the discussion motivating
n
-twined semitopologies in Remark
5.1.3
, will be familiar territory to a distributed-systems expert.
However, their application in this logical setting may be new to readers coming from a logic background.

We play safe and explain everything.
If the reader finds we are going a bit slow in places, then better that than going too fast and skipping details that might help some other reader follow the argument.

1.3
Structure of the paper

1.

Section
1
is the Introduction; You are Here.
The rest of the paper splits into two halves: Sections
2
to
5
have to do with logic and semitopologies; and Sections
6
to
8
use
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to declaratively specify and study Paxos.

2.

Section
2
introduces the basics of three-valued logic, including notable connectives (
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
) and modalities (
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
and
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt
).
We also introduce a notion of validity for truth-values.

3.

Section
3
defines semitopologies (our model of the quorums that are ubiquitous in consensus algorithms), then it builds the syntax and semantics of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
.

4.

Section
4
studies
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
predicates; notably developing some logical equivalences, and well-behavedness criteria like being
pointwise
.

5.

Section
5
returns to semitopologies, and shows how antiseparation properties can be represented in logical form in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
.
One particular antiseparation property corresponds to the
quorum intersection property
which is used in the correctness proofs for Paxos (see Remark
5.2.4
), but we will put this fact in a more general logical/semitopological context.

6.

Section
6
introduces Declarative Paxos; this is representative of the kinds of algorithms that we built
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to express.
We take our time to explain how Paxos works, state the axiomatisation, and discuss it in detail.

7.

Section
7
states the standard correctness properties for Paxos, phrases them in declarative form, and then proves them from the axioms of Declarative Paxos.
What all our work has accomplished is to convert proving correctness properties into (relatively straightforward) proofs of facts about an abstract model, reasoning from axioms.

8.

Section
8
revisits the axioms of Declarative Paxos from Section
6
, in the light of the proofs in Section
7
— and then prunes them down to a minimal theory required to prove correctness.

We call this minimal theory
Simpler Declarative Paxos
: it is simpler, and because further details have been elided, it is more abstract.
The reader may prefer Declarative Paxos to Simpler Declarative Paxos because it is more identifiably related to the Paxos algorithm, but if we want to prove correctness properties, then Simpler Declarative Paxos distils the logical essence of what makes those proofs work.

9.

Section
9
concludes with conclusions and reflections on future work.

2
Three truth-values

2.1
Basic definition of
{\mathbf{3}}
, and some truth-tables

Definition 2.1.1

(Three-valued truth-values)
.

Let
{\mathbf{3}}
be the poset of
truth-values
with elements
{\mathbf{f}}
(false),
{\mathbf{b}}
(broken), and
{\mathbf{t}}
(true) ordered as

{\mathbf{f}}<{\mathbf{b}}<{\mathbf{t}}.

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\mathrel{\to}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}\end{array}\\[30.1388pt]
\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}\begin{array}[]{cc}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0ptp\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0ptp\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0ptp\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0ptp\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0ptp\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathsf{F}\hskip-1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0ptp\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}\end{array}\\[30.1388pt]
\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Rightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\leq$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}\end{array}\end{array}

Above, the vertical axis of a table indicates values for
p
; the horizontal axis (if nontrivial) denotes values for
q
.

Figure 1
:
Truth-tables for some operations on
{\mathbf{3}}
(Remark
2.1.2
)

\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftarrow$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightarrow}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftharpoonup$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightharpoonup}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c c c c}p\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}q&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{b}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}&{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{f}}&{\mathbf{t}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 2
:
Truth-tables for notions of equivalence (Definition
2.3.6
)

Remark 2.1.2
.

We start with some elementary observations about
{\mathbf{3}}
.

1.

{\mathbf{3}}
has bottom element
{\mathbf{f}}
, top element
{\mathbf{t}}
, and meets
\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
and joins
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
.
This makes it a
finite lattice
.

2.

{\mathbf{3}}
has a negation operation
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
.

3.

{\mathbf{3}}
has several natural modalities that identify truth-values, including
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
,
\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
,
\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt
,
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
.

For example, the reader can examine the truth-table for
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
in Figure
1
and see that it identifies elements of
\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
.
13
13

13

One could also call these modalities
characteristic functions
for various subsets of
{\mathbf{3}}
, by which we mean functions which (literally) return
{\mathbf{t}}
on elements in the set and
{\mathbf{f}}
on elements not in the set.

4.

{\mathbf{3}}
also has multiple notions of implication and equivalence, some of which are illustrated in Figures
1
and
2
.
We will discuss these implications in Subsection
2.3
.

Remark 2.1.3
.

We will use the third truth-value
{\mathbf{b}}
to represent participants in a distributed algorithm
crashing
(or
{\mathbf{b}}
reaking).
The method is simple: the truth-value of a predicate describing the outcome of a crashed computation, is deemed to be
{\mathbf{b}}
.

This is similar in spirit to how in domain theory an additional value — usually written
\bot
— is added to a denotation to represent nontermination; the function-value of a nonterminating computation is deemed to be
\bot

[
AJ94
]
.
The nonterminating computation does not return a value, but we represent this absence of a value in the denotation, using
\bot
.

In our declarative axiomatisation of Paxos, we use
{\mathbf{b}}
in much the same way, as a truth-value to describe the
absence
of a return value (either
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
) from participants that have crashed.

2.2
Valid and correct truth-values

Definition 2.2.1
.

1.

Define subsets
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}},{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}},{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{true}},{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{false}}\subseteq{\mathbf{3}}
by

{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{true}}=\{{\mathbf{t}}\}\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{false}}=\{{\mathbf{f}}\}.

2.

We refer to
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}
as being
valid
/
invalid
,
correct
/
incorrect
, and
true
/
untrue
, and
false
/
unfalse
14
14

14

…‘unfalse’ is ‘
{\mathbf{t}}
-or-
{\mathbf{b}}
’, not ‘
{\mathbf{t}}
’. Similarly, ‘untrue’ is
{\mathbf{b}}
-or-
{\mathbf{f}}
. We cannot resist stating the following gem of three-valued clarity: not-
{\mathbf{t}}
, is not
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{t}}
, and not-
{\mathbf{f}}
is not
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{f}}
, and not-
{\mathbf{b}}

is
not-
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{b}}
, because
{\mathbf{b}}
is
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{b}}
.
depending on whether it is / is not in
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
,
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}
,
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{true}}
, and
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{false}}
respectively.

3.

Define
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
(read ‘
\mathit{tv}
is valid
’) by

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\quad\text{when}\quad\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}},\ \ \text{which just means}\ \ \mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}.

Thus, ‘
\mathit{tv}
is valid’ holds precisely when
\mathit{tv}
is a valid truth-value.

Remark 2.2.2

(Why
{\mathbf{b}}
is valid)
.

We take a moment to discuss Definition
2.2.1
:

1.

We can think of
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
as specific ‘yes/no’ results from the successful completion of some computation.
Therefore, we will call
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}

correct
.

2.

We can think of
{\mathbf{b}}
as representing failure of a computation to complete correctly — e.g. because a participant has crashed and so can return neither
{\mathbf{t}}
nor
{\mathbf{f}}
.

3.

In Definition
2.2.1
we set
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
and define
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
when
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
, and we call
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{b}}

valid
.

This identifies our treatment of validity as being that in Priest’s
logic of paradox
LP
[
Pri79
]
, which sets
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
to be valid; the jargon is that
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
are
designated
.
This is as opposed to Kleene’s
K_{3}
, which makes the other choice of designating only
{\mathbf{t}}

[
Kle52
]
.

It might seem strange at first to let
{\mathbf{b}}
be valid; if
{\mathbf{b}}
represents the (non)behaviour of a crashed participant, then surely crashing should
not
be valid?

Not necessarily.
To see why, consider that many of the assertions we consider will have the form

‘if
p
is not crashed, then
\vDash\phi
at
p
’,

or equivalently

‘either
p
is crashed, or
\vDash\phi
at
p
’.

If we let
{\mathbf{b}}
be valid (i.e. if we define
\vDash{\mathbf{b}}
to hold), then we can simplify the above to the conveniently succinct assertion

‘
\vDash\phi
at
p
’.

The ‘if
p
is not crashed’ precondition gets swallowed up into the fact that if
p
is crashed then
\phi
returns
{\mathbf{b}}
, and
\vDash{\mathbf{b}}
so there is nothing to prove.

Remark 2.2.3
.

Later on in Definition
3.6.1
(
1
) we will extend validity to predicates-in-context, just by evaluating a predicate-in-context to a truth-value using a denotation function, and checking that truth-value is valid in the sense we define here.

We make some elementary observations about
{\mathbf{3}}
and Definition
2.2.1
:

Lemma 2.2.4
.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$},\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}
are truth-values.
Then:

1.

\vDash{\mathbf{t}}
and
\vDash{\mathbf{b}}
.

2.

\not\vDash{\mathbf{f}}
.

3.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
if and only if
{\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}}\lor{\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}}
.

4.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
if and only if
{\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}}\land{\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Fact of Definition
2.2.1
and the truth-tables in Figure
1
.
∎

Negation is conspicuous by its absence in Lemma
2.2.4
.
We consider this next:

Lemma 2.2.5
.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}
is a truth-value.
Then:

1.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
, and as a corollary
\not\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
implies
\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
.

2.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
is possible, and as corollaries:
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
does not necessarily imply
\not\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
; and it is possible that
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
and
\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
both hold.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Using Lemma
2.2.4
(3) it would suffice to show that
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
or
\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
.
So suppose
\not\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
.
By Definition
2.2.1
(
3
)
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\not\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
, thus
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}={\mathbf{f}}
and by Figure
1

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}={\mathbf{t}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
and so
\vDash{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
.

2.

We just take
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}={\mathbf{b}}
and check from Figure
1
that
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{b}}
.
We use Lemma
2.2.4
(1&4).
∎

Remark 2.2.6
.

Some comments on Lemma
2.2.5
:

•

Lemma
2.2.5
(
2
) notes that our notion of validity in three-valued logic is naturally
paraconsistent
, meaning that it is possible for a thing, and the negation of that thing, to both be valid.
This is a feature of
{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbf{3}}
, not a bug, and we will make good use of it later when we use
{\mathbf{b}}
as a return value for ‘I return no correct truth-value’.

•

Lemma
2.2.5
(
1
) asserts validity of the
excluded middle
(‘
\phi
-or-negation-of-
\phi
’), but its corollaries implicitly illustrate another fact of three-valued logic, by what they do
not
say:
looking at the proof of Lemma
2.2.5
(
1
), it is clearly also true that
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
, and that
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
, and that
\not\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
implies
\vDash\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
, and also that
\not\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
.

These are stronger assertions, so why did we not state them in the lemma instead?
Because we will not need the result in those forms later.

In two-valued logic, if something is true then it is probably a useful lemma.
In contrast, in three-valued logic, there is so much more structure with the third truth-value that more things are
true
than are necessarily
useful
.
So, we have to pick and choose our lemmas, and it is not always the case that the strongest form of a result is the most useful.

Lemmas
2.2.7
and
2.2.8
are easy, but they spell out some useful facts about truth-values and validity judgements:

Lemma 2.2.7
.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}
is a truth-value.
Then:

1.

{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}\cap{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}={\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{true}}
.

In words: a truth-value is true if and only if it is correct and valid.

2.

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
if and only if
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{true}}
.

In words: ‘
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
is valid’ holds if and only if
\mathit{tv}
is true.

3.

\vDash\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
if and only if
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{false}}
.

In words: ‘
\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
is valid’ holds if and only if
\mathit{tv}
is false.

4.

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
if and only if
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
if and only if
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
.

In words: ‘
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
is valid’ if and only if
\mathit{tv}
is valid.
15
15

15

Note that
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
is not necessarily
literally equal
to
\mathit{tv}
, in the sense of
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
from Definition
2.3.6
. For example,
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt{\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{t}}\neq{\mathbf{b}}
.

5.

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
if and only if
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}
.

In words: ‘
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
is valid’ if and only if
\mathit{tv}
is correct.

Proof.

Part
1
is a fact of sets.
Parts
2
and
3
and
4
and
5
are just from the truth-tables for
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
,
\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
,
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
in Figure
1
and Definition
2.2.1
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.8
.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}
is a truth-value.
Then the following are all equivalent:

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}=\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}=\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathsf{F}\hskip-1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}=\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}

Informally — we do not build the machinery to make this formal, but it may still be a helpful observation — we can write that the modality
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
is equivalent to the modal equivalences
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt=\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt=\mbox{$\mathit{id}$}
and
\mathsf{F}\hskip-1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt=\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt={\boldsymbol{\neg}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.2.7
(
5
) and Definition
2.2.1
(
1
),

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
when

\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}={\mathbf{t}}\ \lor\ \mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}={\mathbf{f}}\quad\text{and when}\quad\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\neq{\mathbf{b}}.

We check the truth-tables in Figure
1
, and see that all of the other statements above precisely characterise the same condition.
∎

2.3
The rich implication and equivalence structure of
{\mathbf{3}}

2.3.1
Weak and strong implications, and others

Three-valued logic supports many kinds of implication, as has already been noted
[
AAZ11
, note 5, page 22]
.
We discuss two in Definition
2.3.1
, and mention two more in Remark
2.3.4
:

Definition 2.3.1
.

1.

Define
weak implication

\mathrel{\to}
, using
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
and
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
, as follows:

\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\to}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}=({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$})\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}.

This ‘not-or’ equality identifies
\mathrel{\to}
as what some would call
material implication
; it is the natural generalisation of Boolean ‘not-or’ implication to the three-valued setting.

2.

Define
strong implication

\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
, using
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
, as follows:

\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}=\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\to}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}.

Strong implication appears to be original to this paper, so far as we know.
The closest explicit analogue we can find in the literature is that it is equal to the contrapositive of a connective
p\supset q
from page 278 of
[
Avr91
]
; that is,
p\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}q
in our notation is equal to
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q\supset{\boldsymbol{\neg}}p
in Avron’s notation.

These and other encodings are assembled in Figure
6
.

We motivate the weak and strong implications from Definition
2.3.1
(
1
&
2
):

Remark 2.3.2

(How to interpret
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
)
.

For this Remark, let us interpret
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in{\mathbf{3}}
as the result of a computation process which either succeeds and returns true (truth-value
{\mathbf{t}}
), or it succeeds and returns false (truth-value
{\mathbf{f}}
), or the process does not succeed (e.g. it crashes) and it returns a broken value / no value (represented by the truth-value
{\mathbf{b}}
).
Recall also from Definition
2.2.1
(
3
) that
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
means
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}
.
Then by this reading:

1.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
means
“the process
\mathit{tv}
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
, if valid”
.

2.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\to}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
means
“
if
process
\mathit{tv}
returns true
then
process
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
, if valid”
.

Implication does not imply sequentiality: we are just making a logical assertion about outcomes.

3.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
means
“
if
process
\mathit{tv}
returns true
then
process
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
is
also
valid, and it returns
{\mathbf{t}}
”
.

We can sum this up as a
modus ponens
result, which relates the validity
\vDash
from Definition
2.2.1
, the weak implication
\mathrel{\to}
and strong implication
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
from Figure
1
, to real-world implication:

Proposition 2.3.3

(Weak and strong modus ponens)
.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$},\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}
are truth-values.
Then:

1.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\to}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
if and only if
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
implies
\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
.

In words: ‘
\mathit{tv}
weakly implies
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
’ is valid, if and only if
\mathit{tv}
is true implies
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
is valid (but
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
need not be true).

We may call this logical equivalence
weak modus ponens
.

2.

\vDash\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
if and only if
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}
implies
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
.

In words: ‘
\mathit{tv}
strongly implies
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
’ is valid if and only if
\mathit{tv}
is true implies
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
is true.

We may call this logical equivalence
strong modus ponens
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

By direct inspection of the truth-table for
\mathrel{\to}
in Figure
1
, using Definition
2.2.1
(
3
).

2.

By a similar direct inspection.
∎

Remark 2.3.4

(Explicit and implicit implications)
.

So far, the structure we have seen (the lattice structure, the negation, the modalities, and the two implications
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
) is reflected explicitly in the syntax of our logic in Figures
3
and
6
.
For instance, Figure
3
has a unary negation connective
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
whose denotation as per Figure
5
is precisely the negation operator
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
in Figure
1
.
So far, so explicit.

However,
{\mathbf{3}}
has further implication structure, which we do not mention explicitly in our logic, but which is still there, is important, and which appears implicitly in the proofs.
Notably:

1.

An implication

\Rightarrow

defined by
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Rightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}=(\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$})\mathrel{\to}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
has a truth-table spelled out in Figure
1
(bottom left).
This can be read as “if
\mathit{tv}
is valid then
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
is valid”.

\Rightarrow

is clearly implicit in results like Proposition
4.4.3
– see all the parts whose statement uses logical implication
\Longrightarrow
— and in Proposition
4.8.8
.

2.

Another implication is implicit in the use of
\leq
in results like Lemma
3.5.6
and Corollary
4.3.2
.
That symbol refers to the lattice order on
{\mathbf{3}}
, but this could easily be internalised in our logic — e.g. we can set
\mathit{tv}

\leq

\mathit{tv^{\prime}}
to be
(\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime})\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\Rightarrow$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime})
, to obtain the connective

\leq

also illustrated in Figure
1
(bottom right).

So in fact, we use all four of

\Rightarrow

and

\leq

, and
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
.

The reason that
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
make it into our logic as (sugared) syntax, and

\Rightarrow

and
\leq
do not, is that
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
naturally arise in our axioms (see most of the axioms in Figure
11
) whereas we do not need

\Rightarrow

or

\leq

in the same way for the axiomatisations in this paper.
Different axiomatisations of different algorithms might make different demands on our logic, and it would be straightforward to include (sugar for) connectives accordingly.

Remark 2.3.5
.

A word of warning:
\mathrel{\to}
curries with itself as the reader might expect;
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
displays currying behaviour too, but via interactions with
\mathrel{\to}
.
In more detail, the following yield equal truth-tables:

\begin{array}[]{c@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }c@{\ \ }c}p\mathrel{\to}(q\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}r)&\text{equals}&(p\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}r\\
p\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(q\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}r)&\text{equals}&p\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(q\mathrel{\to}r)&\text{equals}&(p\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0ptq)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}r\\
p\mathrel{\to}(q\mathrel{\to}r)&\text{equals}&(p\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q)\mathrel{\to}r\end{array}

All three lines represent distinct truth-tables.
Note in particular that
p\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(q\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}r)
is not equal to
(p\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}q)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}r
.

2.3.2
Equivalence
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}

Our three-valued domain of truth-values
{\mathbf{3}}
has rich notions of equivalence, to match its rich notions of implication.
We mention three of them:

Definition 2.3.6

(Notions of equivalence in
{\mathbf{3}}
)
.

Define
\phi\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftarrow$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightarrow}\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftharpoonup$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightharpoonup}\phi^{\prime}
and
\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}
by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}\phi\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftarrow$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightarrow}\phi^{\prime}=&(\phi\mathrel{\to}\phi^{\prime})\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(\phi^{\prime}\mathrel{\to}\phi)\\
\phi\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftharpoonup$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightharpoonup}\phi^{\prime}=&(\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\phi^{\prime})\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(\phi^{\prime}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\phi)\\
\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}=&(\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi^{\prime})\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}(\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi^{\prime})\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}(\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi^{\prime})\end{array}

Truth-tables for
\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftarrow$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightarrow}
,
\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftharpoonup$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightharpoonup}
, and
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}
are given in Figure
2
, and we unpack this further in Remark
2.3.7
.

Remark 2.3.7
.

Figure
2
gives us three notions of equivalence:
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftarrow$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightarrow}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
,
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftharpoonup$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightharpoonup}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
, and
\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mbox{$\mathit{tv}$}^{\prime}
.
Of the three,
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}
will be most useful to us later.
We will consider it in our predicate language in Subsection
4.1
, and (for instance) it will help us to express notions of extensional equivalence in Figure
9
.

We will call
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}

literal equivalence
, and
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}
appears in the sugared syntax of Figure
6
.
As per the discussion in Remark
2.3.4
, there would be no barrier to including
\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftarrow$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightarrow}
or
\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{$\leftharpoonup$\hss}\hskip-0.86108pt\rightharpoonup}
but we do not need to for now.

3
Three-valued Fixpoint Quorum Logic (
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
)

3.1
A preliminary: semitopologies

Remark 3.1.1
.

A semitopology is like a topology, but generalised to not require that intersections of open sets necessarily be opens.

The intuition of an open set is an
actionable coalition
; a set of participants with the voting power (or other resources) to make updates to their local state.
The reader might be familiar with the notion of
quorum
from literature like
[
Lam98
,
MR98
,
NW94
]
, or the notion of
winning coalition
in social choice theory (the theory of voting)
[
Rik62
, Item 5, page 40]
.
Semitopologies are similar to this, except that open sets are always closed under arbitrary (possibly empty) unions, whereas

•

quorums may be closed under arbitrary nonempty unions (in which case the correspondence is precise, except that
\varnothing
is an open set but is not a quorum), or

•

quorums may not be closed under arbitrary unions (but usually it is harmless to suppose that they are).
16
16

16

The reader who comes from a distributed systems background might be used to
threshold
quorum systems, where quorums have the form ‘more than
k
participants’ for some
k
.
These quorum systems are closed under unions, but they are also
up-closed
; if
Q
is a quorum and
Q\subseteq Q^{\prime}
then
Q^{\prime}
is a quorum.
This is an important special case, but there
are
natural systems of actionable coalitions — quorums, in the reader’s language — that are not up-closed.
For example, consider a quorum of Tezos nodes
T
, and a quorum of Ethereum nodes
E
, and a single bridging node
b
whose job is to facilitate token transfers between the two. Then
T
is a quorum, and
T\cup\{b\}\cup E
is a quorum of the combined system, but
T\cup\{b\}
and
\{b\}\cup E
are
not
quorums because the bridging node requires one quorum from each system to agree to a token transfer before this transfer is considered actionable.
To take a more domestic example, consider the situation of sampling a packet of Pringles crisps in a new flavour (UK English; in US English they are called chips).
You may be disciplined and have no crisps, or you may eat the top crisp from the packet and not like the flavour — but if you do have that one crisp, and you
do
like the flavour, then the only possible remaining action is to eat the entire packet.
Having (say) three crisps and stopping there is not an option.
There is even a word for this: more-ish.
We can analyse this situation mathematically as the
more-ish semitopology
, which is non-up-closed, containing the empty set
\varnothing
, a singleton set
\{x\}
(for some
x
which we call the
first element
), and then the whole set of elements.

Our presentation of semitopologies will be quite brief — we just define the parts we need to give semantics to the modalities in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
— and the reader who wants to know more is referred to previous work
[
Gab24
]
.

Definition 3.1.2
.

A
semitopology
is a pair
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
where:

1.

\mathord{\text{Point}}
is a set of
points
, which following terminology from distributed systems one might also call
participants
,
processes
,
parties
, or
nodes
.

2.

\mathord{\text{Open}}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathit{pow}$}(\mathord{\text{Point}})
is a set of
open sets
such that:

(a)

If
\mathcal{O}\subseteq\mathord{\text{Open}}
then
\bigcup\mathcal{O}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}
.
In words:
\mathord{\text{Open}}
is closed under arbitrary (possibly empty) unions.

(b)

\mathord{\text{Point}}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}
.

We may write
\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
for the set of nonempty open sets, so

\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}=\{O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}\mid O\neq\varnothing\}.

Notation 3.1.3
.

Call the semitopology
(\varnothing,\{\varnothing\})
the
empty semitopology
.
Call a semitopology that is not the empty semitopology, a
nonempty semitopology
.
It is easy to check that a semitopology is nonempty if and only if its set of points is nonempty.

Definition 3.1.4

(All-but-
f
-many semitopology)
.

1.

Suppose
X
is a finite set and
N\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
.
Define
\mbox{$\mathit{pow}$}_{\geq N}(X)
to be the set of subsets of
X
that contain at least
N
elements.
In symbols:

\mbox{$\mathit{pow}$}_{\geq N}(X)=\{O\subseteq X\mid\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}O\geq N\}.

2.

Suppose
N,f\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
N\geq f
.
Define a semitopology
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
by

\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)=\bigl(\{0\dots N{\shortminus}1\},\{\varnothing\}\cup\mbox{$\mathit{pow}$}_{\geq N{\shortminus}f}(\{0\dots N{\shortminus}1\}\bigr)\bigr).

Thus: points are numbers
0\leq n<N
; and nonempty open sets are sets of points of cardinality at least
N{\shortminus}f
.

Lemma 3.1.5
.

Suppose
N,f\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
N\geq f
.
Then:

1.

\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
is indeed a semitopology.

2.

\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
is a topology precisely when
f\in\{N,N{\shortminus}1,0\}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

It is routine to check that the conditions on open sets in Definition
3.1.2
are satisfied.
The key observation is just the elementary fact that if
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}O\geq N{\shortminus}f
and
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}O^{\prime}\geq N{\shortminus}f
then
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(O\cup O^{\prime})\geq N{\shortminus}f
.

2.

For
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
to be a topology, open sets need to be closed under intersections.
It is routine to check that this can happen only when
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
is the full powerset (so
f=N
or
f=N{\shortminus}1
) or when open sets are just
\varnothing
and
\mathord{\text{Point}}
(so
f=0
).
∎

Remark 3.1.6
.

The original Paxos paper by Lamport
[
Lam98
]
used semitopologies of the form
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
where
N=2n
and
f=n{\shortminus}1
, for
n\geq 1
(i.e. open sets are strict majorities of points).
This invites a question: why bother with semitopologies at all?

1.

Because we can.
More specifically, our axiomatisation is parametric over the underlying semitopology.
As good mathematicians, if we can generalise at no cost, then we should.
17
17

17

…and this applies not just in mathematics papers. Software engineers follow a similar principle and have many names for it: e.g. encapsulation, APIs, modules, classes, typeclasses, and so on.

2.

Semitopology not only generalises; it simplifies.
The only properties of
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
that we will need, are precisely that it is a semitopology.
If all we need about
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
is its semitopological structure, then we should work with that structure because that is what actually matters.

3.

Viewing
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
as a semitopology, rather than just as a collection of quorums, adds real meaning and understanding.
In particular, the notions of ‘dense subset’ and ‘nonempty open interior’ will be relevant to understanding the axioms, and these arise naturally in the predicates for declarative axiomatisations of distributed algorithms.
These are clearly native topological notions, so the mathematics is speaking to us here, and what it has to say is inherently topological.

3.2
Syntax of the logic

We now develop our three-valued modal fixpoint logic for reasoning about distributed systems.
We do this in the following order:
signatures

\Sigma
,
models

\mathcal{M}
,
contexts

\mathit{ctx}
, and finally
syntax
.
We develop denotation for this syntax in Subsection
3.3
.
For quick reference, the reader can just look at the core syntax and denotation in Figures
3
and
5
.

Definition 3.2.1

(Signatures)
.

1.

Fix
\mathsf{VarSymb}
a countably infinite set of
variable symbols

a,b,c\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
.
This set will never change and we may assume it henceforth.

2.

Fix a
fixpoint variable symbol

\mathsf{X}
(we will only need one of these, though there is no inherent obstacle to having more).

3.

A
signature

\Sigma
is a pair

\Sigma=(\mathord{\text{PredSymb}},\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$})

where:

(a)

\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
is a (possibly finite) set of
predicate symbols

\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
.

(b)

\mathit{arity}
is an
arity function

\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
mapping each predicate symbol in
\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
to a nonnegative natural number (which is intuitively its number of arguments).

4.

We may write a signature
\Sigma
in list form as

\Sigma=[\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}^{\prime}:i,\ \mathsf{Q}:j,\ \dots]

meaning that
\mathsf{P},\mathsf{P}^{\prime},\mathsf{Q}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
and
\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})=\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P}^{\prime})=i
and
\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{Q})=j
and so on.

5.

If the signature in part
3a
above contains no predicate symbols then we call it
empty
; otherwise we call it nonempty.
There is only one empty signature, which we will call
the empty signature
.
18
18

18

It is not particularly useful.

Notation 3.2.2
.

We mention some standard terminology:

1.

If
\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})=0
then we may call
\mathsf{P}

nullary
or a
predicate constant symbol
.
19
19

19

{\mathbf{b}}
is a predicate constant symbol, but it is special so we give it a distinct identity in the syntax.

2.

If
\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})=1
then we may call
\mathsf{P}
a
unary
predicate symbol.

3.

We may call a predicate of the form
\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
or
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
an
atomic predicate
.

Definition 3.2.3

(Models)
.

A
model

\mathcal{M}
is a tuple
\mathcal{M}=(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}},\mathord{\text{Value}})
where:

1.

(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a nonempty semitopology (Definition
3.1.2
and Notation
3.1.3
), which we may call the
underlying semitopology
of the model.
20
20

20

We insist on nonemptiness to avoid that
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
in Definition
3.6.1
(
3
), meaning
\forall p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.(n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi)
, might be vacuously satisfied because there are no points.
Specifically, Lemma
4.4.2
(
1
&
3
) and Proposition
4.7.14
(
1
&
3
) fail on the edge case of the empty semitopology. Rather than carry a side-condition in these results and their consequences, it is convenient to just exclude the edge case.
Just to really spell this out, we would also have
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
on the empty semitopology. This is not wrong —
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{\bot}}

does
hold for every
p\in\varnothing
, vacuously and regardless of what
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{\bot}}
even means! — but this is arguably pathological. Again, we just exclude this case.

We may call
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
a
point
or a
participant
; also
party
,
process
, or
node
would be consistent with the literature.
For our purposes, these are all synonyms: the terminology
point
is from topology;
participant
,
party
, and
process
are from distributed systems; and
node
is from graph theory (thinking of a network as a graph of communicating processes).

2.

\mathord{\text{Value}}
is a nonempty set of
values
.
21
21

21

We assume
\mathord{\text{Value}}
is nonempty to avoid the degenerate case where a universal quantification over values holds just because there are no values on which to test it. See in particular the argument in Proposition
5.4.2
(
3
).

For our purposes we will not care what values are, so long as some exist, so we do not put any type system on values or endow them with structure; but we could easily do so if required.

Definition 3.2.4

(Contexts)
.

Suppose
\Sigma=(\mathord{\text{PredSymb}},\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$})
is a signature, and
\mathcal{M}=(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}},\mathord{\text{Value}})
is a model.

1.

A
stage
is a nonnegative number
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
.

2.

A
valuation

\varsigma
inputs a predicate symbol
\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
and a stage
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and a point
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and returns a function
\varsigma(\mathsf{P})_{n,p}:\mathord{\text{Value}}^{\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})}\to{\mathbf{3}}
.

We write the set of valuations
\mathord{\text{Valuation}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
.

3.

A
context

\mathit{ctx}
is a tuple
(n,p,O,\varsigma)
where
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
is a stage,
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
is a point,
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
is a nonempty open set, and
\varsigma\in\mathsf{Valuation}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
is a valuation.

We write the set of contexts
\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
; if
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
are understood, we may write this just
\mathsf{Ctx}
.

4.

A
context valuation
is a function
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
.
For reference, context valuations will inherit a bounded lattice structure from
{\mathbf{3}}
in the natural way; see Figure
4
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}t::=&(a\in\mathsf{VarSymb})\mid(v\in\mathord{\text{Value}})\\
\phi::=&{\mathbf{b}}\mid t{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}t\mid(\mathsf{P}(\overbrace{t,\dots,t}^{\scalebox{0.65}{$\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})$}}):\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}})\\
\mid&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\mid\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mid\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mid{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mid{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\\
\mid&\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi^{\prime}\\
\mid&\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mid\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\\
\mid&\mathsf{X}\mid\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi\mid(\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}})\end{array}

Figure 3
:
Term and predicate syntax of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
(Definition
3.2.5
)

Definition 3.2.5

(Syntax)
.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a signature (Definition
3.2.1
) and
\mathcal{M}
is a model (Definition
3.2.3
).

1.

Define the syntax of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
over
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
as in Figure
3
.

In this syntax,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
binds
a
in
\phi
, and
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
binds
\mathsf{X}
in
\phi
.
We call a term or predicate with no free variable symbols or fixpoint variable symbols
closed
.

2.

One special case will be of particular interest: we will write

‘suppose
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate’,

and often we do not really care about
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi

per se
, except that when this is a closed predicate it means that
a\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
and
\phi
is a predicate whose set of free variables is at most
\{a\}
.
See for example Remark
4.6.3
and Lemma
4.6.5
.

3.

We define substitution of variable symbols / fixpoint variable symbols by values / context valuations as usual, and write these
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
and
\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]
respectively.

Remark 3.2.6
.

We apply
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to Paxos in this document, but we see this logic as having independent interest and a broader relevance for specifying and reasoning about the kinds of algorithms that get written for distributed systems.
Their hallmark is a requirement for distributed collaborative action — action by quorums, or actionable coalitions, or winning coalitions; these things have many names — to progress an algorithm to its next step.

Example 3.2.7
.

We give some example predicates and suggest intuitive meanings for them.
We do not yet have the denotation (Figure
5
) nor the rich derived syntax (Figure
6
) that we build later, so these examples are simple and informal, but we hope they will be helpful.

Assume our signature has a unary predicate symbol
\mathsf{output}
(representing the output of some process), and assume
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
are values.

1.

\mathsf{output}(v)
means ‘where we are, today, if the process succeeds, then it outputs
v
’ and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{output}(v^{\prime})
means ‘where we are, if the process succeeds, then it does not output
v^{\prime}
’.

2.

\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{output}(v)
means ‘where we are, today, the process succeeds and outputs
v
’.

3.

\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{output}(v)
means ‘there is a place where, today, if the process succeeds, then it outputs
v
’.

4.

\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{output}(v)
means ‘where we are, tomorrow, the process succeeds and outputs
v
’.

5.

\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{output}(v)
means ‘where we are, yesterday, the process succeeded and output
v
’.
This assertion is subtly stronger than it might seem; our notion of time starts at day 0, at which by convention all assertions about yesterday are false.
See Remark
3.3.4
(
7
).

6.

{\mathbf{b}}
means ‘where we are, today, the process (which we have not named with a predicate symbol) crashes’.

More sophisticated examples will appear later; notably in the derived syntax in Figure
6
, in Remark
3.5.1
, and of course when we apply our logic to axiomatise distributed algorithms.

Remark 3.2.8

(Three pedant points)
.

The thing about being pedantic is that it does not matter, until it does.
We now discuss three pedant points, which may not matter …until perhaps a moment arrives when they do:

1.

We may treat
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
as fixed parameters, and elide them.
In practice this means that we may write ‘suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate’ instead of ‘suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate in the syntax of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
over
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
’.
But note next point.

2.

Our syntax from Definition
3.2.5
and Figure
3
contains denotational elements:
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})}
.
