In case the reader has not seen denotations added directly to syntax before, this is a standard technical device, in the style for example of Shoenfeld
[
Sho67
]
(page 18, Subsection 2.5 “for each individual
a\in\mathcal{A}
we choose a new constant, called the
name
of
a
”) or Smullyan
[
Smu68
]
(page 46, ‘formulas with constants in
\mathsf{U}
’).
22
22

22

The alternative is augment our notion of context in Definition
3.2.4
(
3
) with two valuations, e.g.
\sigma
mapping
a\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
to
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
, and some element
\varkappa
representing a value for
\mathsf{X}
respectively. We prefer not to do this because our notion of context is already a 4-tuple
(n,p,O,\varsigma)
, and
(n,p,O,\varsigma,\sigma,\varkappa)
would be too much of a notational handful.

It is also key to standard proofs of well-known mathematical results, like the
Lindenbaum-Tarski algebras
used for proving completeness, and for proving the upward and downward Löwenheim-Skolem theorems
[
CK90
]
.

In this paper, it means that when we write ‘suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate’ then this statement is perfectly correct and well-defined but it
really does matter
what the signature and model are (even if the
precise
choice of model will not matter to the theorems we prove), because closed predicates
\phi
can include elements from the denotation such as
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})}
(this function-space is determined by both the signature
\Sigma
and the model
\mathcal{M}
) and
v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}
for some
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
(
\mathord{\text{Value}}
is determined by the choice of model
\mathcal{M}
).

Note also that the choice of model matters for validity (as it must!).
For example: in Definition
4.1.1
we define equivalence of predicates (and we explicitly mention
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
);
if
\mathcal{M}
has only a single value
v
, then
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a,b.a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}b
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v
are equivalent to
{\mathbf{t}}
; and if
\mathcal{M}
has more than one value then
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a,b.a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}b
is equivalent to
{\mathbf{f}}
—
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is shorthand for
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi
; see Figure
6
.
In contrast: in Lemma
4.1.2
we elide
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
for brevity, and just write ‘suppose
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are closed predicates and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context’, without specifying over what signature or model, because this is clear in context.

The reader can mentally add
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
everywhere if they like.
We will always be precise and explicit where it counts.

3.

We may quantify over
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
in our formal syntax of predicates, writing things like
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v^{\prime}.v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}
as predicates.
Technically this is wrong and is not syntax!
Figure
3
permits e.g.
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a^{\prime}.a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}a^{\prime}
where
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
(Definition
3.2.1
(
1
)); it does not permit
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v
in the formal syntax of predicates where
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
, so writing e.g.
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v^{\prime}.v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}
as if it were formal syntax of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
is an abuse of notation.

However,
a
and
a^{\prime}
get instantiated to values
v
and
v^{\prime}
in our denotation (see the line for

\exists

in Figure
5
).
It seems not worth the disruption of maintaining a distinction between
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
(which is syntax, assuming
\phi
is) and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
(which looks like syntax but technically is not).
So in practice this seems to us one example of an abuse of formal notation that is positively useful and clarifies meaning.

If this bothers the reader, then every time they see
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\phi
(and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\phi
, and similar) in our text, they can either declare that
v
here is
actually
a variable symbol in
\mathsf{VarSymb}
which we happen to have called
v
(and not a value in
\mathord{\text{Value}}
after all), or they can pick a fresh variable symbol
a
and replace every unbound
v
in
\phi
with
a
.
Either way they will get well-formed syntax and everything will be as it should be.

Notation exists to serve clarity, and not the other way around, so we will simply ignore this henceforth and carry on writing
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\phi
, just whenever we feel like it, and this will denote just what it says: ‘there exists a
v
such that
\phi
’.
Meaning should always be clear.

Note that an unbound value
v
in a predicate is legal syntax as per Figure
3
;
e.g. in ‘
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v
’,
v
is a value, and this is fine; the (constructive and useful) abuse of notation comes when we write
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v
.

3.3
Denotation (semantics) of the syntax

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}{\boldsymbol{\bot}}=&\lambda\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}.{\mathbf{f}}\\
{\boldsymbol{\top}}=&\lambda\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}.{\mathbf{t}}\\
\bigvee\mathcal{X}=&\lambda\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}.\bigvee\{\varkappa\,\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\mid\varkappa\in\mathcal{X}\}\\
\bigwedge\mathcal{X}=&\lambda\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}.\bigwedge\{\varkappa\,\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\mid\varkappa\in\mathcal{X}\}\\[8.61108pt]
\varkappa\leq\varkappa^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow&\forall\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}.\varkappa\,\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\leq\varkappa^{\prime}\,\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\end{array}

Above,
\mathcal{X}\subseteq{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
and
\varkappa,\varkappa^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
.

Figure 4
:
Bounded complete lattice structure on the function space
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}

We start with an elementary observation about
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
, that it inherits a bounded lattice structure from
{\mathbf{3}}
in the standard way:

Definition 3.3.1
.

Recall from Remark
2.1.2
(
1
) that
{\mathbf{3}}
with ordering
{\mathbf{f}}<{\mathbf{b}}<{\mathbf{t}}
is a finite lattice.
As is standard, the function space
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
(functions from
\mathsf{Ctx}
to
{\mathbf{3}}
) inherits this ordering and lattice structure
pointwise
, as indicated in Figure
4
.

Definition
3.3.1
makes
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
into a
bounded complete lattice
, meaning that it has a least and greatest element, and arbitrary joins (least upper bounds) and meets (greatest lower bounds).
23
23

23

Having a least and greatest element follows from having arbitrary joins and meets; the least element is the join of the empty set, and the greatest element is the meet of the empty set. So in fact a complete lattice is already a bounded complete lattice.

We will need arbitrary meets for the case of
\mu\mathsf{X}
in Figure
5
.

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\mathbf{b}}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&{\mathbf{b}}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\begin{cases}{\mathbf{t}}&v=v^{\prime}\\
{\mathbf{f}}&v\neq v^{\prime}\end{cases}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})})]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\varsigma(\mathsf{P})_{n,p}(v_{1},\dots,v_{\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$}(\mathsf{P})})\\[8.61108pt]
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\,{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\bigvee_{p^{\prime}\in O}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p^{\prime},O,\varsigma}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\bigvee_{O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\bigvee_{v{\in}\mathord{\text{Value}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\\[8.61108pt]
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\bigvee_{\mbox{$\mathit{max}$}(0,n{\shortminus}1)\leq n^{\prime}<n}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O,\varsigma}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n{+}1,p,O,\varsigma}\\[8.61108pt]
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\bigl(\bigwedge\{\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq\varkappa\}\bigr)(n,p,O,\varsigma)\\[4.30554pt]
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\varkappa]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)\end{array}

:
Denotational semantics of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
(Definition
3.3.2
)
