Figure 6
:
Derived syntactic sugar expressions (Definition
3.4.1
)

We are now ready to give a denotation to our syntax:

Definition 3.3.2

(Denotation)
.

Suppose
\Sigma=(\mathord{\text{PredSymb}},\mbox{$\mathit{arity}$})
is a signature (Definition
3.2.1
(
3
)), and
\mathcal{M}
is a model (Definition
3.2.3
).
Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate (Definition
3.2.5
(1)).
Define the
denotation
of
\phi

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})}\quad\text{such that}\quad\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\longmapsto{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}

by the rules in Figure
5
.

We will usually treat
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
as fixed parameters, so we may write
\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
just as
\mathsf{Ctx}
.

Remark 3.3.3
.

Usually when we talk about denotation as defined in Definition
3.3.2
above, the signature
\Sigma
and model
\mathcal{M}
are either understood or do not matter, so we may not mention them explicitly.
Meaning will always be clear.

Remark 3.3.4

(Initial comments on the denotation)
.

We make some initial comments on Figure
5
:

1.

\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
acts as a
fixpoint operator
where
\mathsf{X}
is positive in
\phi
.
24
24

24

However,
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
is well-formed syntax even without positivity, and we will make good use of this generality in axiom
{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}
in Figure
11
. For more details on this, see Remark
6.5.1
(
2
) and Subsection
6.6.3
.

Including a fixpoint operator is a standard method to include the power of recursion / iteration in a logic; an instance of
\mathsf{X}
inside
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
, where
\mathsf{X}
is positive in
\phi
, can be thought of as a recursive call back to
\phi
.
For such
\phi
,
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
is what would be written
\mathrm{LFP}_{\mathsf{X}}\phi
in
[
Imm99
, Chapter 4]
(a Wikipedia article is also accessible and clear
[
Wik24a
]
).
We will discuss this in Subsection
3.5
.

2.

In the clause
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\mathbf{b}}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}={\mathbf{b}}
in Figure
5
, the
{\mathbf{b}}
on the left is the syntax symbol from Figure
3
, and the
{\mathbf{b}}
on the right is
{\mathbf{b}}
-the-truth-value from Definition
2.1.1
.

3.

In the clause for
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
in Figure
5
, the
=
on the left is the syntax symbol from Figure
3
, and the
{\mathbf{t}}
,
{\mathbf{f}}
, and
=
on the right are truth-values and (actual) equality.

We really do mean
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
in this clause (equality of a pair of values
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
) rather than
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[t{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}t^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
(equality of a pair of terms) because we assume
\phi
is closed, and the reader can easily check from Figure
3
that the only closed terms are values.

4.

Similarly for the clauses for
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
and
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
and
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
; the symbols
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
,
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
, and
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
on the left are syntax, and the symbols
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
,
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
, and
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
on the right refer to functions on truth-values as per Figure
1
.

5.

\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
resembles the usual modal operator ‘somewhere’, but note that
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
looks for somewhere
in
O
, where
O
is the nonempty open set in the context.

6.

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
is a modality read ‘for some quorum’ (where quorum = nonempty open set).

7.

The clause

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=\bigvee_{\mbox{$\mathit{max}$}(0,n{\shortminus}1)\leq n^{\prime}<n}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O,\varsigma}
is just a one-line encoding of

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{f}}&n=0\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n{\shortminus}1,p,O,\varsigma}&n>0.\end{cases}

8.

Intuitively, we see that
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
is anti-monotone.
We note further that it is very special in this respect: it is the only naturally
non-monotone
operator in the primitive syntax from Figure
3
.
Any non-monotonicity in the derived syntax from Figure
6
, comes from
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
.

This is deliberate and it is the design feature which powers Lemma
3.5.6
.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
is an expressive language and we will explore it with plenty of exposition.
Meaning will always be precise.

3.4
Derived expressions: simple programming in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}

Definition 3.4.1

(Derived expressions in Figure
6
)
.

The syntax of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
in Figure
3
and Definition
3.2.5
is very expressive.
In the left-hand column of Figure
6
we define some derived expressions.
We can think of this as syntactic sugar, or as some simple programming in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
.

For clarity and convenience we unpack the relevant denotation in the right-hand column.

Remark 3.4.2

(On the complexity of the derived expressions)
.

Figure
6
has a lot in it.

However, we do not need to understand it all at once.
We can think of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
as the core of an abstract descriptive logic programming language; then Figure
6
collects the useful subroutines (in roughly increasing order of complexity).
The reader can read and examine these directly, just like they might dip into the standard libraries of a programming language, or just wait to see how they are used.

In principle we could fully unpack our sugar down to the primitive syntax from Figure
3
, but in practice we work directly with Figure
6
because this is more convenient.

For reference, further discussions of syntax are in Remarks
3.3.4
and
3.5.1
.

Notation 3.4.3
.

We take a moment to recall some standard conventions and terminology:

1.

Implications
\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
associate to the right, so that
\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\psi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\chi
means
\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(\psi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\chi)
.

2.

Unary connectives (like
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
,
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
) bind more strongly than binary connectives (like
\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
and
\mathrel{\to}
), so that
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi
means
(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi
.

3.

The scope of a quantification like
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a
extends as far to the right as possible.
Thus
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi
means
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
.
Note however that
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi
still means
(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi))\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi
, because the

\exists

quantifier is trapped in the scope of the
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
.

4.

All the above said, we will (as is usual) use spacing and some common sense to convey scope.
For example

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\mathbin{\mathrel{\to}}\psi\quad\text{means}\quad{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.(\phi\mathrel{\to}\psi),

and

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\ \mathrel{\to}\psi\quad\text{means}\quad({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi)\mathrel{\to}\psi.

In practice, meaning will always be clear.

Remark 3.4.4
.

The reader may not be surprised that quantifier ordering can matter:
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}b.a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}b
is completely different from
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}b.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}b
.
Modalities are also (weak) quantifiers, so the reader may not be surprised that
\Box\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
(a universal modality followed by an existential one) is different from
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt
(existential followed by universal); the interested reader may enjoy unpacking these to check.

Less immediately obvious is that modality ordering can matter
even between modalities with the same universal/existential
polarity, like
\Box\hskip 0.0pt
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
.

Consider this, for
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\bigwedge_{O^{\prime}{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}\bigwedge_{p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma}\\
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Box\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=&\bigwedge_{p^{\prime}\in O}\bigwedge_{O^{\prime}{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma}\end{array}

In the first case, the quantification is over every
O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
and every
p^{\prime}\in O^{\prime}
.
In the second case, the quantification is over every
O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
and every
p^{\prime}\in O
; that is a completely different thing.

3.5
The operator
\mu\mathsf{X}

Remark 3.5.1
.

Our operator
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
is a powerful construct.
As per Remark
3.3.4
(
1
),
via
\phi
such that
\mathsf{X}
is positive in
\phi
,
\mu
inserts the power of recursion / iteration over
\phi
in the logic, in the following sense:
a mention of
\mathsf{X}
inside
\phi
in
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
can be thought of as a recursive call back to
\phi
, and we can think of
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
as encoding an iterative loop that repeatedly executes a computation encoded in
\phi
, and loops if it encounters
\mathsf{X}
in
\phi
.

(There is also a useful non-positive use of
\mu
in our axioms; see Subsection
6.6.3
. But in this Remark we focus on the positive, fixpoint uses.)

With this intuition, we can look at some of the derived syntax in Figure
6
that uses
\mu
:

1.

{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi=\mu\mathsf{X}.\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{X})
can be imagined as the following loop:

(a)

Set an accumulator to
{\mathbf{f}}
.
25
25

25

We mean ‘accumulator’ in the sense of an ‘accumulator variable’ inside a machine that (we imagine) is running this pseudocode. This is not a technical term from logic.

(b)

If we are at day 0 then terminate and return the value of the accumulator; otherwise go to yesterday.

(c)

Compute the truth-value of
\phi
, and
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
this truth-value with the accumulator.

(d)

Loop back to step
1b
.

2.

The difference between
{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi
and
{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}\phi
is that
{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi
returns the greatest truth-value that
\phi
has taken in the past, whereas
{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}\phi
returns the greatest truth-value that
\phi
has taken today or in the past.

The notation

\mathsf{R}

(‘

\mathsf{R}

’, underlined) is a pun
26
26

26

A bad one perhaps, and the reader may or may not find this pun-acceptable.
on
<
and
\leq
(‘
<
’, underlined).

3.

\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi=\mu\mathsf{X}.\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{X})
can be viewed as executing the following loop:

(a)

Set an accumulator to
{\mathbf{f}}
.

(b)

Go to tomorrow.

(c)

Compute the truth-value of
\phi
and
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
with the accumulator.

(d)

Loop back to step
3b
.

(e)

After infinite time (see Remark
3.5.2
about why that makes sense), return the limit value of the accumulator.

4.

\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mu\mathsf{X}.\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{X})
can be viewed as the following loop:

(a)

Set an accumulator to
{\mathbf{t}}
.

(b)

Go to tomorrow.

(c)

Compute the truth-value of
\phi
, and
\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
this truth-value with the accumulator.

(d)

Loop back to step
4b
.

(e)

After infinite time return the limit value of the accumulator.

5.

\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi=\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
can be understood as follows: it iterates forward in time looking for a time when everywhere (= at every open set
O
, at every point
p\in O
) and forever,
\phi
holds.

6.

The clause

\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]=\mu\mathsf{X}.(\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}(({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})))

looks more imposing, but the idea is simply to

•

iterate back in time looking for when
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
has truth-value
{\mathbf{t}}
, i.e. when
some
value makes
\phi(a)
be true, and

•

if we find such a time then we check if
at that time

\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
has truth-value
{\mathbf{t}}
and

•

only if both steps above succeed, do we return
{\mathbf{t}}
.

Thus, we return
{\mathbf{t}}
when
v
is one of the values that
most recently
make
\phi(a)
be true.

In detail:

(a)

If we are at day 0, terminate with value
{\mathbf{f}}
; otherwise go to yesterday.

(b)

If
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
has truth-value
{\mathbf{t}}
then terminate with value
{\mathbf{t}}
; otherwise continue to the next step.

(c)

If
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
has truth-value
{\mathbf{t}}
then terminate with value
{\mathbf{f}}
; otherwise go to step
6a
.

Remark 3.5.2

(Comment on the omniscient observer)
.

The programs in Remark
3.5.1
above are executed by an omniscient observer who stands outside of the model’s notion of time and space.
This is why (for example)

\mathsf{forever}

can run an infinite loop --- the omniscient observer can observe it because the observer is looking at the model from outside.
27
27

27

Let’s make this concrete. Consider the natural numbers
\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
. We can easily observe this and note that
n{+}1\gneq n
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
; whereas a local observer trapped on the number line and constrained to perceive this line as the passage of time can make a similar observation, but from the point of view of that observer, the calculation involved is never-ending.
Note that even if we could speak with this local observer, it would be no good to appeal to it to ‘just realise that it is on the number line’, because in order to check this to a mathematical certainty the observer would have to …traverse all of its local notion of time to
check
, amongst other things, that
n{+}1\gneq n
. After all, the observer might be on
\mathbb{Z}/p
for some astronomically enormous
p
, i.e. their time might be on a large loop.
The reader interested in a branch of mathematics explicitly devoted to studying these questions, and who is willing to possibly end up seriously doubting the nature of their own reality, can start by reading up on nonstandard models of arithmetic.

Remark 3.5.3
.

The rest of this Subsection will show how our denotation for
\mu
from Figure
5
does indeed yield a fixpoint operator.
The culminating result is Proposition
3.5.7
(which just says that
\mu
is indeed a fixpoint operator for positive predicates) but the key technical lemma is Lemma
3.5.6
(monotonicity of positive predicates).

The reader is welcome to read the rest of this Subsection, but this is all just a standard use of the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem
[
DP02
, Theorem 2.35, page 50]
.

In fact, the real design effort has already happened: we designed the derived syntax of Figure
6
so that it would respect the positivity condition of Definition
3.5.5
, without having to fully expand it out.
For instance, we defined
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
to be
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi
(which clearly puts
\phi
in a positive position) instead of
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
(which is logically equivalent, but which does not).

Remark 3.5.4
.

Our denotation
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\text{-}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
from Definition
3.3.2
and Figure
5
maps predicates to functions in
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
.
One particular consequence of the order on
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
from Definition
3.3.1
is that (denotations of) predicates are naturally ordered, and we can write

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\quad\text{which just means}\quad\forall\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\in\mathsf{Ctx}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\psi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.

We use this fact for example in Lemma
3.5.6
.
We can also use this fact, and the complete lattice structure on
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
from Definition
3.3.1
, to simplify the presentation of the clause for
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
in Figure
5
as follows:

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}=\bigwedge\{\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq\varkappa\}.

Definition 3.5.5
.

Recall the predicate syntax from Figure
3
and suppose
\phi
is a predicate.
Then:

1.

Call a free occurrence of
\mathsf{X}
in
\phi

•

positive
when the occurrence appears under an even number (which may be
0
) of negations
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
, and

•

negative
when the occurrence appears under an odd number of negations.

For example: all free occurrences of
\mathsf{X}
are positive in
\mathsf{X}
and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{X}
and
\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{X}
and
\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{X}
; and the free occurrence of
\mathsf{X}
in
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{X}
is negative.

2.

Call a predicate
\phi

positive
when every free occurrence of
\mathsf{X}
in
\phi
is positive.

Lemma 3.5.6
.

Suppose
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
is a closed predicate.
Then:

1.

If
\phi
is positive then
\varkappa\mapsto{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
is monotone as a function from
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
with the partial order
\leq
from Figure
4
, to itself.
In symbols, for every
\varkappa,\varkappa^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
:

\varkappa\leq\varkappa^{\prime}\quad\text{implies}\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa^{\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}.

2.

If
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi
is positive then
\varkappa\mapsto{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
is antimonotone as a function from
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
with the partial order
\leq
from Figure
4
, to itself.
In symbols, for every
\varkappa,\varkappa^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
:

\varkappa\leq\varkappa^{\prime}\quad\text{implies}\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa^{\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}.

(The meaning of
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\text{-}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\text{-}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
is unpacked in Remark
3.5.4
.)

Proof.

Both parts follow by a routine simultaneous induction on the syntax of
\phi
, using the rules in Figure
5
.
∎

Proposition 3.5.7
.

Suppose
\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi
is a closed predicate and
\phi
is positive.
Then:

1.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
is a least fixpoint of the function
\varphi:{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}\to{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
given by

\varphi(\varkappa)=\lambda\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.

2.

If
\mathit{ctx}
is a context then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mu\mathsf{X}.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.

Proof.

By the Knaster-Tarski fixpoint theorem for
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
as a complete lattice as per Definition
3.3.1
.
A concise but exceptionally clear presentation is in
[
DP02
, Theorem 2.35, page 50]
(a Wikipedia article
[
Wik24b
]
is also accessible and clear).
∎

3.6
Validity

We defined in Definition
2.2.1
what it means for a truth-value to be
valid
; now we extend this notion to predicates-in-context:

Definition 3.6.1

(Validity)
.

Suppose that:

•

\Sigma
is a signature (Definition
3.2.1
(
3
)).
We take
\Sigma
to be fixed for the rest of this Definition, which means that we do not explicitly include it (e.g. as a subscript) in the notation.

•

\mathcal{M}
is a model (Definition
3.2.3
).
Again, we fix
\mathcal{M}
as a parameter for the rest of this definition.

Suppose further that:

•

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}=(n,p,O,\varsigma)
is a context in
\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
, so that by Definition
3.2.4
(
3
)
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation to
\mathcal{M}
.

•

\phi
is a closed predicate in
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
(cf. Remark
3.2.8
(
2
)).

Then we define:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi
means
\vDash{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
, which by Definition
2.2.1
(
3
) means
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
, which by Definition
2.2.1
(
1
) means
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
.

Because this is a core definition, we sum this up for the reader’s clear reference:

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{\vDash{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}\lor{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}.

We read this as
\phi
is valid in the context
\mathit{ctx}
.

2.

n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
means
\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.(n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi)
.

3.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
means
\forall p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.(n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi)
.

4.

\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
means
\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}.\forall p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.(n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi)
.

5.

\vDash\phi
means
\forall\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\in\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M}).(\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi)
.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l@{\quad}l}n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi&\text{means}&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{b}}\}\\[4.30554pt]
\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi&\text{means}&\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}.\forall p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.(n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi)\\[4.30554pt]
\vDash_{\varsigma}\Theta&\text{means}&\forall\theta\in\Theta.({\vDash_{\varsigma}\theta})\\[4.30554pt]
\Theta\vDash\phi&\text{means}&\forall\varsigma\in\mathord{\text{Valuation}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M}).({\vDash_{\varsigma}\Theta})\Longrightarrow({\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi})\end{array}

Figure 7
:
Selected clauses on validity of
\phi
/ validity of
\phi
in a theory
\Theta
(Definitions
3.6.1
and
3.6.2
)

Later on, when we start to build declarative axiomatisations of consensus algorithms, we will express these as
theories
.
We include the relevant definition here, since it follows on naturally from Definition
3.6.1
:

Definition 3.6.2

(Validity in a theory)
.

Suppose that:

•

\Sigma
is a signature and
\mathcal{M}=(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}},\mathord{\text{Value}})
is a model.

•

\phi
is a closed predicate in
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
.

•

\Theta
is a (possibly infinite) set of closed predicates.
28
28

28

It would be unusual for
\Theta
, which is intended to be a collection of axioms, to mention specific elements
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
or
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})}
from a model (cf. Remark
3.2.8
), but the maths does not seem to mind, so we do not exclude this possibility.

Then:

1.

A
theory
is a pair
(\Sigma,\Theta)
of

•

a signature
\Sigma
, and

•

a set
\Theta
of
axioms
, which are closed predicates over
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
.

2.

Given a valuation
\varsigma\in\mathord{\text{Valuation}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
(Definition
3.2.4
(
2
)), we define

\vDash_{\varsigma}\Theta\quad\text{and}\quad\Theta\vDash\phi

as per Figure
7
.
When
\Theta\vDash\phi
holds, we say that
\phi
is
valid in the theory
\Theta
(in
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
).

Examples of theories are in Figures
11
and
12
.

Remark 3.6.3
.

Note the scope of the quantifiers in Definition
3.6.2
.
The assertion
\Theta\vDash\phi
means

\textstyle\forall\varsigma.((\bigwedge_{\theta\in\Theta}\vDash_{\varsigma}\theta)\Longrightarrow(\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi)).

In words: if every
\theta\in\Theta
holds everywhere in the model under the valuation
\varsigma
, then
\phi
holds everywhere in the model under the valuation
\varsigma
.
Spelling this out further we obtain:

\forall\varsigma.\bigl(\forall\theta\in\Theta.\forall n,p,O.\,n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\theta)\Longrightarrow(\forall n,p,O.n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\bigr),

where above
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
,
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
, and
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
.

This pattern of quantifiers is what makes
\Theta
a theory, rather than just a set of assumptions: as a theory,
\Theta
is a restriction on the valuation
\varsigma
.

Remark 3.6.4
.

One final pedant point:
in Definition
3.6.2
(
1
) we defined a theory to be a pair
(\Sigma,\Theta)
of a signature and a set of closed predicates over a signature
\Sigma

and
over a model
\mathcal{M}
.
This technical convenience lets us write axioms that directly reference specific values in the model (instead of introducing explicit constant symbols to our signature, and interpreting them as …values in the model).

We will exploit this to assume a distinguished ‘undefined’ value
\mathsf{undef}
in axiom
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose?)}}
in Figure
11
, as per Definition
6.3.1
(
2
).

3.7
Comments on notation: eliding parameters, and functional notation

Notation 3.7.1

(Eliding parameters)
.

It will be quite common that we make validity judgements where it is clear that part of the context is irrelevant.
For example the reader can check the denotational semantics in Figure
5
and the definition of validity in Definition
3.6.1
(
1
), to see that:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\mathbf{b}}
is valid; we do not need to know the values in
\mathit{ctx}
.

2.

The validity of
n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{P}(v)
depends on
\varsigma
,
n
, and
p
— but not on
O
.

3.

The validity of
n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
depends on
n
and
\varsigma
(and of course on
\phi
), but it does not depend on the values of
p
and
O
.

In such cases, we may omit parameters in the context if they do not matter — we may even omit the entire context if appropriate.
Thus, we may write
\vDash{\mathbf{t}}
,
\vDash a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}a
, and
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

This notation remains within the scope of Definition
3.6.1
— which universally quantifies over any omitted parameter — so omitting parameters in this way is not an abuse of notation.
However, if the reader sees us omit a parameter, there may be two reasons for it:

1.

It may indicate a universal quantification over all possible values.
For example, this is clearly what we intend when we write
\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
in Figure
7
(second row).

2.

It may indicate that we have elided irrelevant parameters.
For example, this is clearly what we intend in the example of
\vDash{\mathbf{t}}
above.

In practice, it will always be clear which is intended and meaning will always be clear.
29
29

29

Actually, meaning will be
clearer
.
In early drafts of this paper, we dutifully included all parameters, whether relevant or not.
But this turned out to be just too confusing: the notational overhead of carrying around irrelevant information was burdensome; but furthermore, it was actually easier to understand the sequents once we dropped irrelevant parameters, because this helped us to keep track of which parameters
did
matter.

\begin{array}[]{l@{\quad}l@{\quad}l@{\quad}l@{\quad}l}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{write}(a)&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{send}(a)&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(a)&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{propose}(a)&(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}(a))[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]\\[4.30554pt]
&\text{may be written as}\\[4.30554pt]
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{write}&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{send}&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{propose}&(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept})\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v\end{array}

Figure 8
:
Abbreviations inspired by higher-order logic (Notation
3.7.2
)

Notation 3.7.2

(Functional notation)
.

We will frequently apply quantifiers (like

\exists

,

\exists_{1}

, or

\forall

) to unary predicate symbols, possibly with modalities.
For example:

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{decide}(a)\quad\text{is}\quad\text{${\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}$ quantifying $a$ in\ $({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{decide})$\ applied to $a$.}

For clarity, we may use a
functional notation
inspired by higher-order logic (HOL) to elide the variable, as illustrated in Figure
8
.
Meaning will always be clear.

See also Remark
6.3.2
for examples of this notation applied in an axiomatisation.

4
Properties of predicates

This Section collects elementary technical results which we will need later.
Some of these results, and their proofs, are more technical than others, but they are not necessarily surprising if we look at what they mean in the model.
Mostly the statements are simple and it is obvious by common sense that they should work (simple is good, because it means greater productivity and fewer errors).

A typical example is Lemma
4.5.2
(
1
): once we look at what it actually says about the model, this is that “if
\phi
is true today, then in the future it will have been true in the past”.

For saying this, a mathematician earns a living?
— we might think.

What is less evident is
which
elementary properties will be important later, nor is it obvious what definitions and structures make these properties all fit together nicely.
So the reader should note that the simplicity of this Section is a design feature, which was only attained in retrospect and after some work.

Broadly speaking, we start this Section with the most elementary results, and build complexity from there.
The reader is welcome to read this Section now, or to skip it and refer back to its results as they are used in later proofs.

4.1
Equivalence of predicates, and some (de Morgan) equivalences

For the rest of this Section we make a fixed but arbitrary choice of signature
\Sigma
(Definition
3.2.1
(
3
)) and model
\mathcal{M}
(Definition
3.2.3
).
All predicates considered will be over
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{M}
, unless otherwise stated.

We generalise equivalence
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}
(Definition
2.3.6
) from truth-values, to predicates viewed as functions from contexts to truth-values:

Definition 4.1.1

(Equivalence of predicates)
.

Suppose
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are closed predicates.

Call
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}

extensionally equivalent
when

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}.

Unpacking Definition
3.3.2
, this means

\forall\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\in\mathsf{Ctx}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M}).({[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}).

For brevity, we may write ‘extensionally equivalent’ just as ‘equivalent’ henceforth.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Suppose
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are closed predicates and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
.

2.

\vDash\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}
if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
(i.e.
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are equivalent as per Definition
4.1.1
).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

By Definition
3.6.1
(
1
)
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}
means
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
, and by the clause for
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}
in Figure
6
this is if and only if
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
.

2.

By Definition
3.6.1
(
5
),
\vDash\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}
means precisely
\forall\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}.\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}
.
We use part
1
of this result.
∎

We will see many equivalences in what follows.
We start by using Definition
4.1.1
and Lemma
4.1.2
to express some standard equivalences in logic, having to do with implication being expressible using negation and disjunction, and with
de Morgan
dualities between modalities and connectives:

Lemma 4.1.3
.

For each of the equivalences
\mbox{$\mathit{LHS}$}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mbox{$\mathit{RHS}$}
listed in Figure
9
, we have that
\mathit{LHS}
is extensionally equal to
\mathit{RHS}
in the sense of Definition
4.1.1
.

Proof.

By routine calculations using Figures
5
and
6
and facts of the lattice
{\mathbf{3}}=({\mathbf{f}}<{\mathbf{b}}<{\mathbf{t}})
.
∎

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l@{\quad}r@{\ }l}\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi^{\prime}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi^{\prime})&\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi^{\prime}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi^{\prime})&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\mathbf{f}}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi\mathrel{\to}{\mathbf{f}}\\
\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\phi^{\prime}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&\phi\mathrel{\to}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi^{\prime}&\phi\mathrel{\to}\phi^{\prime}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi^{\prime}\\
\Box\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi&\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Box\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\\
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\\
\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi&\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\\
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi&\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\phi\\
\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi&\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{F}\hskip-1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi&\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}&{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\end{array}

Figure 9
:
Some easy equivalences

4.2
Two commutation lemmas

We note some simple commutation lemmas.
These results are all easy facts of our model, but it only takes a few lines to spell them out:

Lemma 4.2.1

(Commuting connectives)
.

Suppose
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are closed predicates and suppose

\bullet\in\{\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}},\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \mathsf{M}\in\{\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt,\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\}.

Then

\vDash\mathsf{M}(\phi\bullet\phi^{\prime})\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}(\mathsf{M}\phi\bullet\mathsf{M}\phi^{\prime}).

Proof.

Routine from Lemma
4.1.2
(
2
) and Figures
5
and
6
, and facts of
{\mathbf{3}}
as a lattice.
∎

Lemma 4.2.2

(Commuting modalities)
.

Suppose
\mathsf{Q}\phi
is a closed predicate for

\textstyle\mathsf{Q}\in\{\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt,\Box\hskip 0.0pt,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt,{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}},{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}},\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt,\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt,\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt,\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt,\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{infinitely}$}\hskip 0.0pt,\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt\}\cup\bigcup_{x\in\mathsf{VarSymb}}\{{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}x\}.

Then

\vDash\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{Q}\phi\quad\text{and}\quad\vDash\mathsf{Q}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{Q}\phi.

Proof.

Routine from Lemma
4.1.2
(
2
) and Figures
5
and
6
, and facts of
{\mathbf{3}}
as a lattice.
∎

4.3
Lattice inequalities

Corollary
4.3.2
is in the same spirit as Lemmas
4.2.1
and
4.2.2
.
In practice we usually just use it as a direct fact of the model, but we spell it out anyway.
It relies on this simple fact about lattices:

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Suppose
(\mathcal{L},\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}},\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}})
is a complete lattice and
X
and
Y
are sets and
f:(X\times Y)\to\mathcal{L}
is any function.
Then:

1.

\bigvee_{x}\bigwedge_{y}f(x,y)\leq\bigwedge_{y}\bigvee_{x}f(x,y)
,

2.

It is not in general the case that

\bigwedge_{y}\bigvee_{x}f(x,y)\leq\bigvee_{x}\bigwedge_{y}f(x,y)
.

3.

\bigvee_{x}\bigvee_{y}f(x,y)=\bigvee_{y}\bigvee_{x}f(x,y)
.

Proof.

By easy lattice calculations.
∎

Corollary 4.3.2
.

Suppose
\mathsf{Q}\phi
is a closed predicate, where

\mathsf{Q}\in\{{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a\mid a{\in}\mathsf{VarSymb}\}\cup\{\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt,\Box\hskip 0.0pt,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\},

and suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then:

1.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\mathsf{Q}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{Q}{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
.

2.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{Q}{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\mathsf{Q}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
for

\mathsf{Q}\in\{{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a\mid a{\in}\mathsf{VarSymb}\}\cup\{\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\}
.

3.

It need not be the case that
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{Q}{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\mathsf{Q}\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
for

\mathsf{Q}\in\{{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a\mid a{\in}\mathsf{VarSymb}\}\cup\{\Box\hskip 0.0pt,{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\}
.

Proof.

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
We freely use strong modus ponens (Proposition
2.3.3
(
2
)).

1.

We consider
\mathsf{Q}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
.
We use the clauses for

\mathsf{R}

and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
in Figure
6
, and then Lemma
4.3.1
(
1
):

\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}{<}n}\bigwedge_{O^{\prime}{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.quantifier.swap}(\ref{item.quantifier.swap.1})}}{{\leq}}\bigwedge_{O^{\prime}{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}{<}n}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}.

Now we consider
\mathsf{Q}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a
:

\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}{<}n}\bigwedge_{v{\in}\mathord{\text{Value}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O,\varsigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.quantifier.swap}(\ref{item.quantifier.swap.1})}}{{\leq}}\bigwedge_{v{\in}\mathord{\text{Value}}}\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}{<}n}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O,\varsigma}.

The other cases are precisely similar.

2.

We consider just
\mathsf{Q}=\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
; the other cases are precisely similar.
We just use Lemma
4.3.1
(
3
):

\bigvee_{p^{\prime}{\in}O}\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}{<}n}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p^{\prime},O,\varsigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L\ref{lemm.quantifier.swap}(\ref{item.quantifier.swap.2})}}{{=}}\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}{<}n}\bigvee_{p^{\prime}{\in}O}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p^{\prime},O,\varsigma}.

3.

We consider just
\mathsf{Q}={\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a
; the other cases are precisely similar.
We note by Lemma
4.3.1
(
2
) that

\bigwedge_{v{\in}\mathord{\text{Value}}}\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}_{v}{<}n}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime}_{v},p,O,\varsigma}\leq\bigvee_{0{\leq}n^{\prime}{<}n}\bigwedge_{v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O,\varsigma}\ \ \ \text{does not hold in general}.

∎

4.4
The compound modalities
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt

Remark 4.4.1
.

We will make much use of the modalities
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt
.

We call these four combinations
compound modalities
, because they are built out of
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
(which quantify over open sets) and
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
and
\Box\hskip 0.0pt
(which quantify over points in an open set).
In this Subsection we study some of their properties.

Lemma
4.4.2
just unpacks definitions, where
\between
is notation from
[
Gab25
,
Gab24
]
meaning ‘has nonempty intersection with’:

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then:

1.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
if and only if
\exists O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\between\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}
.

2.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
if and only if
\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\subseteq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}
.

3.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
if and only if
\exists O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\subseteq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}
.

4.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
if and only if
\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\between\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\exists O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.\exists p{\in}O.n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi&\text{Figure~\ref{fig.3.derived}}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\exists O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\between\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Fact}\end{array}

2.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.\forall p{\in}O.n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi&\text{Figure~\ref{fig.3.derived}}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\subseteq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Fact}\end{array}

3.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\exists O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.\forall p{\in}O.n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi&\text{Figure~\ref{fig.3.derived}}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\exists O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\subseteq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Fact}\end{array}

4.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[b]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.\exists p{\in}O.n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi&\text{Figure~\ref{fig.3.derived}}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\between\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Fact}\end{array}\qed

Proposition
4.4.3
packages up some simple corollaries of Lemma
4.4.2
, in forms that will be useful for our later proofs:

Proposition 4.4.3
.

Suppose
\phi
and
\psi
are closed predicates and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\psi\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
.

2.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\psi\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
.

3.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\psi)\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
, and

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\psi)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
.

4.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
.

5.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
.

Proof.

&

).

4.5
Some comments on time

Remark 4.5.1
.

This Subsection notes Lemmas
4.5.2
and
4.5.3
.
They both have to do with time, and underneath all their complicated-looking symbols they are both straightforward:

•

Lemma
4.5.2
reflects a fact that if something holds now, then in the future it will have held.

•

Lemma
4.5.3
reflects the fact that something repeats forever if and only if it happens infinitely often.

Lemma 4.5.2
.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}=(n,p,O,\varsigma)
is a context and
\phi
is a closed predicate.
Then:

1.

n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
implies
\forall n^{\prime}{\gneq}n.\,n^{\prime},p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi
.

2.

n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}\phi
implies
