\forall n^{\prime}{\geq}n.\,n^{\prime},p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}\phi
.

3.

As corollaries,

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi\quad\text{and}\quad\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}\phi.

Proof.

This is just from the clauses for

\mathsf{R}

and

\mathsf{R}

in Figure
6
.
The corollaries follow using strong modus ponens (Proposition
2.3.3
(
2
)).
∎

\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{infinitely}$}\hskip 0.0pt=\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt
has a characterisation as ‘infinitely often’, with the convenient feature that its denotation is independent of the stage at which it is evaluated (called
n^{\prime}
in the statement of the Lemma below):

Lemma 4.5.3
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Suppose further that
n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\mbox{$\mathit{tvs}$}\in\{{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{true}},{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}\}
.
Then we have

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{infinitely}$}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n^{\prime},p,O,\varsigma}\in\mbox{$\mathit{tvs}$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\in\mbox{$\mathit{tvs}$}\}\text{ is infinite}.

Proof.

By routine arguments from the clause for

\mathsf{infinitely}

in Figure
6
.
∎

4.6
Unique existence and affine existence in three-valued logic

4.6.1
The definition, and main result

Remark 4.6.1
.

Distributed algorithms often require an implementation to make (at most) one choice from (zero or more) possibilities.
In our axioms, we model this using unique existence and affine existence:

•

Unique existence
means ‘there exists precisely one’; we write this
\exists_{1}
, for uniformity with affine existence, defined in the next item.
(The standard symbol is
\exists!
.)
30
30

30

There is another reason to not write
\exists!
for unique existence: to avoid a mild notation clash with our forward rules, which will have names mentioning
!
(for ‘forward’) and
01
(for affine), and ‘Uniq’ (for uniqueness). In context, writing
\exists_{1}
and
\exists_{01}
seems the cleaner and more systematic option.

•

Affine existence
means ‘there exist zero or one’; we write this
\exists_{01}
.
(We are not aware of a standard symbol for affine existence.)

See for example
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose01)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite01)}}
in Figure
11
(a discussion of which is in Remark
6.5.1
(
5
)).

So we need to think about what
\exists_{1}
and
\exists_{01}
even mean in a three-valued setting.
This turns out to be not completely straightforward, because we have a third truth-value that is neither true nor false.

For concreteness, suppose
\phi
is a predicate with one free variable
a
, and suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context, and suppose that

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
for all
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
.
Then what truth-values should
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
and
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
have?

Now suppose
v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
, and suppose that
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime\prime\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{f}}
for all
v^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}\setminus\{v,v^{\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\}
and

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}\quad\land\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}\quad\land\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}.

Then what truth-values should
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
and
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
have?

Now suppose that
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime\prime\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{f}}
for all
v^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}\setminus\{v\}
and

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}.

Then what truth-values should
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
and
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
have?

The answer is not completely automatic: it is a real design decision that we have to make.

Definition 4.6.2
.

Suppose
\phi
is a predicate.
Define
affine existence

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
and
unique existence

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
by:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi=&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a,a^{\prime}.(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}a^{\prime}])\mathrel{\to}a{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}a^{\prime}\\
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi=&({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi)\end{array}

Above,
a^{\prime}\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
is a variable symbol chosen fresh for
\phi
, and
[a{{:}\text{=}}a^{\prime}]
denotes the replacement of every instance of
a\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
with
a^{\prime}
.
31
31

31

Secretly,
[a{{:}\text{=}}a^{\prime}]
is a nominal swapping permutation
(a^{\prime}\ a)

[
GP02
]
.

Remark 4.6.3
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate (i.e.
a\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
and
\phi
is a predicate with free variable symbols at most
\{a\}
), and suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
We unpack the denotations of

\exists_{1}

and

\exists_{01}

from Definition
4.6.2
:

1.

If
\exists v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.v\neq v^{\prime}\land{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
, then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{f}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.

2.

If
\exists_{1}v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
and
\forall v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
, then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.

3.

If
\exists_{1}v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
and
\exists v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
, then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.

4.

If
\neg\exists v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
and
\exists_{1}v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
, then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}\quad\text{and}\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}.

5.

If
\neg\exists v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
and
\exists v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.v\neq v^{\prime}\land{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
, then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.

6.

If
\forall v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{f}}
, then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}\quad\text{and}\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{f}}.

Remark 4.6.4
.

We make two elementary observations on Remark
4.6.3
:

1.

The equality
=
in our logic is two-valued:
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
, and never
{\mathbf{b}}
.
32
32

32

In the terminology of Definition
4.8.2
(
1
) below, equality predicates are always
correct
.

2.

Two notions of ‘unique existence’ are in play in this discussion:

•

‘
\exists_{1}v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
’ refers to the actual unique existence quantifier in the real world (whatever that is).
This computes actual truth-values ‘true’ and ‘false’ and returns true when there actually exists a unique value.

•

‘
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a
’ is part of the (derived) syntax of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
, and is defined in Definition
4.6.2
.
Its denotation computes elements of
{\mathbf{3}}=\{{\mathbf{f}},{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{t}}\}
.
As is standard (though confusingly) we
also
call elements of
{\mathbf{3}}
truth-values; but they are truth-values of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
, rather than actual truth-values in the real world (whatever that is).

So:
\exists_{1}
and

\exists_{1}

are both unique existence operators, but they live at different levels and they are not the same thing.
33
33

33

This kind of thing is far from unusual. A book on formal logic might use a notion of implication and existence in its proofs, which is related to but distinct from implication and existential predicate-formers in the formal logic under consideration. Nor is this limited to logic: a programming language may have multiple versions of the number
1
(8-bit, floating point, 64-bit, and infinite precision) all of which are called ‘one’, but all of which are different from each other, and also different from the actual
1\in\mathbb{N}
(whatever that is).

We will use unique/affine existence in our proofs via Lemma
4.6.5
:

Lemma 4.6.5
.

Suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate and
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
are values.
Then:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
if and only if

\exists_{01}v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
.

2.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
if and only if

\exists v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}\land\exists_{01}v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
.

3.

If
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
and
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
and
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}]
, then
v=v^{\prime}
.

4.

If
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
and
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
and
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}]
, then
v=v^{\prime}
.

Proof.

We unpack Definition
3.6.1
(
1
) and check the possibilities in Remark
4.6.3
.
∎

4.6.2
Comments on an edge case

Lemma
4.6.6
notes a (perhaps surprising, at first glance) fact about our denotation, that
if a unary predicate symbol
\mathsf{P}
takes truth-value
{\mathbf{b}}
when applied to all values (as would model a participant that has entirely crashed), then
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
are valid — even though
\mathsf{P}(v)
is valid on every value.
This reflects the common side-condition in the literature ‘if the participant is correct, then
stuff
’, which is trivially true if the participant is
not
correct:

Lemma 4.6.6
.

Suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context and
\mathsf{P}
is a unary predicate symbol.
34
34

34

There is nothing mathematically special about using a
unary
predicate symbol here, but in practice this is the case that we will use.

Then

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}\Longrightarrow(\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}\land\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}).

The above statement uses Notation
3.7.2
.
Unpacking this, we obtain:

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(a)\Longrightarrow(\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{P}(a)\land\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{P}(a)).

Proof.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}
.
Unpacking Definition
3.6.1
(
1
) this means
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{valid}}
, and by Figure
6
it follows that
\forall v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{P}(v)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
.

By
Remark
4.6.3
(
4
) (if
\mathord{\text{Value}}=\{v\}
for some
v
)
or Remark
4.6.3
(
5
) (if there exist
v\neq v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
)
it follows that

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
and
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
, or

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
,
respectively.

Both
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{b}}
are
valid
by Definition
3.6.1
(
1
), and thus
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
and
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
as required.
∎

4.7
Pointwise predicates (a well-behavedness property)

For this subsection we fix a signature
\Sigma
and a model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}},\mathord{\text{Value}})
.

4.7.1
The definition, and basic properties

Remark 4.7.1
.

Being pointwise
is a well-behavedness property on predicates, that their denotation in a context
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}=(n,p,O,\varsigma)
does not depend on the open set parameter
O
of the context.

Many important syntactic classes of predicates are naturally pointwise, including any predicate of the form
\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
(Lemma
4.7.7
).

Being pointwise is desirable for us because it simplifies denotation to holding ‘at a point’;
35
35

35

Actually: at a stage, point, and valuation, but intuitively we tend to view the stage and valuation as fixed, and the point
p
and open set
O
as varying.
and also because several nice properties depend on this (see for example Proposition
4.7.14
); and finally also because there is an easy syntactic condition (given in Lemma
4.7.7
(
2
)) that guarantees that a predicate is pointwise.

Definition 4.7.2

(Pointwise predicates)
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate.

1.

If
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma\in\mathord{\text{Valuation}}(\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
is a valuation then call
\phi

pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
when

\forall p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\forall O,O^{\prime}{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}.

In words,
\phi
is pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
when at every point
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
, the open set parameter
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
does not affect the value of the denotation
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}
.

2.

Call
\phi

absolutely pointwise
when it is pointwise for every
n
and
\varsigma
.

Definition 4.7.3
.

Suppose
\phi
is closed, and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then define
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,\varsigma}
by

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,\varsigma}=\bigwedge_{O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}.

In a manner of speaking we have already seen
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,\varsigma}
from Definition
4.7.3
in our logic:

Lemma 4.7.4
.

Suppose
\phi
is closed and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,\varsigma}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}.

Proof.

Direct from the clause for
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
from Figure
6
.
∎

Lemma 4.7.5
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then
\phi
is pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
in the sense of Definition
4.7.2

if and only if

\forall p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,\varsigma}.

Proof.

By a routine calculation from Definitions
4.7.2
and
4.7.3
.
∎

Corollary 4.7.6
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Suppose
\phi
is pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
.
Then

\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\Longleftrightarrow n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi.

Proof.

Routine from Definition
3.6.1
and Lemma
4.7.5
. ∎

Being pointwise is particularly nice because of two simple syntactic criteria that guarantee it:

Lemma 4.7.7

(Conditions for being pointwise)
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate.
Then:

1.

If
\phi
is atomic (Notation
3.2.2
(
3
); having the form
\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
or
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
) then it is absolutely pointwise (Definition
4.7.2
).

2.

If
\phi
does not mention
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
or
\Box\hskip 0.0pt
or
\varkappa
or
\mu
except within the scope of a
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
or
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
modality, then
\phi
is absolutely pointwise.
36
36

36

In particular,
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt
and
\Box\hskip 0.0pt
are within the scope of
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
in the compound modalities
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt
.

Proof.

For part
1
, we just unpack the definition of
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
in Figure
5
and note that the ‘
\varsigma(\mathsf{P})_{n,p}
’ used in the clause for a predicate symbol
\mathsf{P}
depends just on the values of
n
,
p
, and
\varsigma
, but not on the open sets
O
and
O^{\prime}
.

Part
2
follows by a routine induction on predicate syntax, using part
1
as the base case.
37
37

37

This result could be generalised to permit
\mu
not in the scope of
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
or
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
, subject to suitable positivity conditions (Definition
3.5.5
). But we will not need the generality so we do not bother.

∎

4.7.2
Pointwise, as a modality in our logic

In fact, we can express the property of being pointwise within our logic:

Definition 4.7.8
.

Suppose
\phi
is a predicate.
Then define a predicate
\mathsf{pointwise}[\phi]
by

\mathsf{pointwise}[\phi]\ =\ {\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi).

Lemma 4.7.9
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then

n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\bigvee_{O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}=\bigwedge_{O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}.

(The predicate
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
itself satisfies the conditions for being pointwise from Lemma
4.7.7
(
2
), so we can sensibly write
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
above.)

Proof.

From Lemma
4.1.2
(
1
) and the clauses for
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
in Figure
5
, and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt
from Figure
6
(or Lemma
4.7.4
).
∎

Lemma 4.7.10
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then
\phi
is pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
if and only if
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{pointwise}[\phi]
.

Proof.

By routine calculations using Lemma
4.7.9
, and the clause for
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
in Figure
6
, noting the elementary fact that for every
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
,

\bigwedge_{O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\leq\bigvee_{O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O^{\prime},\varsigma}.

∎

4.7.3
An interlude: dense sets, and sets with a nonempty open interior

We recall a pair of standard topological definitions which will turn up repeatedly later, most notably in the context of pointwise predicates as in Proposition
4.7.14
immediately below, and again later in Lemma
5.2.1
:

Definition 4.7.11

(Dense subset)
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and suppose
P\subseteq\mathord{\text{Point}}
is a (not necessarily open) set of points.

1.

Say that
P
is
dense
when
\forall O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\between P
.

2.

Say that
P

has a nonempty open interior
when
\exists O{\in}\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\subseteq P
.

3.

Write
\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(P)
when
P
is dense, and
\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P)
when
P
has a nonempty open interior.

Lemma 4.7.12
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and
P,P^{\prime}\subseteq\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
Then:

1.

If
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
then
\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(P)\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(P^{\prime})
(being dense is
up-closed
).

2.

If
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
then
\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P)\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P^{\prime})
(having a nonempty open interior is
up-closed
).

3.

If
P\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
then
\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P)
(a nonempty open set has a nonempty open interior).

Proof.

By routine set calculations from Definition
4.7.11
.
∎

Lemma 4.7.13
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and
P\subseteq\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
Then

\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(P)\Longleftrightarrow\neg\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus P)\quad\text{and}\quad\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P)\Longleftrightarrow\neg\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus P).

In this sense,
\mathit{noi}
and
\mathit{dense}
form a
de Morgan dual pair
of operators — just as
\forall=\neg\exists\neg
(as noted in Figure
9
) and
\exists={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\forall{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
.

Proof.

We sketch the (easy) argument:

•

Suppose
P
is not dense; by Definition
4.7.11
this means that there exists
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
such that
P\cap O=\varnothing
, thus
O\subseteq\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus P
, thus
\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus P
has a nonempty open interior.

•

Suppose
\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus P
has a nonempty open interior
\varnothing\neq O\subseteq\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus P
.
Then clearly
P
is not dense.
∎

More on how ‘being dense’ works in semitopologies is in
[
Gab24
, Chapter 11]
.
The notion behaves somewhat differently than it does in topologies, but not in ways that will affect us here.

4.7.4
Application to pointwise predicates

Technically, Proposition
4.7.14
is a corollary of Lemma
4.4.2
and Corollary
4.7.6
.
The result links the four logical modalities
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt
to elementary topological properties of the sets of points at which these modalities are valid — being nonempty, being full, having a nonempty open interior, and being dense.
We will use Proposition
4.7.14
, both directly and via other lemmas that depend on it.

The most important use-case of Proposition
4.7.14
will be when
\phi
is atomic (Notation
3.2.2
(
3
)) — having the form

\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\quad\text{or}\quad{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{P}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})

— since then by Lemma
4.7.7

\phi
is automatically pointwise:

Proposition 4.7.14
.

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation and
\phi
is a closed predicate that is pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
(Definition
4.7.2
).
Then:

1.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
if and only if
\varnothing\neq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}
.

2.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
if and only if
\mathord{\text{Point}}=\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}
.

3.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
if and only if
\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\})
.

4.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
if and only if
\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\})
.

Recall that:

•

\mathit{dense}
and
\mathit{noi}
are from Definition
4.7.11
.

•

The notations
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
and
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
are from Definition
3.6.1
.

Proof.

If
\phi
is pointwise then by Corollary
4.7.6

n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
if and only if
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi
, for any
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
, and we just simplify parts
1
to
4
of Lemma
4.4.2
:

1.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\exists O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\between\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Lemma~\ref{lemm.dense.char}(\ref{item.dense.char.someoneAll})}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\mathord{\text{Point}}\between\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Fact}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}\neq\varnothing&\text{Fact}\end{array}

2.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\subseteq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Lemma~\ref{lemm.dense.char}(\ref{item.dense.char.everyoneAll})}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\mathord{\text{Point}}\subseteq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Fact}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}=\mathord{\text{Point}}&\text{Fact}\end{array}

3.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\exists O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\subseteq\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Lemma~\ref{lemm.dense.char}(\ref{item.dense.char.QuorumBox})}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\})&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.dense.subset}}\end{array}

4.

We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[b]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O\between\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}&\text{Lemma~\ref{lemm.dense.char}(\ref{item.dense.char.CoquorumDiamond})}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\})&\text{Definition~\ref{defn.dense.subset}}\end{array}\qed

See also Lemma
5.3.1
, which instantiates Proposition
4.7.14
(
3
&
4
) to a special case
familiar from the literature on distributed systems.

4.8
Correct predicates (another well-behavedness property)

Remark 4.8.1
.

We defined
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
in Definition
2.2.1
(
1
) and a corresponding modality
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
in Figure
1
(which returns
{\mathbf{t}}
when its argument is in
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
otherwise), and we studied some of their properties, most notably in Lemma
2.2.8
.

We now extend this to notions of
correctness
on predicates
\phi
and predicate symbols
\mathsf{P}
.

Correctness turns out to be highly relevant to how we will use our logic.
Partly this is because when a predicate is correct, we are returned to the familiar two-valued world of Boolean logic — but more subtly, correctness is interesting because of how this concept interacts with the predicate and modal parts of our logic; in certain circumstances we can use a combination of correctness
somewhere
and validity
everywhere
to deduce information about truth (see Remark
4.8.10
) and this turns out to be a good logical model for how reasoning on distributed algorithms is often done in practice.

We start with the definition and some basic lemmas, and develop from there:

Definition 4.8.2

(Correctness)
.

Suppose
\phi
is a predicate and
a\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
is a variable symbol.
Then:

1.

Consistent with the definition of
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
from Definition
2.2.1
(
1
),
we define
\mathsf{correct}[\phi]
, read
\phi
is correct
, by

\mathsf{correct}[\phi]=\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi.

Note that
\phi
being correct does not mean that
\phi
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
; it means that
\phi
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
and in particular it does
not
return
{\mathbf{b}}
.

2.

Suppose
\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
is a unary predicate symbol.
Define a predicate
\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]
by

\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]\ =\ {\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(a)]\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\ref{nttn.hos}}}{{=}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}.

3.

If
\mathsf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{P}_{n}
are unary predicate symbols then we may write

\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{P}_{n}]\quad\text{for}\quad\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}_{1}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\dots\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}_{n}].

(See for example axiom
{\mathsf{(LdrCorrect)}}
in Figure
11
.)

4.

Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation, and suppose
\mathsf{P}
is a unary predicate symbol.
Then when
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]
we may call
p

\mathsf{P}
-correct
.

Lemma 4.8.3
.

Suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context and
\phi
is a closed predicate.
Then

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\quad\text{if and only if}\quad{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}.

Proof.

This just re-states Lemma
2.2.7
(
5
).
∎

Remark 4.8.4
.

Definition
4.8.2
(
1
) gives us two synonyms for the modality expressing that the truth-value returned by a predicate
\phi
is either
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
(not
{\mathbf{b}}
):
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
and
\mathsf{correct}
.

Although these are synonymous, there is a difference in emphasis:

•

\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
is a logical modality and just means ‘true or false’.

•

The literature on distributed systems uses a term ‘correctness’, which indicates that a process succeeds and returns a correct value.
In our logical model this corresponds to returning
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
— and thus not returning
{\mathbf{b}}
.
So when we write
\mathsf{correct}
, the emphasis is on ‘not
{\mathbf{b}}
’ with emphasis on a process ‘not failing’.

We use whichever notation seems most natural in context.
For example, in Figure
11
:

1.

In
{\mathsf{(LdrCorrect)}}
we write
\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{propose},\mathsf{write},\mathsf{decide}]
because intuitively we are asserting that at a particular time the leader
does not crash
when proposing, writing, or deciding.

2.

In
{\mathsf{(LdrExist)}}
we write
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{leader}
, because
\mathsf{leader}
is a predicate that identifies whether a participant is the leader at a particular time, and this is either true or false.
38
38

38

In practice determining who the leader is requires a participant to compute a function, but the computation is not distributed, in the sense that prior agreement amongst participants is assumed on what the function is and what seed data it uses, and each participant computes it locally to get (the same) definitive yes/no answer.

If the reader prefers, they can safely read
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
every time they see
\mathsf{correct}
, and they will be precisely correct (pun intended).

Many predicates of interest are correct, including correctness itself, our truth-modalities, and equality and equivalence:

Lemma 4.8.5
.

Suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context and
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are closed predicates and
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
.
Then:

1.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{correct}[\phi]]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
.
We can (perhaps cheekily) read this as ‘correctness is correct’.

2.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{Q}\phi]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
for
\mathsf{Q}\in\{\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt,\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt,\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt,\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt,\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt,\mathsf{F}\hskip-1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\}
.

3.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v^{\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
.
In words: equality is correct.

4.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\phi^{\prime}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{t}}
.
In words: equivalence is correct.

Proof.

By routine checking from Figures
1
,
5
,
6
, and (for equivalence
\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}
)
2
.
∎

Lemma
4.8.6
and Corollary
4.8.7
are straightforward, but we spell it out:

Lemma 4.8.6
.

Suppose
\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
is a unary predicate symbol and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then

1.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}=\bigwedge_{v{\in}\mathord{\text{Value}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(v)]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}

and

2.

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(v)]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
for every value
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
.

Proof.

By Definition
4.8.2
(
2
) and Figure
6

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(a)]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}=\bigwedge_{v{\in}\mathord{\text{Value}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(v)]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\leq{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(v)]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}.\qed

Corollary 4.8.7
.

Suppose
\mathit{ctx}
is a context and
\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
is a unary predicate symbol and
a\in\mathsf{VarSymb}
is a variable symbol.
Then

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]\quad\text{implies}\quad\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}]\ \land\ \mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}].

Proof.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]
.
By Lemma
4.8.6
we see that
\forall v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(v)]
.
By Lemma
4.8.3

\forall v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{P}(v)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\in{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}
.
Now the clauses for

\exists

in Figure
5
and

\forall

in Figure
6
take a supremum and infimum respectively of
\{{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{P}(v)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}\mid v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}\}\subseteq{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
, and this supremum or infimum is also clearly in
{\mathbf{3}}_{\scalebox{0.6}{correct}}=\{{\mathbf{t}},{\mathbf{f}}\}
.
We just use Lemma
4.8.3
.
∎

Proposition
4.8.8
(
1
) is a wrapper for Lemma
2.2.8
.
This deceptively simple equivalence turns out to be a key way that correctness can be usefully applied:

Proposition 4.8.8
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
\mathsf{P}
is a unary predicate symbol and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\phi]
if and only if
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

In words:
\phi
is correct precisely when ‘
\phi
is valid’ is equivalent to ‘
\phi
is true’.

2.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]
if and only if
\forall v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.(\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{P}(v)\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(v))
.

3.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]
implies
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
and
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\phi]
.
By Lemma
2.2.8

\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
.
By Lemma
4.1.2
(
1
) and the clause for
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
in Figure
5
,
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.
The reverse implication just reverses the same reasoning.

2.

We combine Lemma
4.8.6
with part
1
of this result for
\phi=\mathsf{P}(v)
for every
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
.

3.

From Corollary
4.8.7
and Proposition
4.8.8
(
1
).
∎

Proposition
4.8.9
explores how correctness interacts with our compound modalities from Subsection
4.4
.

Proposition 4.8.9
.

Suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate and
\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
is a unary predicate symbol.
Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then:

1.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
implies
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.
39
39

39

Alert: note that we write
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
in the right-hand conjunct, not
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

2.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
implies

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{P}
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
.
By Proposition
4.4.3
(
2
)

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi)
.
By Proposition
4.8.8
(
1
) and Lemma
4.2.2
(for
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
)
we have
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
as required.

2.

We combine part
1
of this result with Corollary
4.8.7
.
∎

Remark 4.8.10

(Why this is interesting)
.

The statement of Proposition
4.8.9
is quite heavy on the symbols.
For clarity, let us re-read it, but let us replace
\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}
with the phrase ‘mostly correct’, and
\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
with the phrase ‘everywhere valid’, and

\exists

with the phrase ‘for some value’.
Then we get:

1.

If
\phi
is
mostly correct
and
everywhere valid
, then it is
mostly true
.

2.

If
\mathsf{P}
is
mostly correct
and
everywhere valid
for some value, then it is
mostly true
for some value.

So what we see is that we can promote weak statements about validity everywhere, with stronger but more limited statements about correctness, to obtain limited statements about truth.

The reader familiar with how distributed algorithms work may recognise this style of reasoning.
So: here it is beginning to appear in our logic.

4.9
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
: a most recent
v
that makes
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
true

Remark 4.9.1
.

We see from Figure
6
and Remark
3.5.1
(
6
) that
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
looks back into the past, searching for a most recent time when
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}={\mathbf{t}}
, and checks if at that most recent time,
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}={\mathbf{t}}
.

\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
if this search succeeds, and
{\mathbf{f}}
if this fails.
It never returns
{\mathbf{b}}
.

In words:
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
checks whether
v
is a value that has most recently made
\phi
true.

We need
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
for our axiomatisation of Paxos in Figure
11
, where

•

in
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
we write
\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v
(short for
(\mathsf{accept}(a))[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
, as per Figure
8
), and

•

in
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}
we write
(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept})\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v
(short for
(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}(a))[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
).

We read
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
as

v
is a value that most recently makes
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
true
.

In the common case that
\phi=\mathsf{P}(a)
for some unary predicate symbol
\mathsf{P}
, then we might just say

v
is a value that most recently makes
\mathsf{P}
true
.

In this Subsection we study the properties of this construct in more detail.

Lemma 4.9.2
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate and
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{correct}[\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]]
.

2.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

By Definition
4.8.2
(
1
)
\mathsf{correct}[\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]]=\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
, and this means that
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
always returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
(but not
{\mathbf{b}}
).
We just check the relevant clause in Figure
6
and see that this is so.

2.

We just use part 1 of this result and Proposition
4.8.8
(
1
).
∎

Remark 4.9.3
.

1.

Lemma
4.9.2
(2) notes that
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
and
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])
are extensionally equivalent (Definition
4.1.1
).

We may write one or the other, depending on what seems most clear in a proof.
In particular, if we are using strong modus ponens (Proposition
2.3.3
(
2
)) then we might retain a
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt
modality for clarity.

2.

We try to head off an easy error: note that
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
is not equivalent to
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
, nor the (by Lemma
4.2.2
) equivalent form
{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
.

•

\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
when
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
has returned
{\mathbf{t}}
at some stage in the past.

•

\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
(‘
v
is a value that most recently makes
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
true’) returns
{\mathbf{t}}
when
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
has returned
{\mathbf{t}}
in
the most recent stage
such that
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
returned
{\mathbf{t}}
(i.e. such that
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}]
returned
{\mathbf{t}}
for
some

v^{\prime}
).

These are not the same thing.

Remark
4.9.3
notes that
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
is different from
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
.
That said, there is a close link between them, which will turn out to be important for understanding our axiomatisation of Paxos (see Lemma
6.6.2
and surrounding discussion):

Lemma 4.9.4
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate and
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}=(n,p,O,\varsigma)
is a context.
Then

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi.

Proof.

First, note from Lemma
4.2.2
that
\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.
And, note that
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
denote the same syntax, as per Remark
3.2.8
(
3
).

We unpack the meaning of
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
in Figure
6
and note that it returns
{\mathbf{t}}
when there exist
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
0\leq n^{\prime}<n
such that
n^{\prime},p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]
, and
{\mathbf{f}}
otherwise.
But this is also what
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v])]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}
does, so by Lemma
4.1.2
(
1
) we are done.
∎

Lemma
4.9.5
connects
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
to the fact that our notion of time is discrete and so has a well-defined notion of a ‘tomorrow’ (i.e. a ‘next timestep’):

Lemma 4.9.5
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate and
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}(\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}(({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])).

Proof.

It is a fact of Figure
6
that
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{yesterday}$}\hskip 0.0pt\psi\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\psi
for any closed
\psi
.
The result follows from this observation and from the clause for
\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]
in Figure
6
.
∎

Corollary 4.9.6
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate and
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])
.

2.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{tomorrow}$}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])
.

Proof.

Elementary from Lemma
4.9.5
using strong modus ponens (Proposition
2.3.3
(
2
)).
∎

Lemma 4.9.7
.

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\phi
is a closed predicate and
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
\mathit{ctx}
is a context.
Then:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash(\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v^{\prime}])\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}(\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}])
.

2.

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash(\phi[a\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v])\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}(\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v])
.

If
\phi=\mathsf{Q}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(a)
for a unary predicate symbol
\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
and some prefix of modalities
\mathsf{Q}\hskip 0.0pt
, then we can elide the variable symbol
a
as per Notation
3.7.2
and Figure
8
, and this becomes

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash(\mathsf{Q}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P})\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}\mathsf{Q}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(v).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Unpacking the clause for
