\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}
in Figure
6
, if
v
and
v^{\prime}
are values that most recently make
\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}\text{-}]
be
{\mathbf{t}}
, then certainly
{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}(\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v^{\prime}])
.

2.

Follows from part
1
as the special case that
v^{\prime}=v
.
∎

5
n-twined semitopologies

5.1
Antiseparation properties

We are particularly interested in models over semitopologies whose open sets have certain
antiseparation properties
, by which we mean properties that involve the existence of nonempty intersections between open sets.
40
40

40

For comparison, topology is often concerned with
separation
properties, like Hausdorff separation, which have to do with existence of
empty
intersections between open sets. In this sense, the interest in this Section is the precise dual to where the emphasis traditionally lies in topology.

The design space of antiseparation properties is rich, as the first author explored in
[
Gab24
]
— but for our needs in this paper we will consider just one canonical property which we will call being
n
-twined
.
We define the
n
-twined property in Definition
5.1.1
, we explain why it matters in Remark
5.1.3
, and then we study its properties.

Definition 5.1.1
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
.
Then call
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})

n
-twined
when every collection of
n
nonempty open sets has a nonempty intersection.
In symbols:

\forall O_{1},\dots,O_{n}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.O_{1}\cap\dots\cap O_{n}\neq\varnothing.

Lemma 5.1.2
.

We note two edge cases:

1.

A semitopology is
0
-twined if and only if it is nonempty (Notation
3.1.3
).

2.

Every semitopology is
1
-twined.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

If
n=0
then we need to show that
\mathord{\text{Point}}
(the intersection of zero nonempty sets) is not equal to
\varnothing
.
By Notation
3.1.3
this is true precisely when the semitopology is nonempty.

2.

By Definition
5.1.1
, we must show that
O\neq\varnothing
for every
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
.
This is true.
∎

v
must equal
v^{\prime}
“Output is
v
”
“Output is
v^{\prime}
”
Byzantine / broken
“Output is
v
”
“Output is
v^{\prime}
”
v
must equal
v^{\prime}

Figure 10
:
Compatibility in 2- and 3-twined semitopologies (Remark
5.1.3
)

Remark 5.1.3

(Discussion of the special cases
n=2
and
n=3
)
.

Lemma
5.1.2
shows that being
0
-twined and
1
-twined are straightforward properties.
In contrast, the
2
- and
3
-twined semitopologies will be very interesting to us in this paper.
We give a high-level discussion of why this is:

•

Suppose we are in a distributed system running on a
2
-twined semitopology.
We assume that all participants are honest.
Suppose further that we have

–

a nonempty open set of participants
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
who agree that some predicate
\phi
is true (respectively: valid), and

–

a nonempty open set of participants
O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
who agree that some predicate
\phi^{\prime}
is true (respectively: valid).

Then because the system is
2
-twined, there exists some
p\in O\cap O^{\prime}
who agrees that both
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are true (respectively: valid).
41
41

41

We will render this in our logic later on, in Proposition
5.3.2
(
1b
).

This simple observation underlies guarantees that participants generate compatible outputs (for some protocol-dependent notion of compatibility) in many distributed algorithms, because it guarantees that
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are not logically conflicting — e.g. that we do not have something like
\phi^{\prime}=\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
— because if they were, then an honest
p
could not believe both.

In the special case that
\phi
is ‘the output of our consensus algorithm is
v
’ and
\phi^{\prime}
is ‘the output of our consensus algorithm is
v^{\prime}
’, the
2
-twined property makes the strong compatibility guarantee that where consensus algorithms succeed (i.e. where participants do not crash), the output is compatible in the sense that two participants will agree on the output of the consensus algorithm (assuming outputs are supposed to be unique).

This is illustrated in the left-hand diagram in Figure
10
.

•

Suppose we are in a distributed system running on a
3
-twined semitopology.
Some participants may be dishonest (also called Byzantine or broken), and they might not follow the protocol.

Suppose further that we have nonempty open sets
O,O^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
of participants who agree on the truth (respectively: validity) of predicates
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
respectively.
Suppose further that we have a nonempty open set
O^{\prime\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
of participants who are honest (meaning that we bound dishonest participants to at most some
closed
set).
Because the system is
3
-twined, there exists some
p\in O\cap O^{\prime}\cap O^{\prime\prime}
— this
p
is a participant who agrees on the truth (respectively: validity) of
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
and is also honest.
42
42

42

We will render this in our logic twice: in Proposition
5.3.2
(
2b
) as a general result, and then as a useful special case in Corollary
5.3.3
(
1a
).

In this case,
\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi^{\prime}
must be consistent, because if it were not then the honest participant
p
could not believe both
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
.
Such a situation is illustrated in the right-hand diagram in Figure
10
.

Just as for the
2
-twined case, this simple argument underlies many correctness arguments in distributed systems in the presence of dishonest participants.

There is no barrier to considering
n
-twined semitopologies for
n\geq 4
, and this would just give us stronger guarantees of intersection.

A brief survey of intersection properties in the literature is in Remark
5.2.4
.

5.2
Being
n
-twined and being dense / having nonempty open interior

The property of being
n
-twined is closely related to the topological notion of being a
dense set
and having a
nonempty open interior
(Definition
4.7.11
).
We explore this in Lemma
5.2.1
and Proposition
5.2.2
; then in Proposition
5.2.3
we specialise the discussion to the important special case of
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
.

Lemma 5.2.1
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
.
Then:

1.

(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
n
-twined if and only if every collection of
n{\shortminus}1
nonempty open sets has a dense intersection:

\forall O_{1},\dots O_{n{\shortminus}1}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(O_{1}\cap\dots\cap O_{n{\shortminus}1}).

2.

As a corollary,
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
2
-twined if and only if every nonempty open set is dense:

\forall O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(O).

Proof.

Both parts follow from Definitions
4.7.11
(
1
) and
5.1.1
, just by pushing around definitions.

One corner case merits brief mention: if
n=1
then we must show that every collection of
0
sets (i.e.
\varnothing
) is dense.
So, we need to show that
\bigcap\varnothing
is dense.
By convention
\bigcap\varnothing=\mathord{\text{Point}}
, and this is indeed dense, so we are done.
∎

Proposition 5.2.2
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

1.

(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
n
-twined.

2.

\forall P_{1},\dots,P_{n{\shortminus}1}\subseteq\mathord{\text{Point}}.(\bigwedge_{1{\leq}i{\leq}n{\shortminus}1}\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P_{i}))\Longrightarrow\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(\bigcap_{1{\leq}i{\leq}n{\shortminus}1}P_{i})
.

Proof.

Part
2
trivially implies part
1
because we can take
P_{1},\dots,P_{n{\shortminus}1}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
, so that by Definition
4.7.11

\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n{\shortminus}1
holds by construction by Lemma
4.7.12
(
3
), and we use Lemma
5.2.1
(
1
).

Showing that part
1
implies part
2
is only slightly less trivial.
Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
n
-twined and
P_{1},\dots,P_{n{\shortminus}1}\subseteq\mathord{\text{Point}}
and
\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq n{\shortminus}1
, meaning by Definition
4.7.11
that for each such
i
there exists an
O_{i}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
such that
O_{i}\subseteq P_{i}
.
By Lemma
5.2.1
(
1
)
O_{1}\cap\dots\cap O_{n{\shortminus}1}
is dense, and by Lemma
4.7.12
(
1
) so is
P_{1}\cap\dots\cap P_{n{\shortminus}1}
.
∎

Proposition
5.2.3
provides the canonical example of
n
-twined semitopologies.
It mildly generalises known properties in the literature; the case
n=2
in Proposition
5.2.3
(
1
) corresponds to the
Q^{2}
property from
[
DDFN07
, Theorem 1]
, and the case
n=3
corresponds to the
Q^{3}
property of
[
ACTZ24
, Definition 2]
.
The topology-flavoured presentations in Proposition
5.2.3
(
2
&
3
) are new.

Proposition 5.2.3
.

Suppose
0\leq f<N\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
P\subseteq\{0\dots N{\shortminus}1\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
.
Then:

1.

N>n*f
if and only if
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
is
n
-twined.

2.

\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P)
(and equivalently
P\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
) if and only if
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}P\geq N{\shortminus}f
.
43
43

43

In general semitopologies,
\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(P)
(
P
having a nonempty open interior) is not the same as
P\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
(
P
being a nonempty open set); but in the case of
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
, these two properties happen to be equal.

3.

\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(P)
if and only if
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}P\geq f{+}1
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

Suppose
N>n*f
and suppose
O_{1},\dots,O_{n}\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
are nonempty open sets in
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
.
From Definition
3.1.4
this means that they each contain all but
f
elements of
\{0\dots N{\shortminus}1\}
.
It follows that
O_{1}\cap\dots\cap O_{n}
contains (at least) all but
n*f
elements of
\{0\dots N{\shortminus}1\}
.
Since
N\gneq n*f
, this intersection is nonempty.

Conversely, if
N\leq n*f
then it is routine to partition
\{0\dots N{\shortminus}1\}
into
n
sets, each of which has at most
f
elements.
Then their complements are a set of
n
open sets whose intersection is empty.

2.

By routine counting arguments.

3.

By routine counting arguments.
∎

Remark 5.2.4
.

Being
2
-twined corresponds to the
quorum intersection property
of
[
Lam98
, Section 2.1, Condition B2]
, which requires that any two quorums intersect.
Being
3
-twined also corresponds to a quorum intersection property with a stronger requirement that (assuming there is a quorum of honest participants) any two quorums must intersect in a participant that is honest
[
MR98
, Definition 5.1]
.

These intersection properties are closely tied to necessary conditions for some consensus protocols (and other distributed algorithms) to function correctly.
Specifically for the case of the semitopology
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
from Definition
3.1.4
(
2
), requiring nonempty quorum intersection is equivalent to the condition
N>2f
, where
f
denotes the number of processes that may crash during the protocol’s execution.
This condition is often referred to as
Q^{2}

[
DDFN07
, Theorem 1]
.
Similarly, being
3
-twined corresponds to the condition
N>3f
, commonly referred to as
Q^{3}

[
ACTZ24
, Definition 2]
.
A more generalised version of
Q^{3}
exists, known as
B^{3}

[
DDFN07
, Theorem 2]
[
ACTZ24
, Definition 5]
, which accounts for greater heterogeneity.
Specifically,
Q^{3}
applies to distributed systems where failures are homogeneous, meaning they are globally known and agreed upon by all participants.
For example, all participants understand that up to
f
processes may fail.

In contrast, in a heterogeneous setting, participants may have differing beliefs about
who
might fail, leading to a more nuanced description of trust assumptions.
In this context,
B^{3}
generalises
Q^{3}
by incorporating these variations in trust and failure assumptions.
Readers interested in exploring this topic further can consult
[
DDFN07
,
ACTZ24
]
.

5.3
The canonical properties

For this subsection we fix a signature
\Sigma
and a model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}},\mathord{\text{Value}})
.

We now prove results to express how the underlying semitopology of this model
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
being
2
-twined or
3
-twined manifests itself in the logic, in ways that will be relevant in our later proofs.
In Subsection
5.3.1
we concretely unpack what some relevant modalities mean, then in Subsection
5.3.2
we prove the promised results about them.

5.3.1
Unpacking some modalities

Lemma
5.3.1
unpacks concretely what
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
— which are the subject of many results including Propositions
5.3.2
and
5.4.2
— mean in the semitopology
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
from Definition
3.1.4
(
2
), which has
N
points, and has open sets being either the empty set, or any set with at least
N{\shortminus}f
elements:

1.

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
means ‘for at least
N{\shortminus}f
elements’; and

2.

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
means ‘for at least
f{+}1
elements’.

This special case is particularly relevant because (as the reader may know)
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
is commonly used in the literature on distributed algorithms.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

The proof is elementary but we spell it out.
We reason as follows:

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\})&\text{Proposition~\ref{prop.pointwise.dense.char}(\ref{item.dense.char.pointwise.QuorumBox})}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\}\geq N{\shortminus}f&\text{Proposition~\ref{prop.allbut.supertwined}(\ref{item.allbut.NOI.dense})}\end{array}

2.

Much as the previous part, but using Proposition
4.7.14
(
4
).

3.

By Lemma
4.2.2
,
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
if and only if
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.
We just use part 1 of this result.

4.

Much as the previous part, using part 2 of this result.
∎

5.3.2
The results

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation and suppose
\phi
,
\psi
, and
\chi
are pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
(Definition
4.7.2
).
Then:

1.

If
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
2
-twined then we have:

(a)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

(b)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\psi
then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
.

(c)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
.

2.

If
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
3
-twined then we have:

(a)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\psi
then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
.

(b)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\psi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\chi
then

.

(c)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

Recall that
\mathsf{correct}[\phi]
is from Definition
4.8.2
(
1
) and just means
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

Proof.

We reason as follows:

1.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
2
-twined.

(a)

We reason as follows:

\hskip-20.00003pt\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }l@{\quad}l}n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\Longleftrightarrow&\mbox{$\mathit{noi}$}(\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\})&\text{Proposition~\ref{prop.pointwise.dense.char}(\ref{item.dense.char.pointwise.QuorumBox})}\\
\Longrightarrow&\mbox{$\mathit{dense}$}(\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\phi\})&\text{Proposition~\ref{prop.noi.dense}, $(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})$ $2$-twined}\\
\Longleftrightarrow&n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi&\text{Proposition~\ref{prop.pointwise.dense.char}(\ref{item.dense.char.pointwise.CoquorumDiamond})}\end{array}

(b)

Suppose
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\psi
.
By part
1a
of this result,
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.
By Proposition
4.4.3
(
1
)
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\psi)
as required.

(c)

Suppose
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.
By part
1b
of this result,
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{correct}[\phi]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi)
.
Using Proposition
4.8.8
(
1
)
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.
By Lemma
4.2.2

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\phi
as required.

2.

The reasoning for the
3
-twined case is just as for the
2
-twined case, except we need intersection of three open sets instead of two.
∎

Corollary
5.3.3
specialises Proposition
5.3.2
to the useful special case of a correct unary predicate symbol, such that
\phi=\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]
(Definition
4.8.2
(
2
)) and
\psi=\mathsf{P}(v)
and
\chi=\mathsf{P}(v^{\prime})
.
In our later examples,
\mathsf{P}
will take values such as
\mathsf{accept}
,
\mathsf{send}
,
\mathsf{write}
, and
\mathsf{decide}
, representing basic actions in a consensus protocol:

Corollary 5.3.3
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and suppose
\mathsf{P}
is a unary predicate symbol and
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Then:

1.

If
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
3
-twined then:

(a)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(v)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(v^{\prime})
then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{P}(v)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{P}(v^{\prime}))
.

(b)

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(v)
then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(v)
.

2.

If
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
2
-twined then

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}(v)
implies
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{P}(v))
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

(
1a
)

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
3
-twined and

5.4
Further observations on intersection properties

For this Subsection we fix a signature
\Sigma
and a model
\mathcal{M}=(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}},\mathord{\text{Value}})
.

Remark 5.4.1
.

We collect some further results as follows:

1.

Proposition
5.4.2
proves converses to the implications in Proposition
5.3.2
, provided that the signature is nonempty.

This is interesting because it tells us that, provided the signature is nondegenerate, Proposition
5.3.2
accurately captures and reflects the intersection properties that interest us.

2.

Proposition
5.4.4
is a semitopological distillation of parts
1b
and
2b
of Proposition
5.3.2
.
Proposition
5.4.4

is
Proposition
5.3.2
and Corollary
5.3.3
, in semitopological rather than logical form.

In Proposition
5.4.2
we could replace every instance of the expression ‘
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}
’ with the (by Definition
4.8.2
(
1
) and Remark
4.8.4
) synonymous expression ‘
\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}]
’.
However, we have not set up a corresponding synonym for
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}
(it would be ‘
\mathsf{valid}[\mathsf{P}]
’; we just never wrote that definition) so it seems natural to stay with
\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt
here.

Proposition 5.4.2
.

Suppose
\mathsf{P}\in\mathord{\text{PredSymb}}
is a predicate constant symbol (Notation

(
1

Notation 5.4.3
.

Suppose
\mathord{\text{X}}
is a set.
Then:

1.

A
cover
of
\mathord{\text{X}}
is a set of sets
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathit{pow}$}(\mathord{\text{X}})
such that
\bigcup\mathcal{X}=\mathord{\text{X}}
.

2.

A
partition
of
\mathord{\text{X}}
is a cover of
\mathord{\text{X}}
all of whose elements are pairwise disjoint (so
\forall X,X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}.X\cap X^{\prime}=\varnothing\lor X=X^{\prime}
).

The reader might like to try giving a proof of Proposition
5.4.4
using direct calculations (without building on the results we have so far).
This is possible, but it is messy.
The use of logic endows the proofs with a structure and cleanness that the direct proof cannot match:

Proposition 5.4.4
.

Suppose
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is a semitopology and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation, and suppose
\phi
is a closed predicate that is pointwise in
(n,\varsigma)
.
Then:

1.

If
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
2
-twined then
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

2.

As a corollary, if
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})

2
-twined then any two-element partition of

(Notation
5.4.3

(
1

3.

If
(\mathord{\text{Point}},\mathord{\text{Open}})
is
3
-twined then

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi

1
)

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi)
, and simplifying further using de Morgan equivalences as per Figure
9
, we obtain

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
as required.

2.

Suppose
\{P_{1},P_{2}\}
partitions
\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
Assume a
0
-ary predicate symbol
\mathsf{P}
, and let
\varsigma
be the valuation that sets
\varsigma(\mathsf{P})_{n,p}={\mathbf{t}}
if
p\in P_{1}
and
\varsigma(\mathsf{P})_{n,p}={\mathbf{f}}
if
p\in P_{2}
.
46
46

46

We can assume
\mathsf{P}
and
\varsigma
because this result is about semitopologies, so we are free to impose any model structure on it that we like. It seems particularly nice here how the mere
existence
of a model for our logic gives us a clean proof of a purely semitopological property which, on the face of it, has nothing to do with our three-valued modal logic.

By part
1
and Lemma
2.2.4
either
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}
or
n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}
.
By Proposition
4.7.14
(
4
) either
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
is dense.

3.

We reason as for the proof of part

, using Proposition

(

) and starting from
\mathsf{F}\hskip-1.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\phi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{B}\hskip 0.0pt\phi
.

6
Declarative Paxos

6.1
Case study: a simple protocol

Remark 6.1.1
.

Before Paxos, we will warm up by sketching a simple protocol
Simple
, and then we will give four
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
axiomatisations of
Simple
, reflecting four different failure assumptions (messages can/cannot get lost; participants can/cannot crash).

Simple
is as follows:

1.

Any participant
p
may broadcast a
\mathsf{propose}
message to all participants.

2.

If a participant receives a
\mathsf{propose}
message, then it responds with
\mathsf{accept}
.

3.

If the participant
p
, having broadcast its
\mathsf{propose}
message, receives an
\mathsf{accept}
message, then it declares
\mathsf{decide}
.

Remark 6.1.2
.

Simple
is very simple!
Only one value can be proposed, so we do not even bother to state it; we just ‘propose’.
A participant accepts once it hears someone propose.
A participant that proposes, decides once it hears that someone accepted.

For comparison, in Paxos
multiple values
are available, so we need to make sure that participants all decide on the
same
value if they decide on anything at all.
This makes things much harder: so Paxos has multiple rounds; there is a designated leader participant at each round; the leader must choose a value to propose; and elaborate communication is in place to ensure that (subject to suitable precisely stated assumptions) a single choice of agreed value will eventually be decided.

But that is for later.
Even the simple protocol
Simple
has plenty of subtlety, which will allow us to exhibit (in ‘toy’ form) many of the techniques that we will then apply to Paxos:

Remark 6.1.3
.

We will axiomatise
Simple
subject to two parameters, yielding four possible axiomatisations:

1.

Do all messages arrive, or might there be a finite initial period during which the network may be unreliable so that during that time, messages might not arrive?

2.

Can participants crash (and if so, how many)?

All messages arrive; no participants crash

First, we assume an ideal situation such that all messages arrive, and no participants crash.
We can axiomatise this as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}{\mathsf{(SimpAccept?)}}&\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide?)}}&\mathsf{decide}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\\
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}&({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose})\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mathsf{accept}\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide!)}}&(\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mathsf{decide}\\
{\mathsf{(Correct)}}&\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{propose},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{decide}]\end{array}

Recall from Definition
2.3.1
and Proposition
2.3.3
that
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
is a
strong implication
and means ‘if the LHS is
{\mathbf{t}}
then so is the RHS’, and
recall from Proposition
4.7.14
(
1
) that
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{P}
means ‘
\mathsf{P}
somewhere’, where
\mathsf{P}\in\{\mathsf{propose},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{decide}\}
.

Then we explain the axioms (briefly) as follows:

•

We call
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide?)}}

backward rules
; they express backward versions of the rules in Remark
6.1.1
, namely that:

–

if a participant asserts
\mathsf{accept}
then some participant must have asserted
\mathsf{propose}
; and

–

if a participant asserts
\mathsf{decide}
then it must have asserted
\mathsf{propose}
, and some participant must have asserted
\mathsf{accept}
.

•

We call
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide!)}}

forward rules
.
They express forward versions of the rules in Remark
6.1.1
.
Because our failure assumptions are that nothing fails — all messages arrive; no participants crash — the forward rules are in this case just the backward rules, reversed.

•

Axiom
{\mathsf{(Correct)}}
expresses that all predicates return
{\mathbf{t}}
and
{\mathbf{f}}
(not
{\mathbf{b}}
), as per Definition
4.8.2
(
2
).
This reflects our assumption that no participants crash, because we model a computation crashing as the predicate describing it returning
{\mathbf{b}}
.

Note that
\mathsf{propose}
,
\mathsf{accept}
, and
\mathsf{decide}
above are not messages; they are propositions!
There is no message-passing.

\mathsf{accept}
being true at a participant represents that the participant accepts; this is implemented in the algorithm by the participant broadcasting an accept message, and represented in the logic by
\mathsf{accept}
taking truth-value
{\mathbf{t}}
at that participant.

Our example protocol is simple, but we hope it is already starting to illustrate how the various parts of our logic come together to express the protocol axiomatically.
We continue:

All messages arrive; participants can crash

We assume that all messages arrive, and participants can crash — but we assume there is a dense set of uncrashed participants.
Recall from Definition
4.7.11
(
1
) that a
dense set
is one that intersects every nonempty open set; in particular, this means that every nonempty open set of participants contains a participant that is not crashed.
We model being crashed as returning the third truth-value
{\mathbf{b}}
, and not being crashed as returning
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
.
We can axiomatise this as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}{\mathsf{(SimpAccept?)}}&\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide?)}}&\mathsf{decide}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\\
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}&({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose})\mathrel{\to}\mathsf{accept}\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide!)}}&(\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\mathrel{\to}\mathsf{decide}\\
{\mathsf{(Uncrashed)}}&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}\quad\mathsf{P}\in\{\mathsf{propose},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{decide}\}\end{array}

The backward rules are unchanged: if a participant accepts then from the form of the rules, it
must
be the case that somebody proposed; and if a participant decides then from the form of the rules, it
must
be the case that the participant proposed and some participant accepted.

The forward rules
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide!)}}
have changed, and they now use the weak implication
\mathrel{\to}
.
By Definition
2.3.1
and Proposition
2.3.3
,
\mathrel{\to}
means ‘if the LHS is
{\mathbf{t}}
then the RHS is
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
’.
The
{\mathbf{b}}
truth-value represents ‘crashed’, so that (for example)
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}
expresses that for each participant
p
, if some (possibly other) participant
q
asserts
\mathsf{propose}
, then
p
asserts
\mathsf{accept}

or

p
has crashed.

Axiom
{\mathsf{(Uncrashed)}}
reflects that any quorum (nonempty open set) of participants contains an uncrashed participant; e.g. in topological language, that the set of uncrashed participants is dense.

Messages might not arrive; no participants crash

We assume that there is an initial period during which the network might not deliver some messages (message failure might be partial, i.e. a general broadcast might arrive at some participants and not others), but the network eventually stabilises; in the literature this is called
eventual synchrony
.
No participants crash.
We can axiomatise this as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}{\mathsf{(SimpAccept?)}}&\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide?)}}&\mathsf{decide}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\\
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}&\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt(({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose})\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mathsf{accept})\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide!)}}&\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt((\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\mathsf{decide})\\
{\mathsf{(Correct)}}&\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{propose},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{decide}]\end{array}

The backward rules are unchanged, but the forward rules
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide!)}}
now have an

\mathsf{ultimately}

modality.
This is from Figure
6
and it means intuitively ‘eventually, this holds forever everywhere’, reflecting an assumption that eventually the network stabilises.

Note that
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
does not explicitly represent message-passing, but this does not matter as much as one might suppose.
The

\mathsf{ultimately}

modality captures the essence of what we need, namely, that eventually the forward rules become valid at every open set and every point.

Messages might not arrive; participants can crash

We assume that messages might not arrive, and participants can crash but there is a dense set of uncrashed participants (so that every quorum of participants contains at least one uncrashed participant).
We can axiomatise this just by combining the ideas above, as follows:

\begin{array}[]{l@{\qquad}l}{\mathsf{(SimpAccept?)}}&\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide?)}}&\mathsf{decide}\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}(\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\\
{\mathsf{(SimpAccept!)}}&\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt(({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose})\mathrel{\to}\mathsf{accept})\\
{\mathsf{(SimpDecide!)}}&\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt((\mathsf{propose}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept})\mathrel{\to}\mathsf{decide})\\
{\mathsf{(Uncrashed)}}&{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{P}\quad\mathsf{P}\in\{\mathsf{propose},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{decide}\}\end{array}

The axiomatisations of Declarative Paxos and Simpler Declarative Paxos in Figures
11
and
12
are more elaborate than the axioms above, because Paxos is a more complicated protocol, but the basic ideas are as above; just scaled up.

6.2
A high-level view of the Paxos algorithm

Paxos is notorious for being hard to understand.
The reader can find detailed treatments in
[
Lam98
,
Lam01
,
Cac11
]
.
A Wikipedia article
[
Wik24c
]
is accessible and clear, and a clear modern description is in Section 4 of
[
PLSS17
]
.

Remark 6.2.1

(High-level view of Paxos)
.

Paxos allows participants to crash, and messages to fail to arrive at their destination, but it assumes all participants are honest (i.e. that if they have not crashed then they follow the rules of the algorithm).

Here is a high-level view of the algorithm:

1.

Leader proposes:

A designated leader participant (if it does not crash) sends messages to all participants,
proposing
some unique value
v
.

Message-passing is unreliable, so messages may fail to arrive.

2.

Participant sends:

If a participant
p
receives a propose
v
message from the leader — it is assumed that every participant knows who the leader is, and other participants cannot forge the leader’s signature — then
p
sends a
send
message back to the leader, which carries the value
v_{p}
that
p
most recently accepted, along with the timestamp of when
v_{p}
was accepted.
47
47

47

For reasons of compact presentation we do not explicitly represent the timestamps in our axiomatisation; i.e.
\mathsf{send}
is a unary predicate in Definition
6.3.1
(
1
), not a binary predicate.
If this bothers the reader then we can just say that, actually,
v_{p}
contains a timestamp of the stage
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
at which it was accepted; i.e. that
v_{p}
is actually implemented as a pair
(v_{p}^{\prime},n_{p})
of a value and a time.

If
p
has never accepted any value, so no most recently accepted value exists, then
p
sends back a special
\mathsf{undef}
value.

3.

Leader writes:

If the leader receives messages from a quorum and at least one of these messages reports some previously accepted value, then the leader
writes
a most recently accepted value.
If every reply in that quorum reports
\mathsf{undef}
, then the leader writes the value
v
that it proposed in step
1
.
If the leader receives replies from no quorum, then it writes nothing.

4.

Participant accepts:

If a participant receives a write message from the leader for some value, then it sends a message back
accepting
that value.

5.

Leader decides:

If the leader receives accept messages for some value from a quorum of participants, it decides on that value, and sends out a
decide
message to all participants with that value.

6.

Participant (non-leader) decides:

If a participant receives a decide message from the leader for some value, then it decides on that value and stops.

Remark 6.2.2

(High-level view of our axiomatisation)
.

The theory
TyPax
in Definition
6.3.1
and Figure
11
is a declarative abstraction of Paxos-the-algorithm.
It stands in relation to implementations of Paxos in roughly the same way that (say) the mathematical specification of the Fibonacci function stands in relation to its concrete implementations, in the sense that we elide implementational detail to preserve (and reveal) a mathematical ideal of what the implementation does.

TyPax
elides two features of the concrete implementation:

1.

There is no explicit message-passing; there are only predicates returning truth-values.

This may seem counterintuitive — isn’t Paxos
supposed
to be a message-passing algorithm? — but we shall see that a lot of the character of Paxos survives.
Indeed, arguably (and certainly for the first author) it is much easier to see what is really going on once we have done this.

2.

There is no abstract machine, and therefore no
algorithmic time
, by which we mean no sequence of state-changes of the abstract machine.

The only notion of time is the
stage

n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
from the context as defined in Definition
3.2.4
(
1
).
We may call this
logical time
and (when we are modelling Paxos) it represents the sequence of what the distributed consensus algorithms literature often calls
stages
, or
ballot numbers
.
48
48

48

In a distributed algorithm, at any point in algorithmic time, different participants in the algorithm may be at different points in logical time. A practical example: consider several participants racing across 100 metres. In logical time, they are just counting to 100. In algorithmic time, one of them will get to 100
first
, and win. In this paper, we only care about the logical aspects of the process; there may still be a winner participant, but this would be implemented just as a nullary predicate
\mathsf{winner}
which is true at at most one participant.

We will prove standard Paxos correctness properties for
TyPax
in Section
7
.

6.3
Definition of Declarative Paxos

Figure 11
:
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
: Axioms of Declarative Paxos (Definition
6.3.1
)

Recall from Definition
3.6.2
(
1
) that a
theory
is a pair of a
signature
and a set of
axioms
over that signature.
Then:

Definition 6.3.1
.

We define
Declarative Paxos
to be the logical theory

\text{TyPax}=(\Sigma_{\text{TyPax}},\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}),

where:

1.

the signature
\Sigma_{\text{TyPax}}
is

\Sigma_{\text{TyPax}}=[\mathsf{leader}:0;\ \mathsf{propose},\mathsf{send},\mathsf{write},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{decide}:1],

as per the notation in Definition
3.2.1
(
4
), and

2.

the axioms
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
are as written in Figure
11
.

In axioms
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose?)}}
,
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxNontriv)}}
we assume an element
\mathsf{undef}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
, to model the ‘undefined’ value from Paxos (it does not matter what
\mathsf{undef}\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
is; we just have to choose some element and use it consistently).

Remark 6.3.2
.

For convenient reference, we give pointers to help unpack the notation used in Figure
11
.
Note that this is just an overview focussing on items of notation used in the axioms, to help with reading them; in-depth discussions of the axioms will follow:

1.

We use the functional notation from Notation
3.7.2
to elide quantified variables where convenient.
For example, axioms
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxAccept!)}}
written in full are

\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{propose}(a)\quad\text{and}\quad\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt(({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{write}(a))\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{accept}(a)).

2.

\mathrel{\to}
and
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
are the
weak
and
strong
implications respectively.
Truth-tables are in Figure
1
and a discussion is in Subsection
2.3
.
In particular, by Proposition
2.3.3
,

•

\phi\mathrel{\to}\phi^{\prime}
means ‘if
\phi
is true then
\phi^{\prime}
is valid’ (where ‘valid’ means ‘
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
’) and

•

\phi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\phi^{\prime}
means ‘if
\phi
is true then
\phi^{\prime}
is true’.

3.

The compound modalities —
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
,
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
, and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt
— are discussed in Subsection
4.4
.
Their meaning in full generality is in Lemma
4.4.2
, but because the axioms only apply them to
pointwise
predicates, their meanings simplify to ‘somewhere’ (
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}
), ‘everywhere’ (
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}
), ‘for a set with a nonempty open interior’ (
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
), and ‘for a dense set’ (
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip-1.0pt\Diamond}\hskip 0.0pt
) respectively, as per Proposition
4.7.14
.

4.

\mathsf{ultimately}

means
