\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{sometime}$}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{forever}$}\hskip 0.0pt
and is from Figure
6
.

We use this in our axiomatisation to reflect a standard assumption in distributed systems of
eventual network synchrony
, or synonymously
global stabilisation (of the network)
, meaning that the communications network may be unstable and fail to pass messages at first, but then after a finite but unbounded amount of time, it becomes stable and messages arrive.
Our logic does not explicitly represent messages, but the use of

\mathsf{ultimately}

in our forward rules reflects this assumption about the underlying implementation.

5.

\exists_{01}

in
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose01)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite01)}}
means ‘for zero or one elements’, as per Subsection
4.6
, so these two axioms intuitively state that each participant proposes or writes at most one value.

Note that our logic is three-valued, so what these axioms mean
precisely
is something a little richer than is the case in two-valued logic.
In particular, if
\forall v.{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mathsf{write}(v)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}}={\mathbf{b}}
— corresponding to the case where a participant is crashed — then
\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{01}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{write}
,
even though

\mbox{$\mathit{ctx}$}\vDash\mathsf{write}(v)
holds for every
v
.
See the discussion and results in Subsection
4.6
.

6.

\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{propose}]
means that
\mathsf{propose}
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
(not
{\mathbf{b}}
) on every value; see Definition
4.8.2
(
2
).
Similarly for the other unary predicate symbols in
{\mathsf{(PaxPCorrect)}}
.

7.

\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v
means ‘a most recently accepted value is
v
’.
See the discussion and definition in Subsection
4.9
.

8.

Finally, note that we intend the predicates in Figure
11
to be read as axioms.

What it means for an axiom to be valid in a model — i.e. how we judge whether a model satisfies
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
, or how we judge whether a predicate is valid in all models of
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
— is unpacked in Figure
7
and Definition
3.6.2
(
2
).
We discuss the quantifier scoping in those definitions in Remark
3.6.3
.

Notation 6.3.3
.

Technically,
TyPax
the theory ‘Declarative Paxos’ from Definition
6.3.1
and Figure
11
consists of a pair of
\Sigma_{\text{TyPax}}
the
signature
of Declarative Paxos, and
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
the
axioms
of Declarative Paxos.

For clarity — this is a standard notational shorthand — we may identify
TyPax
with its set of axioms where convenient.
For instance we may write
\vDash_{\varsigma}\text{TyPax}
as shorthand for
\vDash_{\varsigma}\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
; see for example Lemma
7.2.2
and Theorem
7.2.3
.

6.4
High-level discussion of the axioms

Remark 6.4.1
.

The predicates in Figure
11
are intended as a
theory
, in the sense of Definition
3.6.2
, which is why we call them
axioms
and not just ‘some predicates’.

That is: we fix a signature
\Sigma
that has (at least) the predicate symbols required to express the axioms of the theory
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
in Figure
11
, and we fix a model
\mathcal{M}
, and we derive properties that hold in all the valuations
\varsigma
to
\mathcal{M}
such that
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
is valid in the sense of Figure
7
.

At least as much design effort went into crafting the axioms
\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
of Declarative Paxos in Figure
11
, as went into designing the three-valued modal fixpoint logic in which the axioms are expressed.
49
49

49

Of course there was design feedback in both directions; we put into the logic what we needed to express the axioms, and we refined the axioms to elegantly work within the logic.

Therefore, we will take our time to discuss the axioms in detail.
The reader who just wants to see proofs can skip the rest of this Section (for now) and look to Section
7
onwards.

Remark 6.4.2
.

We have organised the axioms in Figure
11
into three groups, which we now discuss:

1.

Backward (inference) rules.

Axioms with names ending with
?
represent backwards reasoning and so we call them
backward rules
.
They use the strong implication
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
(Definition
2.3.1
(
2
)), which means ‘if the LHS is true (
{\mathbf{t}}
) then the RHS is true (
{\mathbf{t}}
)’.

The backward rules represent a strong inference that
if
some
Y
is observed to be
{\mathbf{t}}
then it
must
be because some other property
X
is
{\mathbf{t}}
.
For instance,
{\mathsf{(PaxAccept?)}}
says that if
\mathsf{accept}(v)
is true, then
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{write}(v)
must also be true.

We discuss the backward rules individually in Remark
6.5.2
below.

2.

Forward (progress) rules.

Axioms with names ending with
!
represent forwards reasoning about making progress, so we call them
forward rules
.
They use

\mathsf{ultimately}

and the weak implication
\mathrel{\to}
(Definition
2.3.1
(
1
)), which means ‘if the LHS is true (
{\mathbf{t}}
) then the RHS is valid (
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{b}}
)’.

We derive the forward rules from the backward rules using a systematic recipe which we discuss in Remark
6.4.3
.

The forward rules represent a weak inference that
if
some precondition
X
is
{\mathbf{t}}
then — provided the network is stable and provided that the participant has not crashed (where ‘crashed’ is interpreted as returning
{\mathbf{b}}
) — then the postcondition
Y
is
{\mathbf{t}}
.

For instance,
{\mathsf{(PaxAccept!)}}
expresses that, after network synchrony (Remark
6.3.2
(
4
)), if
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{write}
is true (by Notation
3.7.2
this is shorthand for
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{write}(a)
) then if the participant has not crashed then
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{accept}
(i.e.
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\mathsf{accept}(a)
) is true.

We discuss the forward rules in Remark
6.5.3
below.

3.

Other rules, for correctness and liveness.

Other rules reflect particular conditions of the algorithms.
For example,
{\mathsf{(PaxNontriv)}}
asserts that there exists a value other than
\mathsf{undef}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{undef}
is a value reserved by the later stages of the protocol to indicate the absence of a previously accepted value, so we wish to exclude a pathological model in which the protocol has participants and a leader, but there are no non-
\mathsf{undef}
values for it to propose, so that it is impossible for the leader to behave correctly (even if it wants to).
This would not actually be mathematically wrong, but it is not what we intend.
We discuss the other rules in Remark
6.5.1
.

Remark 6.4.3

(Recipe for deriving forward rules from backward rules)
.

A design feature of Figure
11
is that each forward rule is obtained from the corresponding backward rule by a
recipe
.
Namely: for a backward rule of the form

{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\ \chi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\psi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]

where

•

\chi
expresses some possible
side-conditions
(e.g.
\chi=\mathsf{leader}
in
{\mathsf{(PaxDecideL?)}}
) and

•

\psi
expresses some
postconditions
, and

•

\phi
expresses some
preconditions
,

our recipe constructs a forward rule of the form

({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\,\chi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi)\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}a.\psi.

In most of the forward rules this result also gets wrapped in an

\mathsf{ultimately}

modality, representing network synchrony (Remark
6.3.2
(
4
)).

This recipe holds for every pair of rules:

•

We see it cleanly in the pairs
{\mathsf{(PaxAccept?)}}
/
{\mathsf{(PaxAccept!)}}
,
{\mathsf{(PaxDecideL?)}}
/
{\mathsf{(PaxDecideL!)}}
, and
{\mathsf{(PaxDecide{\boldsymbol{\neg}}L?)}}
/
{\mathsf{(PaxDecide{\boldsymbol{\neg}}L!)}}
.
These rules follow our recipe symbol-for-symbol.

•

We see this also with
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose?)}}
/
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose!)}}
.
Our recipe generates the following version of
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose!)}}
:

({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\,\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}v{\boldsymbol{\neq}}\mathsf{undef})\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\mathsf{propose}(v).

The reader can check that in the presence of
{\mathsf{(PaxNontriv)}}
, this is logically equivalent to what is written in Figure
11
.

•

It is less clear how our recipe works for
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxSend!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite!)}}
; e.g.
{\mathsf{(PaxSend!)}}
looks different from
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
— it is clearly simpler!
Remarkably, these pairs too are consistent with our recipe; see Remark
6.6.1
and the subsequent discussion and results.

Remark 6.4.4
.

We make some further elementary comments on our recipe from Remark
6.4.3
:

1.

We uncurry the forward rules because this is convenient for some proofs.

2.

A forward rule of the form
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.((\chi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi)\mathrel{\to}\psi)
would be
wrong
.
This is just a different predicate and is not what is intended.

3.

A forward rule of the form
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.((\chi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi)\mathrel{\to}\psi)
would not necessarily be wrong, but it is stronger than we need for our proofs.
This reflects a general design principle in logical theories that
weaker axioms are better
.
If we can prove the same result from weaker axioms then generally speaking we should, because that is a stronger result.
50
50

50

In practice, working from weaker axioms tends to force us to think harder and so design a better-engineered proof.

6.5
More detailed discussion of the axioms

Remark 6.5.1
.

We discuss the
axioms for correctness and liveness
:

1.

{\mathsf{(LdrExist)}}
:

In implementations of Paxos, at each stage there is a unique
leader
, whose identity is computed by applying (what amounts to) a random number generator to an agreed seed.
At the level of abstraction in this paper, we just assume that a leader exists — we do not care how it is chosen.

We use a predicate constant symbol
\mathsf{leader}
to identify the leader.
Because our logic is modal — across time
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and participants
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
— we can just assume that a constant symbol
\mathsf{leader}
is assigned truth-value
{\mathbf{t}}
where a participant
p
is a leader at a stage
n
, and is assigned truth-value
{\mathbf{f}}
where a participant
p
is not a leader at a stage
n
.

In more technical language:
\varsigma
is such that
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{leader}
for every time
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and participant
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
, and
p
is a leader precisely when
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{leader}
.

{\mathsf{(LdrExist)}}
just asserts that a participant either is a leader or it is not, and some leader exists.

2.

{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}
:

This axiom expresses that there is a unique leader, as per the standard assumption of Paxos.
Technically,
{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}
expresses
\exists_{01}p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\mathsf{leader}(p)
— that there exist zero or one leaders — but combined with
{\mathsf{(LdrExist)}}
this means that there exists a unique leader.
The axiom does not use an explicit quantification over points because our logical syntax in Figure
3
does not have this.
Perhaps surprisingly, it is still possible to express uniqueness of the leader.
We defer a discussion to Subsection
6.6.3
.

It may also be worth noting that
{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}
, though it expresses a standard Paxos assumption, turns out to be stronger than we need for our proofs; more on this in Subsection
7.3.2
.

3.

{\mathsf{(LdrCorrect)}}
:

{\mathsf{(LdrCorrect)}}
asserts that for infinitely many times
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
, the leader
l
(which exists by the right-hand conjunct in
{\mathsf{(LdrExist)}}
) is
\mathsf{propose}
-correct,
\mathsf{write}
-correct, and
\mathsf{decide}
-correct (terminology from Definition
4.8.2
(
4
)).

Unpacking Definition
4.8.2
(
4
), this means that
n,l\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{propose}(v)
for every
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
, and similarly for
\mathsf{write}
and
\mathsf{decide}
.
Intuitively, this is a
liveness assumption
that there is always a future time when the leader is not crashed in the sense that it can correctly
{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{f}}
-propose,
{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{f}}
-write, and
{\mathbf{t}}{\mathbf{f}}
-decide a value.

4.

{\mathsf{(PaxPCorrect)}}
:

Given a valuation
\varsigma
, we unpack Definition
3.6.1
(
4
) to see what
\vDash_{\varsigma}{\mathsf{(PaxPCorrect)}}
actually means, as per Definition
3.6.2
:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}.\exists O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}.\forall p\in O.\forall v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}.n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{P}(v)]\\
\text{for each}\ \mathsf{P}\in\{\mathsf{propose},\mathsf{send},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{write},\mathsf{decide}\}.

This corresponds to a standard correctness assumption in Paxos that at each stage there is a quorum of uncrashed participants.

However, the axiom
{\mathsf{(PaxPCorrect)}}
is subtly more general than the standard correctness assumption, because there is no restriction that the
same
open set
O
be used for each unary predicate symbol.
51
51

51

If we wanted the same
O
, then we would scope the
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt
modality further out and write
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{propose}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{send}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{accept}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{write}]\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{correct}[\mathsf{decide}])
. This turns out to be unnecessary.

It turns out that the proofs require only this assumption, and no more.

5.

{\mathsf{(PaxPropose01)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite01)}}
:

We justify these axioms with respect to the Paxos algorithm as summarised in Remark
6.2.1
:

•

Step
1
of Remark
6.2.1
states that the leader is crashed or proposes one value.
The intuitive correspondence to
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose01)}}
is clear.

•

Step
3
of Remark
6.2.1
states that the leader writes a value derived from
the first
quorum of participants that it hears back from (or none, if it hears back from no such quorum).
One corollary of this is that it writes at most one value.
Our logical theory has no state machine and notion of algorithmic time (as per Remark
6.2.2
(
2
)) — so there is no sense in which information from one open set ‘arrives’ at the leader ‘before’ information from another — so from the point of view of the logic, of all the open sets available, the leader picks at most one to act on.
This is axiom
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite01)}}
.

It is a fact that
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose01)}}
+
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite01)}}
+
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite!)}}
give us

\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}\quad\text{and}\quad\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{ultimately}$}\hskip 0.0pt((\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{send})\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose})\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists_{1}$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{write}),

and

{\mathsf{(PaxPropose01)}}
+
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite01)}}
+
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}
give us

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}\quad\text{and}\quad{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{write}.

So it follows from our axioms that a leader participant proposes and writes one value (assuming it is not crashed, and if messages arrive), and a non-leader participant proposes and writes zero values.

6.

{\mathsf{(Pax2Twined)}}
:

This axiom collects a set of instances of Proposition
5.3.2
(
1a
), namely taking
\phi=\mathsf{accept}(v)
in that Lemma for every
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
.

There is a design choice here: instead of an axiom
{\mathsf{(Pax2Twined)}}
, we could insist specifically that the semitopology in our underlying model be
2
-twined.
Then
{\mathsf{(Pax2Twined)}}
ceases to be an axiom, but we still have it as a fact of the model by directly invoking Proposition
5.3.2
(
1a
).
Or we could be even more specific: we could insist that the underlying semitopology be
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
(Definition
3.1.4
) for
N>2*f
; by Proposition
5.2.3
(
1
) this is
2
-twined, and then we can use Proposition
5.3.2
(
1a
) to obtain
{\mathsf{(Pax2Twined)}}
.

We choose here to express as an axiom precisely the property that our proofs require (we use
{\mathsf{(Pax2Twined)}}
just once, in the Agreement property Proposition
7.3.5
).
This is concise, general, and yields clean proofs.

Insisting on a
2
-twined semitopology, or specifically on the semitopology
\mathsf{AllBut}_{f}(N)
for
N>2*f
, would also make sense — but the axiom is very precise, allowing us to pinpoint what logical property is required and where.

Remark 6.5.2
.

We discuss the
axioms for the backward rules
:

1.

{\mathsf{(PaxPropose?)}}
:

If we propose some value
v
then

(a)

we are the leader and

(b)

v
is not the undefined value
\mathsf{undef}
.

2.

{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
:

If we send some value
v
then

(a)

somebody must have proposed some value and

(b)

i.

either

v
is the value we have most recently accepted,

ii.

or
we never accepted any value and
v
is the undefined value.

3.

{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}
:

If we write some value
v
then

(a)

we are a leader, and

(b)

there exists an open set of points (= participants) that have all sent us values, and

(c)

i.

either
some point in that open set has most recently accepted
v
,

ii.

or

v
is the value that we proposed, and no point in that open set has ever accepted any value.

4.

{\mathsf{(PaxAccept?)}}
:

If we accept
v
then somebody somewhere must have written
v
.

5.

{\mathsf{(PaxDecideL?)}}
:

If a leader decides
v
then an open set of points must have all accepted
v
.

6.

{\mathsf{(PaxDecide{{\boldsymbol{\neg}}}L?)}}
:

If we are not a leader and we decide
v
, then some leader somewhere must have decided
v
.

Remark 6.5.3
.

We discuss the
axioms for the forward rules
.

We noted in Remark
6.4.3
our design methodology of deriving the forward rules from the backward rules; forward rules are rules for making progress, so they are existentially quantified over the variable
v
and they use the weak implication
\mathrel{\to}
to emulate possible crashes.

The other subtlety is the

\mathsf{ultimately}

modality wrapping all of the forward rules, except for
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose!)}}
.

•

What

\mathsf{ultimately}

does for us in most of the forward rules.

It models possible network failure, and eventual network synchrony.
As touched on in Remark
6.3.2
(
4
), the

\mathsf{ultimately}

modality reflects the possibility that the network might be down for a while, but eventually — ‘finally’ — the network stabilises and messages arrive.

Predicates in our logic are
not
messages, but we get the effect of eventual network synchrony in our logic (even though messages are not explicitly present) by wrapping the implications in our forward rules in the

\mathsf{ultimately}

modality.

More on this when we introduce the notion of
global stabilisation of logical time
in Subsection
7.4.1
.

•

Why

\mathsf{ultimately}

is absent from
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose!)}}
.

Because this rule models a property of the implementation that involves no network communication.
The leader does not need to communicate with any other participant to pick a value to propose.

It is true that the leader’s propose message might not arrive if the network is down — but this is reflected by the use of

\mathsf{ultimately}

in the
{\mathsf{(PaxSend!)}}
rule.

6.6
Some fiddly observations

6.6.1
An aside on
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}

{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}
from Figure
11
contains this predicate:

(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept})\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v.

Unpacking what this means (cf. Remark
4.9.1
), this looks into the past to check whether
v
is a value that was most recently accepted by some participant.

Note that this slightly differently scoped predicate would be
wrong
:

\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v).

This finds some participant such that
v
is a value that was most recently accepted by
that
participant (but there might be another participant who more recently accepted some other value).

The reader with a background in logic will know all about how important scoping can be (e.g.
\forall x.\exists y.\phi
vs.
\exists x.\forall y.\phi
, and
\exists x.(\phi\land\phi^{\prime})
vs.
\exists x.\phi\land\exists x.\phi^{\prime}
), but perhaps the point bears repeating.

6.6.2
An aside on the backward and forward rules for
\mathsf{send}
and
\mathsf{write}

Remark 6.6.1
.

In Remark
6.4.3
we proposed a recipe for deriving a forward rule from a backward one: a backward rule of the form

{\mathsf{(Rule?)}}\qquad{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\ \chi\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\psi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}\phi[a{{:}\text{=}}v]

generates a forward rule (possibly wrapped in an

\mathsf{ultimately}

modality) of the form

{\mathsf{(Rule!)}}\qquad({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\,\chi\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\phi)\mathrel{\to}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\psi.

We noted in Remark
6.4.3
that the recipe works cleanly for some rules — but it was less clear how it can be applied to derive
{\mathsf{(PaxSend!)}}
from
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
, and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite!)}}
from
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}
.
In these cases, the left-hand sides of
{\mathsf{(PaxSend!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite!)}}
seem much simpler than the right-hand sides of
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}
respectively.

We now show why, in fact,
{\mathsf{(PaxSend!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite!)}}
are correct and make sense in the context of our recipe, culminating with Corollary
6.6.3
.

Lemma 6.6.2
.

We have:

1.

\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\bigl((\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)\ \mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}\mathsf{undef})\bigr)\ \mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\ {\mathbf{t}}
.

2.

\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.\bigl(\ ((\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept})\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)\ \mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\ ({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{propose}(v))\ \bigr)\ \mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\ (\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept})\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}
.

Note that:

•

The notation
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{accept}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}
and
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}
is from Notation
3.7.2
.

•

We do not restrict to contexts and valuations that satisfy
TyPax
; the meaning of the
\vDash
symbol above is as in Definition
3.6.1
(
5
).

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

We distribute the
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v
through the
\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}
, and then through the
\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}
on the right-hand side (we can do this because
v
is not free in the left-hand conjunct) and we see that it suffices to show

\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.(\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)\ \mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\ ({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}\mathsf{undef})\ \mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\ {\mathbf{t}}

Using Lemma
4.9.4
this simplifies to just

\vDash\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{accept}\ \mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\ ({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}\mathsf{undef})\ \mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\ {\mathbf{t}}.

This clearly holds, just taking
v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}\mathsf{undef}
.

2.

We distribute
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v
into the predicate as we did for the proof of part
1
.
We see that it suffices to show

\vDash\bigl(\ {\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.((\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept})\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)\ \mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\ ({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose})\ \bigr)\ \mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\ \mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}\ \mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}\ {\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}.

We obtain this by simplifying the left-hand side using Lemma
4.9.4
.
∎

We can now justify the left-hand sides of
{\mathsf{(PaxSend!)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite!)}}
as being consistent with our recipe as discussed above in Remark
6.6.1
:

Corollary 6.6.3
.

Suppose
\varsigma
is a valuation such that
\vDash_{\varsigma}\Theta_{\text{TyPax}}
.
Then we have:

1.

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ }l}\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.&\bigl({\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}\ \mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\bigl((\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)\ \mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}v{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}\mathsf{undef})\bigr)\bigr)\\
&\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}\end{array}

2.

\begin{array}[t]{r@{\ }l}\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.&\bigl(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt(\Box{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{send}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\bigl(((\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept})\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}({\boldsymbol{\neg}}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{propose}(v))\bigr))\bigr)\\
&\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt\bigl(\Box\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{send}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}(\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\boldsymbol{\vee}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose})\bigr)\\
&\mathbin{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\equiv$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{send}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{propose}\end{array}

Proof.

For both parts, the first equivalence follows by elementary predicate manipulations, distributing the
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v
quantifier down into the predicate and using Lemma
6.6.2
.
In the second part, the second equivalence follows by routine reasoning using the other rules of
TyPax
and in particular
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
.
By
{\mathsf{(PaxSend?)}}
and
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose?)}}
and the other rules, if someone
{\mathbf{t}}
-sent a value then some (possibly distinct) value must be
{\mathbf{t}}
-proposed by the unique leader, so we can drop the
\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}
disjunct.
The rest of the argument is routine from truth-tables.
∎

6.6.3
Axiom
{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}
: uniqueness of the leader

Recall axiom
{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}
from
TyPax
in Figure
11
:

{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}\quad\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X}).

Note that
\mathsf{X}
is not positive in
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})
, because
\mathsf{X}
occurs under a negation
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}
.
Thus, the
\mu
is still taking an intersection, but that intersection does not necessarily determine a fixpoint.
This is fine mathematically.
We just are now outside the bounds of fixpoint logic, and in a larger design space.

In Remark
6.5.1
(
2
) we claimed that
{\mathsf{(LdrUniq)}}
expresses that there are zero or one leaders.
We now show how this works, culminating with Corollary
6.6.7
.

Definition 6.6.4
.

Suppose

n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
and
\varsigma
is a valuation and
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
.
We define:

1.

\mbox{$\mathit{leaders}$}_{n,\varsigma}=\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid\varsigma(\mathsf{leader})_{n,p}={\mathbf{t}}\}
.
Call this the set of
n,\varsigma
-leaders
, or if
n
and
\varsigma
are understood, just the set of
leaders
.

2.

z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}={[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\varkappa)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=\bigvee_{p\in O}(\varsigma(\mathsf{leader})_{n,p}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma))
.

3.

Say that
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
is
admissible
when either of the two following equivalent conditions holds:

•

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}z_{n^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma^{\prime},\varkappa}\leq\varkappa(n^{\prime},p^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma^{\prime})
for every
n^{\prime},p^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma^{\prime}
, or equivalently

•

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\varkappa(n^{\prime},p^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma^{\prime})\leq z_{n^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma^{\prime},\varkappa}
for every
n^{\prime},p^{\prime},O^{\prime},\varsigma^{\prime}
.

4.

Define

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ }c@{\ }l}M(n,p,O,\varsigma)&=&{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{Fig.~\ref{fig.3.phi.f}}}}{{=}}&\bigl(\bigwedge\{\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}\mid{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\varkappa)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq\varkappa\}\bigr)(n,p,O,\varsigma)\\
&=&\bigwedge\{\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}\mid\varkappa\ \text{ admissible}\}\,(n,p,O,\varsigma)\\
&=&\bigwedge\{\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)\mid\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}\ \text{ admissible}\}.\end{array}

In words:
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})]\hskip-1.99997pt]}
as a context valuation is equal to the meet (in
{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
the set of all context valuations) of all the admissible
\varkappa
.

Remark 6.6.5
.

The proofs below are quite detailed, so we first offer an intuitive sketch of why in some sense Proposition
6.6.6
and Corollary
6.6.7

should
work.

Note below that a key construction is to take an intersection over all
\varkappa
such that (in simplified form)
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\varkappa)]\hskip-1.99997pt]}\leq\varkappa
.
This condition is monotone, by which we mean that if
\varkappa
satisfies it and
\varkappa\leq\varkappa^{\prime}
then so does
\varkappa^{\prime}
.
Thus the intersection is in some sense ‘created’ by minimal
\varkappa
.
Now note that a
\varkappa
that identifies one leader (by being non-false at that leader and false everywhere else), satisfies the condition.
Therefore, if there is just one leader to identify, then the intersection of all suitable
\varkappa
is just the unique least identifying
\varkappa
; but if there are two leaders then the intersection of all suitable
\varkappa
will include an intersection of two minimal identification functions identifying two distinct leaders, whose intersection identifies no elements.

Proposition 6.6.6
.

Suppose that

(n,p,O,\varsigma)
is a context, so that
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
is a stage,
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
is a nonempty open set, and
\varsigma
is a valuation.
Suppose further that
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{leader}
.

Then using the notation and terminology from Definition
6.6.4
, we have that
M(n,p,O,\varsigma)
takes the following values on
(n,p,O,\varsigma)
:

•

If
O
contains no leaders then
M(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{t}}
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.

•

If
O
contains precisely one leader
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
then
M(n,p,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{b}}
and
M(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{f}}
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus\{p\}
.

•

If
O
contains two or more leaders then
M(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{f}}
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.

Proof.

Note that since we assumed
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{leader}
, we know that
\mathsf{leader}
is two-valued (returns
{\mathbf{t}}
or
{\mathbf{f}}
, but not
{\mathbf{b}}
); so a point either is a leader or is not a leader.

We are interested in determining the value of
M
, and this value is a meet (greatest lower bound) of admissible context valuations
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
.

Fix some arbitrary
\leq
-minimal admissible context valuation
\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}
(if any such exists).
We now consider the possible values of
\lambda p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)
, and from this we will deduce what values
M
must take:

1.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{leaders}$}_{n,\varsigma}\cap O=\varnothing
.
In words: there are no leaders in
O
.

By Definition
6.6.4
(
2
)

z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}={\mathbf{f}}
(regardless of
\varkappa
) and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}={\mathbf{t}}
.
We assumed that
\varkappa
is admissible so by Definition
6.6.4
(
3
)
\varkappa(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{t}}
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
, and thus

M(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{t}}

for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.

2.

Suppose
\mbox{$\mathit{leaders}$}_{n,\varsigma}\cap O=\{p\}
for some
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
In words: there is precisely one leader
p
in
O
.

By Definition
6.6.4
(
2
)

z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}=\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)
and
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}={\boldsymbol{\neg}}\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)
.

We assumed that
\varkappa
is admissible,
so by Definition
6.6.4
(
3
)

{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)\leq\varkappa(n,q,O,\varsigma)
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
In particular,
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)\leq\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)
, so that we have two subcases: that
\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{b}}
or
\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{t}}
.
We consider each in turn:

•

Suppose
z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}=\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{b}}
.

Then
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{b}}={\mathbf{b}}\leq\varkappa(n,q,O,\varsigma)
for all
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.

•

Suppose
z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}=\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{t}}
.

Then
{\boldsymbol{\neg}}{\mathbf{t}}={\mathbf{f}}\leq\varkappa(n,q,O,\varsigma)
for all
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
This condition is vacuous.

It follows that for
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
,

M(n,q,O,\varsigma)=\begin{cases}{\mathbf{b}}&q=p\\
{\mathbf{f}}&q\neq p\end{cases}

3.

Suppose
p\neq p^{\prime}\in\mbox{$\mathit{leaders}$}_{n,\varsigma}\cap O
for two distinct
p\neq p^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
In words: there is more than one leader in
O
.

By Definition
6.6.4
(
2
)

z_{n,O,\varsigma,\varkappa}=\bigvee_{p\in O\cap\mbox{$\mathit{leaders}$}_{n,\varsigma}}\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma).

We just require
\varkappa
to be admissible, and unpacking this requirement, we require that

\forall q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}.\ {\boldsymbol{\neg}}\varkappa(n,q,O,\varsigma)\leq\bigvee_{p\in O\cap\mbox{$\mathit{leaders}$}_{n,\varsigma}}\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma).

We see that possible values for
\varkappa
include the following:

•

\varkappa(n,p,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{t}}
and
\varkappa(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{f}}
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus\{p\}
, or

•

\varkappa(n,p^{\prime},O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{t}}
and
\varkappa(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{f}}
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\setminus\{p^{\prime}\}
.

M(n,q,O,\varsigma)
is the meet of all admissible
\varkappa
, and it follows that
M(n,q,O,\varsigma)={\mathbf{f}}
for every
q\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
.
∎

Corollary 6.6.7
.

Suppose
(n,p,O,\varsigma)
is a context, so that
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
is a stage,
O\in\mathord{\text{Open}}_{\hskip-0.5pt\scalebox{0.6}{\pdfliteral${\neq}\varnothing$\pdfliteral}}
is a nonempty open set, and
\varsigma
is a valuation such that
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip-2.5pt\mathsf{F}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{leader}
.
Write

L_{n}=\{p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}\mid p\text{ is an $n,\varsigma$ leader}\}.

Then:

•

If
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(L_{n}\cap O)=0
then
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}={\mathbf{t}}
.

•

If
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(L_{n}\cap O)=1
then
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}={\mathbf{b}}
.

•

If
\hbox to0.4pt{\vbox to0.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(L_{n}\cap O)\geq 2
then
{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}={\mathbf{f}}
.

As corollaries:

1.

n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})
precisely when
n,q,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{leader}
for at most one
q\in O
.

2.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mu\mathsf{X}.{\boldsymbol{\neg}}\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{X})
precisely when there exists at most one leader at stage
n
.

3.

If
\vDash_{\varsigma}\text{TyPax}
then there exists precisely one leader at each stage
n
.

Proof.

We consider each part in turn:

1.

By elementary reasoning from Proposition
6.6.6
, using the definition of
n,p,O\vDash_{\varsigma}\psi
from Definition
3.6.1
(
1
) (for arbitrary
\psi
) and the clause for
\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\phi
in Figure
5
(for arbitrary
\phi
).

2.

By the definition of
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\psi
from Definition
3.6.1
(
3
).

3.

Combining the above with
{\mathsf{(LdrExist)}}
.
∎

Remark 6.6.8
.

We are not aware of the precise technical construction from Definition
6.6.4
appearing in the literature — but arguably it echoes an idea used in Kit Fine’s PhD thesis
[
Fin69
]
(see Chapter 3) which (paraphrasing slightly) implements a ‘there exist zero or one points satisfying
p
’ modality as
\forall q.(\Box(p\to q)\vee\Box(p\to{\boldsymbol{\neg}}q))
— where here the impredicative
\forall q
plays the role of our
\mu\mathsf{X}
as applied to a non-monotone argument.

We originally introduced
\mu\mathsf{X}
to model iteration, which is a programming construct.
But the comparison above suggests that
\mu\mathsf{X}
might also be useful as a logical construct, namely as an impredicative universal quantification over all
\varkappa
.
Therefore, a tempting generalisation of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
is simply to replace
\mu\mathsf{X}
with an explicit and full-blooded
\forall\mathsf{X}
, such that

{[\hskip-1.99997pt[{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{X}.\phi]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}=\bigwedge_{\varkappa\in{\mathbf{3}}^{\mathsf{Ctx}}}{[\hskip-1.99997pt[\phi[\mathsf{X}{{:}\text{=}}\varkappa]]\hskip-1.99997pt]}_{n,p,O,\varsigma}.

There would be no mathematical barrier to doing this if it turns out to be useful, but the example of Paxos does not require such power so we do not include it for now.

7
Correctness properties

7.1
Summary of the properties

Remark 7.1.1
.

We will derive the following standard correctness properties for Paxos (as laid out in
[
Cac11
, Subsection 2.2.1]
) from the theory
TyPax
in Figure
11
:

1.

Validity:
If a participant decides some value
v
, then
v
was proposed by some participant.

A formal statement and proof is in Theorem
7.2.3
.

2.

Agreement:
If two participants decide values, then those values are equal.

A formal statement and proof is in Theorem
7.3.6
.

3.

Termination:
Every correct participant eventually decides some value.

A formal statement and proof is in Theorem
7.4.7
.

4.

Integrity:
Every correct (non-crashing) participant decides at most once.

Our logic abstracts away from a direct notion of execution, by design, but this property is represented indirectly in how Theorem
7.4.7
is expressed.
See Remark
7.1.2
below.

Remark 7.1.2

(On integrity)
.

In implementations of Paxos, once a participant has decided, it stops.
But in our logic, ‘stop’ is not even a concept.
There is no abstract machine to stop; there are just predicates, with denotations.

Nevertheless, the integrity property is echoed in our maths, as the
infinite repetition
in Theorem
7.4.7
.
Once a predicate
\mathsf{decide}(v)
returns
{\mathbf{t}}
, it will do so again and again — and because of Agreement, it can never decide any other value.
A predicate eventually repeatedly returning the same result, and never returning any other result, is a model of an abstract machine that has computed its result — and then just sits there forever, displaying that same result, unchanged, every time we look.

7.2
Validity

Remark 7.2.1
.

Validity
is expressed in English as:

If a participant decides some value
v
, then
v
was proposed by some participant.

We render this in our logic in Theorem
7.2.3
as

\text{TyPax}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.(\mathsf{decide}(v)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{propose}(v))).

In words we can read this as:

If someone decides
v
now, then at some stage now or in the past a leader of that stage proposed
v
.

We will now set about proving this property from our axioms, culminating with Theorem
7.2.3
.

Lemma 7.2.2
.

Suppose
\varsigma
is a valuation such that
\vDash_{\varsigma}\text{TyPax}
(
\vDash_{\varsigma}\text{TyPax}
is from Figure
7
).
Suppose
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
.
Then:

1.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{write}(v)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v)
.

2.

n\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept}(v)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v)
.

Proof.

We prove both parts by a simultaneous induction on
n
.
We freely use strong modus ponens (Proposition
2.3.3
(
2
)):

1.

Suppose
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{write}(v)
.
Then using
{\mathsf{(PaxWrite?)}}

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)\quad\text{or}\quad n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v).

We consider the two cases:

•

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v)
,
then

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v)
follows immediately from the clause for

\mathsf{R}

in Figure
6
.

•

If
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}\hskip 0.0pt(\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}\mathbin{\hbox to0.0pt{\scalebox{0.73}{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hss}\hskip-2.0pt\rightarrow}v)
,
then by Lemma
4.9.7
(
2
)
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept}(v)
,
so that by Figure
6

n^{\prime}\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept}(v)
for some
n^{\prime}<n
.
By part
2
of this result for
n^{\prime}<n
, we have that
n^{\prime}\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v)
, and thus using Lemma
4.5.2
(
2
)
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v)
.

In either case, we are done.

2.

Suppose
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept}(v)
.
By
{\mathsf{(PaxAccept?)}}

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{write}(v)
.
We use part
1
of the inductive hypothesis for
n
.
∎

Theorem 7.2.3

(Validity)
.

We have:

\text{TyPax}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.(\mathsf{decide}(v)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{propose}(v))).

Proof.

Suppose
\varsigma
is a valuation such that
\vDash_{\varsigma}\text{TyPax}
.
We reason using strong modus ponens (Proposition
2.3.3
(
2
)).
Suppose
v\in\mathord{\text{Value}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{\scriptscriptstyle\geq 0}
and
p\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
, and suppose
n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{decide}(v)
.
We consider two cases:

•

Suppose
p=\mbox{$\mathit{leader}$}(n)
.

By
{\mathsf{(PaxDecideL?)}}

n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\Box}\hskip 0.0pt\mathsf{accept}(v)
.
It follows by Proposition
4.4.3
(
4
) that
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{accept}(v)
.
By Lemma
7.2.2
(
2
)
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{propose}(v)
, and using
{\mathsf{(PaxPropose?)}}
we conclude that
n\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{T}\hskip 0.0pt{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{propose}(v))
as required.

•

Suppose
p\neq\mbox{$\mathit{leader}$}(n)
.

By
{\mathsf{(PaxDecide{\boldsymbol{\neg}}L?)}}

n,p\vDash_{\varsigma}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{decide}(v))
.
Thus there exists
p^{\prime}\in\mathord{\text{Point}}
such that
n,p^{\prime}\vDash_{\varsigma}\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{decide}(v)
and we can proceed using the previous case.
∎

7.3
Agreement

Remark 7.3.1
.

Agreement
is expressed in English as:

If two participants decide values, then those values are equal.

We render this in our logic in Theorem
7.3.6
as

\text{TyPax}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v,v^{\prime}.({\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v^{\prime})\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}v^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v).

In words we can read this as:

If someone decides
v
now or in the past, and someone decides
v^{\prime}
now, then
v^{\prime}=v
.

It is convenient to prove the ‘now’ and the ‘in the past’ parts of this as two sub-cases:

1.

Proposition
7.3.3
handles the case of
{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v^{\prime})
, i.e. when the two decisions are made at the same time.

2.

Theorem
7.3.6
then extends this with the case of
{\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v^{\prime})
, i.e. when one decision precedes the other.

Agreement at time
n

Lemma 7.3.2
.

Suppose
\varsigma
is a valuation such that
