:
Simpler Declarative Paxos (Definition
8.2.1
)

Definition 8.2.1
.

We define
Simpler Declarative Paxos
to be the logical theory

\text{TySPax}=(\Sigma_{\text{TySPax}},\Theta_{\text{TySPax}}),

where:

1.

the signature
\Sigma_{\text{TySPax}}
is

\Sigma_{\text{TySPax}}=[\mathsf{leader}:0;\ \mathsf{propose},\mathsf{write},\mathsf{accept},\mathsf{decide}:1],

as per the notation in Definition
3.2.1
(
4
), and

2.

the axioms
\Theta_{\text{TySPax}}
are as written in Figure
12
.

The same validity properties hold of
TySPax
from Figure
12
as hold for
TyPax
from Figure
11
, namely: Validity (Theorem
7.2.3
), Agreement (Theorem
7.3.6
), and Termination and Integrity (Theorem
7.4.7
):

Theorem 8.2.2
.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a signature that includes
\Sigma_{\text{TySPax}}
, and suppose
\mathcal{M}
is a model.
Then we have:

1.

\text{TySPax}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v.(\mathsf{decide}(v)\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}{\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}(\mathsf{leader}\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}\mathsf{propose}(v)))
.

2.

\text{TySPax}\vDash{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}v,v^{\prime}.({\hbox{\hskip 3.22917pt\hskip-3.22917pt\hbox{${\reflectbox{$\mathsf{R}$}}$}\hskip-3.22917pt\hskip-0.25pt\raisebox{-0.6pt}{\hbox{\rule{4.30554pt}{0.77496pt}\hskip 0.5pt}}\hskip-0.25pt\hskip 3.22917pt}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v)\mathrel{\boldsymbol{\wedge}}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.6pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Diamond\hskip 0.0pt}\mathsf{decide}(v^{\prime}))\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}v^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{\text{=}}}v
.

3.

\text{TySPax}\vDash\scalebox{0.8}[1.0]{$\mathsf{infinitely}$}\hskip 0.0pt{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\forall$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}^{\hskip-0.3pt\mathord{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\displaystyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\textstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}{\scalebox{0.65}{\pdfliteral$\scriptscriptstyle\mathgeo{Q}$\pdfliteral}}}}_{\hskip 0.0pt\Box\hskip 0.0pt}{\boldsymbol{{\hskip 0.8pt\raisebox{0.5pt}{\scalebox{.85}{$\exists$}}\hskip 0.2pt}}}\mathsf{decide}
.

Proof.

This is by construction: from the discussion in Subsection
8.1
, the theory
TySPax
consists of just those parts of
TyPax
that are actually required in the correctness proofs.
∎

Thus, Theorem
8.2.2
just notes that Simpler Declarative Paxos extracts from the proofs in Section
7
the logical essence of Figure
11
, as applied to the standard correctness properties of Paxos.
There is no right or wrong about whether we prefer
TyPax
or
TySPax
; it depends on what level of abstraction we want to work with at any particular moment.
But, implicit in the formal mathematics of Theorem
8.2.2
is an intuitive observation that
TySPax
is
minimal
, in the sense that we put into it only that which we know we need to make some specific correctness proofs work.

9
Conclusions and future work

9.1
Conclusions

We have introduced
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
, a three-valued modal fixed-point logic for the declarative specification of distributed algorithms, and demonstrated its use by considering Paxos — a canonical and nontrivial consensus protocol which tolerates benign failures of participants.

This case study shows how our approach can capture the essential logical structure of distributed protocols in a concise and principled way.
High points include the use of a third truth-value to handle the case of incorrect participants without requiring explicit ‘for a correct participant’ side-conditions (Remark
2.2.2
), the syntax and semantics of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
itself as a core language (Figures
3
and
5
), the Declarative Paxos and Simpler Declarative Paxos axiomatisations (Figures
11
and
12
),
how we capture 2-twined and 3-twined quorum intersection in clean logical form (Propositions
5.3.2
and
5.4.2
; see also Remark
5.1.3
), and an illustration of the (non-evidently) rich expressive power of our framework that we can even identify unique leaders (Subsection
6.6.3
).

While we have not developed full axiomatisations of more complex consensus algorithms in this paper, preliminary investigations suggest that our framework extends naturally to these settings.
This indicates that
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
has the potential to serve as a general logical foundation for specifying and reasoning about distributed consensus, paving the way for more systematic analysis, clearer communication of protocol designs, and ultimately, stronger guarantees of correctness.

This paper is aimed at

•

logicians, who might be interested in exploring a logic with a new and unusual combination of features inspired by strong practical motivations, and

•

researchers in distributed systems, who might consider (and benefit from) adopting such a framework.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
offers benefits that could make it a useful addition to the toolkit of anyone working in distributed systems:

1.

Much of what we have presented reflects reasoning patterns that practitioners already employ informally.
For instance, the third truth-value (
{\mathbf{b}}
) in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
corresponds to familiar side conditions such as “for every correct participant,” and the backward axioms capture the reverse inferences that system designers routinely rely on.
What is new here is making these implicit practices explicit, systematic, and mathematically precise.

By crystallising these patterns into a single logical framework,
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
provides more than just a tidy formalisation: it offers a common language in which both logicians and distributed-systems researchers can communicate, verify, and extend consensus protocols.
This not only clarifies existing algorithms but also creates a principled foundation for analysing new ones, reducing ambiguity and opening the door to mechanical verification.
In this way,
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
turns tacit reasoning into a tool for systematic design, bridging the gap between informal intuition and formal correctness.

2.

Logic is a concise, powerful, and unambiguous language.
It distils paragraphs of English prose into a single line of precise symbols, enabling clear and rigorous reasoning.

While explanatory English is helpful for accessibility and context, formal logic excels at working through the details of complex algorithms.
It ensures precision and eliminates the ambiguities that often arise in informal descriptions.

3.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
operates at a new (and higher) level of abstraction, which complements existing logic-based techniques.
TLA+
[
Lam94
]
provides a state-based view of distributed algorithms, whereas
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
abstracts lower-level implementation details while retaining the essential behaviours and properties of the algorithms.
This abstraction does not obscure the logic of the system; on the contrary we have found that it clarifies it, making the underlying mechanisms and invariants more apparent.

4.

One strength of logic is its ability to abstract away what is irrelevant while maintaining precision about what is important.
In
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
, constructs like strong and weak implications (
\mathrel{\rightharpoonup}
and
\mathrel{\to}
) exemplify this principle, capturing subtle distinctions in meaning with precise formal symbols.
These constructs distil complex ideas into simple yet powerful tools that can be manipulated symbolically and reasoned about systematically.

While mastering these constructs requires some initial effort, the benefits of precision and clarity justify the investment.

5.

Learning
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
requires some effort,
and axiomatisations in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
are subtle, this is the opposite of gratuitous: it reflects the inherent nature of the distributed systems they are designed to model.
By formalising these complexities,
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
captures their structure and reveals and addresses the challenges that are
already present
in the underlying algorithms.

This transparency makes it a powerful tool for understanding and reasoning about distributed protocols.
56
56

56

Certainly, the first author (who has a background in logic) has found this study invaluable for gaining a better understanding of consensus algorithms. It seems likely that other readers might benefit similarly from using
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
.

6.

Distributed algorithms — and consensus protocols in particular — are foundational to the operation of blockchain systems and other critical infrastructures.
Yet these algorithms are notoriously difficult to design and implement correctly, and ambiguities or informal specifications can leave room for vulnerabilities and failures.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
addresses this challenge by bridging the gap between declarative specification of invariants and practical verification.
It provides researchers with a rigorous framework for clarifying, reasoning about, and verifying the essential properties of distributed protocols.
This contribution is especially relevant to blockchain systems, where unambiguous and trustworthy specifications are not merely desirable but indispensable.

In summary,
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
turns implicit reasoning patterns into an explicit and principled logic, opening the door to new kinds of specification, reasoning, analysis, and ultimately we hope to more reliable distributed systems.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
unites the abstraction of declarative reasoning with the rigour of logical precision.
It makes reasoning patterns explicit, and offers a common language in which correctness can be articulated, communicated, and ultimately assured.

9.2
Related and future work

9.2.1
Applications of Paxos and logic

Paxos is a foundational distributed protocol, variants and elaborations of which are very much of practical interest to this day: a Paxos-based production distributed storage system called
Megastore

[
BBC+11
]
was used by Google; another distributed storage system
PaxosStore

[
ZLX+17
]
was used by WeChat and was partially inspired by Megastore (so far as we know it is still in use at time of writing); Google now uses
Spanner

[
TA25
,
Goo26
]
, which is also Paxos-based.
The
Chubby
distributed lock system
[
Bur06
]
is Paxos-based and is still in use today
[
CGR07
,
LCD+24
]
.

A modern example of a Paxos formalisation in TLA+ is documented in
[
CLS16
]
; a relevant theory file is in
[
LMD
]
.
Constructing TLA+ theories and proofs has historically not been cheap,
57
57

57

The rise of AI-generated code, which occurred after this paper was prepared and after the literature cited was published, might change things to some extent — but at time of writing the precise impact and how this will play out specifically for distributed protocols, has not yet become clear.

so attempts have been made to automate proofs; a recent example is
[
GS21
]
.
Another approach is to use a logic for which a practical decision procedure exists, such as
Effectively Propositional Logic
(EPR), which is decidable and has a small model property (which roughly says that if a formula is satisfiable in some model, then it is satisfiable in a model that is not too large relative to the size of the formula).
This means that if a protocol and its correctness properties can be expressed in EPR, then they stand a good chance of being automatically checkable in reasonable time — this is not actually a mathematical guarantee, but empirically this tends to be a good place to start looking.
An example of a suitable Paxos formalisation is in
[
PLSS17
]
.
This is not TLA+, but it is still very much a transition system.

What these approaches all have in common is that they describe
transition systems based on message-passing
.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
, with its declarative style, follows a distinct philosophy: it encourages us to axiomatise protocols such that there is no transition system, no state-machine, no explicit message-passing, and no algorithmic time (i.e. no notion of events in an explicit sequence).
58
58

58

Round numbers are part of the logical structure of Paxos, and as such they are retained.
It might seem extraordinary that protocols could be so represented, given that the very essence of their implementation consists precisely of message-passing state machines embedded in algorithmic time!
However, this paper shows that it can be done, and on reflection that is no more remarkable than the fact that declarative paradigms such as the
\lambda
-calculus or logic clauses can be used to program, even though they may be compiled down to concrete state-transitions of a CPU.

This structural difference in understanding of what a protocol
is
, is distinctive to the approach proposed in this paper.

9.2.2
Impossibility results via logic

A rich literature exists studying
classes of protocols
using logical axiomatisations, especially to prove impossibility or consistency guarantees from relatively weak assumptions.
Examples (non-exhaustive list) include
[
Tau91
]
(an axiomatic impossibility proof for asynchronous resilient consensus),
[
GB17
]
(a consistency result for global sequencing of operations),
[
CEM23
]
, and
[
AFAG+25
]
(another impossibility result).
This literature studies classes of protocols rather than specific protocols, so that the theories and models that arise tend to be more abstract than those arising when specifying specific protocols.
59
59

59

This is not to be read as a claim that no other authors have ever studied specific protocols. They have and we cite examples elsewhere (non-exhaustive list:
[
CEM23
,
CLS16
,
PLSS17
]
). That is just not the part of the literature that we are discussing in this paragraph.

For example, Taubenfeld in
[
Tau91
]
writes that a protocol is a set of runs and a run is a finite sequence of events, and the analysis proceeds at this level of abstraction.

In contrast, in this paper we have presented
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
as an environment within which to specify specific protocols (and, by extension, extensions of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
if necessary to specify specifically extended protocols).

Another equally fundamental distinction is that the four papers cited above, and others in a similar spirit, are still about
events happening in order
, as the quote from Taubenfeld illustrates: a protocol is a set of runs, and a run is
a finite sequence of events
.
This is a general model, dispensing with all details of specific protocols, but it does retain one thing: precisely
algorithmic time
; a finite sequence of events.
This retained abstraction is fundamentally
a different thing
from the logical time in which
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
exists.

9.2.3
Logics of knowledge and belief

A rich field of research exists studying knowledge and collaboration in multi-agent systems, especially using modal epistemic logics.
Foundational works in this field include
[
HM90
,
FHMV04
]
.

Unfortunately confusion can now arise via the following chain of thought: distributed systems consist of multiple agents,
therefore
distributed systems are a subfield of multi-agent systems,
therefore
distributed protocol design is a subfield of multi-agent systems,
therefore
a modal logic for distributed protocols must be some flavour of (or admit a presentation in terms of) modal epistemic logic.

Even the reader who recognises this reasoning as somewhat reductive might still find it colouring ideas about what
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
should be, because (like epistemic logics)
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
is a modal logic for specifying and reasoning about distributed protocols.
But this is no more valid for
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
than it is for TLA+ — this is also a modal logic, and also about specifying and reasoning about distributed protocols, but the TLA+ modalities have nothing to do with belief.

The difference is that participants in a specific protocol need not themselves care about coordination, knowledge, or belief, even as they follow the protocol.
This is touched on explicitly in Section 9 of
[
HM90
]
,
‘A paradox?’
, which essentially notes that a system can have useful notions of coordination that are weaker than a full simultaneous coordination requiring full common knowledge.
For example: do a group of ants moving a leaf know they are part of an ant colony, and does a group of neurons identifying an edge in a retina know they are part of a brain (and, perhaps, looking at a mathematical symbol)?
No.
Their jobs are to be groups of ants or neurons.
And, does an entire ant colony or an entire brain ever even get together for a global council and synchronisation step before every action by its parts?
No, of course not.

From the point of view of Paxos, coordination is an
emergent property
of participants in that system following the rules; coordination is not an explicit goal of individual participants, and parts of the system may progress at different rates.
The analysis of knowledge in distributed systems is valuable for understanding the design space of emergent properties arising from classes of protocols, but in this paper we use
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
as a language for positively specifying and reasoning about the behaviour of individual participants following specific protocols, which is a different goal.
60
60

60

There would be no barrier to
adding
knowledge modalities to
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
. That would be an extension of the logic, but it would serve no purpose here, because Paxos does not require it.

9.3
Coalition Logic (CL) and Alternating-time Temporal Logic (ATL)

Coalition logic includes a modality
[C]\phi
indicating that a coalition
C
can act jointly to ensure that
\phi
holds in the next state (no matter what other participants may do)
[
Pau02
]
.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
and CL share an interest in quorums/coalitions and multi-participant computation, but as logics they are quite distinct:

1.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
abstracts away algorithmic time and so by design lacks any explicit notion of ‘next state’ in the sense intended by CL (see Remark
6.2.2
(
2
)).

2.

A model in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
represents a
single run
of a protocol.
A model in CL is a game representing all possible moves, and thus containing all possible protocol runs — just follow the ‘next step’ accessibility relation.
In CL, the model is typically assumed as an abstract structure, not axiomatised as a specific structure representing a particular protocol (though in principle it might be).

3.

The
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
modalities are existential and universal ‘for some quorum’ and ‘for all quorums’.
It is impossible to explicitly name some particular quorum
C
as in CL; conversely, CL lacks explicit modalities for quantifying over coalitions.

4.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
is optimised to be a natural logic for specifying valid runs of some specific distributed protocol.

CL is optimised to be a modal logic for discussing possible strategies of coalitions within some Kripke-frame-style abstract space of possible runs (Pauly calls these
game frames
).

We are not aware of any specification of Paxos in Coalition Logic, nor in ATL (mentioned below).
This might be possible in principle — but even if this were done, Coalition Logic’s structural commitment to algorithmic time and the lack of other
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
structure (such as explicit modalities quantifying over quorums) means that such an axiomatisation would be a different structure that does not relate to the core concern in
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
of declarative axiomatisation of distributed protocols, and especially fault-tolerant broadcast and agreement protocols and their generalisations.
61
61

61

This focus extends to the CL-like literature. We are aware of no explicit axiomatisations of any fault-tolerant broadcast or agreement protocols.
Where such things are even mentioned, as in a mention in item 2 of Remark 2 of
[
Gor01
]
, it is only as a source of frames over which to reason; no explicit axiomatisation is considered. This really is a distinct literature using distinct tools for distinct purposes.

{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
and CL are overlapping in the sense that they are both modal logics for reasoning about coalitions in distributed systems, but within that design space the goals and technical details are very different.

ATL has broadly similar goals to CL, but has a somewhat richer language of modalities, e.g.
\langle\langle C\rangle\rangle\phi\mathsf{U}\psi
means that coalition
C
can ensure that
\psi
holds eventually and
\phi
holds up to that point
[
AHK02
]
.
A comparison of ATL and CL is in
[
GJ04
]
.
For the purposes of this paper, ATL compares with
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
just as CL does.
62
62

62

Unfortunately, an early version of this paper called the logic
in this paper
‘Coalition Logic’.
This was an error by the first author, who (with apologies to Pauly) had missed the prior art.
The reader can disregard and not mention the previous naming of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
as irrelevant and likely to confuse — we only mention it here to address any confusion generated.

9.4
Future work

In this paper, our focus has been to lay the foundational theory and framework of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
and to demonstrate its application to one of the most well-known consensus protocols, Paxos
[
Lam98
]
.
This is a natural starting point: Paxos is a cornerstone of distributed systems and remains one of the most widely studied and deployed consensus protocols.

At the same time, Paxos is relatively straightforward by modern standards.
In particular, Paxos addresses only crash failures, and is therefore suited to environments that assume benign faults rather than adversarial behaviour.

A natural direction for future work involves extending our framework to handle more complex and realistic fault models, such as Byzantine faults, where participants can deviate arbitrarily from the algorithm.
Consensus protocols that address Byzantine faults, such as Practical Byzantine Fault Tolerance (PBFT)
[
CL99
]
and HotStuff
[
YMR+19
]
, provide robust mechanisms for fault tolerance and serve as the foundation for many modern blockchain systems.
Tendermint (
https://tendermint.com
)
[
BKM18
]
is another BFT consensus protocol.
Applying
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to these protocols would show how it handles the complexity introduced by arbitrary faults, which is necessary to model and verify more sophisticated algorithms.
On precisely this topic, in a shorter sister paper
[
Gab26b
]
to this paper the first author gives a declarative study of Bracha Broadcast and Crusader Agreement.
The logic used there is deliberately smaller and simpler (as the protocols are smaller and simpler than Paxos) but in one respect that paper does substantively extend this one, because its notion of failure (again, consistent with the protocols) is full Byzantine failure.
The three-valued logic framework handles this extremely well, with absolutely no complaint or friction: it is in fact quite remarkable how flexible this framework turns out to be.
Fundamentally, this just serves to illustrate the
unreasonable effectiveness of logic
(apologies to Wigner and then Hamming).
We would urge the reader who may be used to a more imperative style of specification of distributed protocols to not assume that because ‘imperative’ is how they may have been educated, ‘imperative’ is the one and only viable way to go.
It is not.

Recent research has explored DAG-based consensus protocols, which deviate from the traditional linear blockchain structure.
In these protocols, transactions are organised into a directed acyclic graph (DAG), enabling greater parallelism and scalability.
Protocols such as DAG-Rider
[
KKKNS21
]
, Narwhal (a DAG-based dissemination protocol) and Tusk (an asynchronous consensus protocol built on top of it)
[
DKKSS22
]
, and Bullshark
[
SGSKK22
]
represent cutting-edge advancements in this domain.
Extending
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to analyse DAG-based protocols would be another advance, as these systems often involve more intricate interactions between components and require reasoning about concurrent and asynchronous behaviour at a much higher level of complexity.

Another promising avenue for future research involves leveraging the modularity and expressiveness of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to study hybrid protocols
[
NTT21
]
that combine elements of classical consensus with newer techniques, such as Gasper
[
BHK+20
]
and others
[
DZ24
,
DSTZ24
]
.

It would be helpful in future work to develop automated tools for
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
: the tools developed for TLA+
[
Lam02
]
could show the way.
TLA+ is a formal specification language and framework designed to model, specify and verify properties of complex systems, including distributed algorithms.
Its TLC model checker
[
YML99
]
provides exhaustive state exploration to verify properties such as safety and liveness, and the PlusCal translator simplifies specification by offering a high-level pseudocode-like interface.
Additionally, the TLA+ Toolbox
[
KLR19
]
integrates these capabilities into a development environment, supporting workflows that are valuable in both research and industrial applications.

Adapting similar tools to
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
could enable automated verification of correctness properties for consensus protocols, including invariants and liveness guarantees.
For example: a higher-level language akin to PlusCal could simplify the specification of protocols using the axiomatic foundations of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
.
Furthermore, leveraging techniques such as the symbolic model checking enhancements presented by Otoni et al.
[
OKK+23
]
or integrating modular proof systems like TLAPS
[
CDL+12
]
could further enhance the usability and robustness of the framework.

Such tools would streamline the application of
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
to real-world systems, and also make it accessible to a broader audience of researchers and practitioners.
This direction represents an exciting convergence of theoretical advancements and practical tool development, providing a robust framework for analysing both classical and emerging consensus protocols.
As mentioned in the Introduction, we have made a start in this direction by creating a proof-of-concept model-checker
[
Gab26a
]
, which the reader can load and play with online now by going to
https://bellissimogiorno.github.io/3muQL-model-checker/
.

Adopting
{\mathbf{3}}\hskip-0.5pt\upmu\mathsf{QL}
requires some investment in time and effort, but the benefits in clarity, correctness, and reliability may be worthwhile.
Consensus algorithms are known to be particularly challenging, so our relatively high-level declarative approach could help to manage (and debug) the complexity of these algorithms.

We hope this work will inspire readers to explore and adopt this new perspective, contributing to more robust and trustworthy distributed protocols in the future.
